### Chapter 5 The credible testimony of the Revelator and the credibility of his saving word {#tanzella-rivelazione.1.chapter-5-the-credible-t}
#### 5.1 Theology of credibility and the apologia dimension of fundamental theology
The author of the *First Letter of Peter*, in comforting the first Christians in the face of the danger of persecution, exhorted them to adore Christ in their hearts, but also to be **"always ready to respond *to anyone who asks you for reason for the hope that* is in you". (*1Pt* 3.15).** In the version of the New Vulgate, the "giving reason" is more explicitly a "defense" (*defensio*), that is an "apologia" (ἀπολογία) in accordance with the Greek text, a "speaking in front": "Dominum autem Christum sanctificate in cordibus vestris, parati semper ad defensionem (ἕτοιμοι ἀεί πρὸς ἀπολογίαν) omni poscenti vos ratione de ea, quae in vobis est spe (λόγον περὶ τῆς ἐν ὑμῖν ἐλπίδος)". The text in question shows us the simultaneous presence of three important terms: **apology**, **reason** and **hope**. Christians can therefore be asked for "reasons" - in the strongest sense of the term - for the content of their belief. Even if this content is indicated in the text as "hope", it is in reality the hope par excellence, of "living in the hope of the risen Christ", that is, ***the reason for Christian living***. We are referring here, in essence, to the central content of the Christian faith, because essentially faith in Christ's resurrection and industrious expectation of the full fulfilment of all its consequences, both on the individual and the cosmic level (cf. *Rom* 8:19-25).

The exhortation of the *First Letter of Peter* is appropriately completed by a reference to experience, by the call to a witness: "Let this be done with gentleness and respect, with a right conscience, so that at the very moment when evil is spoken of you those who malign your good conduct in Christ may be shamed" (cf. vv. 15-16). **The reasons for hope, therefore, cannot be given as simple gnosis, that is, satisfying the needs of theoretical knowledge, but must be delivered with a credibility that rests on inner righteousness, gentleness and respect towards the interlocutor**, not as a simple strategy of proclamation, but as **the transmission of a lifestyle, of "good conduct in Christ"** (τὴν ἀγαθὴν Χριστὗͺ ἀναστροφήν), which basically represents the very object of what one wants to participate in others.

##### 5.1.1 The sense of an "apologia for Christianity".
Fundamental Theology has always seen in the Petrine page, the object also of numerous patristic references,1 a key text to understand the meaning of its apologetic dimension. However, in order to well understand what this dimension consists of and how it operates within a theological-fundamental program, some clarifications are necessary precisely on the term "apologetics".

The rethinking of the approach of various theological treatises from the second half of the 20th century, particularly around the years of the Second Vatican Council, meant that **contemporary Fundamental Theology was constituted and understood as a result of an *emancipation* from previous Catholic Apologetics**. The decision of the Council Fathers to radically change the orientation of several of the schemes envisaged for some important documents, moving from a "defence" of the Christian faith in the face of errors, to an organic and positive exposition of the mystery of Christ and the Church, guided post-conciliar theological reflection towards the formulation of a programme and a conceptual itinerary suitable for a new fundamental Theology.2 This process led much of the theological language to interpret the term **"apologetics" in a restrictive (and sometimes negative) way, mainly emphasizing its rationalist defense**, often operating on a non-theological ground.3

In reality, an apologia for the faith is **not necessarily to be understood as the defense of Christian belief against those who want to fight or debase it**. First of all, and in accordance with the deep meaning of its etymology, the term "apologia" indicates the ability to *speak in front of an interlocutor*. In this sense, fundamental theology must always have an apologetic dimension, since part of its specificity consists in being able to elaborate an exposition of one's own faith that can also be used *ad extra*. When we speak of usability *ad extra,* we do not mean that faith is placed alongside its own theologian - like any other theological discipline, fundamental theology must also take place within the faith; rather, we point out that the exposition of the content of faith, for fundamental theology, acts on the prolongation of the *missio ad extra of* the Word itself. It is, in an etymological sense, an "exposition", that is to say, an *exposition* according to the dynamics of an announcement always willing to risk the critical sifting of reason. If no theological discipline envisaged such an *extra* exposure, the whole theological discourse would become *ipso facto* self-referential, since the questions posed to the various theological treatises would all come from within the theology itself.

The apologetic dimension of Fundamental Theology therefore contributes to keeping the theological discourse open on reality and history, enabling the believer to address those who do not share his or her beliefs and, in general, allowing him or her to carry out his or her themes as if they had to be presented in front of any interlocutor whose cultural and intellectual context is known. This is in agreement with the deepest logic of the Christian proclamation: a proclamation that was proclaimed by men who never set aside their faith in the risen Christ; but men who, in preaching or writing what they believed, were able to do so with a declared purpose: "that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and that, believing, you may have life in his name" (*Jn* 20:30-31), and that, once they had embraced the faith, "we might be made aware of the solidity of the teachings received" (cf. *Lc* 1.4). In this sense, the apologetic dimension of Fundamental Theology *prevents the whole of theology from the risk of fundamentalism,* accustoming it to carry out its arguments in front of all men and women, whose questions, anxieties and aspirations it learns to share.

To give the reasons for the credibility of the Christian proclamation is up to anyone who is the bearer of that proclamation. According to the Petrine text, the subject who is asked to give "reasons for one's hope" is not the theologian as such, but every believer. In harmony with one's own experience of faith and with a depth of knowledge proportionate to the role occupied in God's people and civil society, every baptized person must therefore possess these reasons, and know how to explain them, why *these reasons exist*. Within a Catholic perspective, to give it up would be manifesting the precariousness of one's faith or, worse, the misrepresentation of its content and dynamics, which is not uncommon in those who believe that confrontation with reason is superfluous or inappropriate.

From the horizon of the believer remains thus excluded, in principle, the easy solution of fideism, a perspective that has been the subject of precise reviews by the Catholic Magisterium, as we have seen in chapter III dedicated to the Faith. **The fideist believes that faith can maintain its cognitive and salvific scope simply by affirming, for epistemological or even existential reasons, its incommensurability with reason**. It should not be forgotten that fideism, even before considering human rationality and philosophy inadequate to make judgments on the Absolute, on truth and on God (this is rather the position of agnosticism), affirms first of all that Christian Revelation **does not** possess a credibility capable of making a meaningful appeal to reason, in particular to reflected philosophical reason. The act of faith therefore remains, in the understanding that fideism has of it, **an act that human beings perform by suspending the judgment of reason**.

##### 5.1.2 What is the reason for the proclamation of Christian hope?
The Christian proclamation is addressed to the **whole human being**, to the **reasons of the intellect and to those of the heart**. The *reason* to appeal to is therefore the reason that involves philosophical rationality and scientific rationality, but also knows how to express the reasonableness of existential questions and that of common sense. It is a reason that is always exercised by a subject, and therefore a *personal reason*, in close relation to the exercise of will and freedom; a reason that is not directed to its object in a detached and independent way, but always evaluating also the value that the object possesses in relation to the fundamental themes of its existence. Here lies the need to develop an integral anthropology which, starting from Maurice Blondel's proposal, is called to use the method of immanence with profit, knowing how to **start from the expectations and aspirations of the subject**.4

##### The characteristics of a *capax fidei* reason
The recipient to whom the Christian proclamation is addressed is **not only the subject of formal reasoning, but also of *illative* meaning**, that is, of that common sense capable of correlating together data from different sources of knowledge, each of which, taken individually, might not be sufficient to formulate a certain judgment, but in their simultaneous appearance in the mind and in their mutual confrontation and confirmation, they push the subject towards an option **considered *reasonable****,* that is, fully human.5

The reasoning of our contemporaries is also certainly forged, to a greater or lesser extent, by scientific rationality (cf. GS, 5), and therefore accustomed to rigorously evaluating sources of knowledge and their reliability, respectful of the logic of reasoning and always open to new and deeper levels of intelligibility. From scientific rationality human reason learns to reflect within space-time horizons of cosmic breath, which know how to go towards the infinitely great and investigate the infinitely small, reaching an understanding of the world, of life and their evolution in time, with which philosophical thought, and therefore also the theological one, must know how to dialogue and know how to confront.

If the proclamation of the faith does not come up with the hoped-for response today it is also due, in good part, to **the weakness of reason and the crisis of metaphysical meaning, to which human beings are no longer spontaneously educated.** It is therefore worth pointing out certain characteristics that reason must possess in order for the proclamation to be meaningful, failing which the revealed Word may not be recognized as meaningful and attractive. **A *ratio capax fidei* is a *humble*, *realistic* reason, *confident in the search for truth* and *free*.** Some of these conditions will be discussed in more detail in the systematic conclusions in this chapter.

Although aware of its openness to infinity and therefore of its capacity to question itself about the whole of reality and the meaning of the whole, a "humble" reason recognizes itself as not competent to provide the truly ultimate answers; it accepts that these are told to it, because it is aware that the foundations of its own knowledge lie in the mystery of being, received and not placed, heard but not said. A reason understood in this way can also well interpret and make its own the best aspirations of human religious thought, appreciating its contribution to the *logos*, even though it has arrived through the vehicle of *mythos*. Reason capable of responding to the call of Revelation is therefore, in the final analysis, **a *non-ideological* reason**, open *to being revealed as created reason*.

##### A shared hope
**To proclaim the faith is not to give a lesson, but to know how to arouse in the interlocutor aspirations of which the Christian is also a participant**. It is about bringing out a "shared hope", from those who already believe and those who do not. Hope does not mean uncertainty or waiting for something that we do not know if it will come, because it is precisely of his hope that the believer is called to give reason. However, since these are goods hoped for and not consumed, believed and not yet fully possessed, as a subject of "hope" the believer finds himself in the condition of being a true companion in the midst of and next to his fellow men, because "the joys and the hopes, the sadness and anguish of people today, of the poor above all and of all those who suffer, are also the joys and hopes, the sadness and anguish of Christ's disciples, and there is nothing genuinely human that is not echoed in their hearts" (GS, 1).

A theological discourse on the credibility of Revelation must therefore show serious interest in the existential and intellectual situation of the interlocutor, not underestimating the travail that the search for truth and goodness brings with it, and the diversity of the paths along which this search is carried out in the history of each one.

In this sense, theological discourse should not be afraid to show itself as a *path to the truth*. Unlike the Magisterium of the Church, which, comforted by the "certain charism of truth" (DV, 8), is called to teach certainties extracted from the deposit of Revelation, the theologian, who moves from certainty towards the acquisition of new conclusions, will often find himself having to share many of the questions which, faced with the problems of human existence and knowledge of reality, those who still do not believe ask themselves and point out to them as still unresolved.

The important difference lies, for the theologian, in knowing that, in confronting them, he has at his disposal the **great guiding star of Revelation** and the rich weave of knowledge already possessed that is made available to him through the *analogia* *fidei*. In the logic of sharing, highlighted very well by the image of the encounter between the Risen One and the disciples of Emmaus, the task of a patient "explanation of history" and sometimes of a necessary "healing of reason" should also find room for fundamental theology, missions that are difficult to tackle by other theological treatises. This is a particularly urgent task today, because the proclamation of Christian hope cannot rely solely on the emotionality and sentiment of those to whom it is directed, but must also take on the burden of providing the necessary elements of education and training to enable the logic of credibility to be recognized as reasonably meaningful.

##### 5.1.3 On the relationship between Apologetics and Theology of Credibility
Credibility and Apologetics are two different notions. The expression **"Theology of credibility" is a *theological notion****,* and must necessarily be **based on a biblical foundation**, while the term **"Apologetics"** has a more **general character** andindicates a ***rational mode of* theological work**. As discussed in chapter I of this volume, this mode of fundamental theology highlights the character of "theology in front of an interlocutor" and of "theology in context", thus demanding that every possible Theology of credibility never ends in a self-referential discourse, that is, totally internal to the theological discourse, but reaches the point of *exposing itself to* reason and the reasons of the interlocutor, exercising that diakonia which is proper to it. If **apologetic** work presents itself as mainly **argumentative work** (e.g., knowing how to demonstrate the erroneous nature of statements against faith or to demonstrate the coherence and harmony of things believed), a reflection on **credibility instead moves within a broader horizon, attentive to the value of testimony, personalist aspects, anthropological understanding of signs.** When we talk about credibility, we refer primarily to the credibility of a subject, *of a witness,* and only secondly to the credibility of a content, i.e. its plausibility, plausibility and not contradiction. The ultimate foundation of credibility is always God, both because he is the subject witness of Revelation and because he is the guarantor of the truth content of revealed things.

Because of its fully theological status, a Theology of the credibility *of* Revelation should not deal with the preambles of faith, nor with arguments of a purely historical or philosophical nature, if the latter are aimed at legitimizing only the "fact" of Revelation without paying attention to its content and to the person of the Revelator. Yet, at the same time, from such rational arguments a reflection on credibility cannot be entirely independent, because they provide a necessary framework of intelligibility and significance to Revelation itself.

Apologetics has a more complex epistemological status, because it pushes fundamental theology on the frontier between theological knowledge and other forms of knowledge. If the Catholic Apologetics of the past identified above all a set of philosophical or positive-rational topics, today's apologetics must be understood as a method, a perspective - a "dimension of apology", precisely - and must always remain in close relation with theological discourse, so as not to risk turning into a simple debate of opinion. We are not therefore faced with a "tripartition" between theology of Revelation, theology of credibility and Apologetics, a division that would give Apologetics its own circumscribed philosophical terrain, when not merely controversial (or sometimes even journalistic); instead, we are faced with a methodological perspective that runs through both the theology of Revelation and the theology of credibility.

René Latourelle proposed a Theology of credibility that today incorporates three aspects, one of historical-hermeneutical, one of philosophical-anthropological and one of semiological-theological.6 Although all three moments are presented by the author within a Theology of credibility, because they formulate questions that are susceptible of access to theological answers, he agrees that it is only the third moment that constitutes the properly theological approach, while the first two refer to arguments of the apologetic sphere, in the sense we have given to this term. Latourelle thus presents a Theology of credibility developed within an "integral apologetics". It

1. must show himself capable of illustrating that the events that call for God's intervention in history, in particular the event of Jesus Christ, the historicity of his person and his teachings, have substantially **happened**;

1. It must also show how the **message** delivered through these events, the Revelation of the One God in the First Testament and the Christian proclamation operating in the New, decipher man, reveal him to himself, offering **an answer to his enigmas**;

1. must finally prove that the (theological) **signs** involved in this self-revelation of God are **recognizable and intelligible**.

Several readings of this pattern are possible. We could consider the three proposed moments as part of the apologetic dimension of the Fundamental,7 seeing the theology of credibility essentially as a "theology of signs", and thus referring mainly to the last moment; or, we could consider them all within a widely understood Theology of credibility, in which there is room for historical and anthropological reflection. In both cases it seems clear that a theology of signs (third moment) should always be linked with arguments of an apologetic nature, a dimension that it does not replace, nor make superfluous, but rather legitimate.

#### 5.2 A new approach to Theology of Credibility
As we have seen in the previous pages, contemporary Fundamental Theology has made a shift from a content-instructive conception (Revelation is a set of contents and instructs on its teachings) to a personalistic-dialogical conception (Revelation is a dialogue between God and man aimed at a communion of life, indeed Revelation is a Person). Credibility, therefore, is today presented above all in its personalist and testimonial value.

However, this state of affairs has a consequence: there is no question of whether there are "reasons" to believe that Revelation is the communication of a God known also by reason (miracles and prophecies), nor how such reasons would come into play in the act of faith. It is affirmed, above all, that **Revelation possesses intrinsic "signs" with which it displays the truth of its Author and the beauty of the plan of salvation** which He communicates to us; these are the signs of the Kingdom of God in our midst, converging towards the one great sign, Jesus Christ himself.

##### 5.2.1 The 'reasons' for credibility and their intrinsic place in Revelation
This new approach is certainly beneficial. Indeed, it would make no sense to reflect on the credibility of the fact or content of Revelation by abstracting from the person of the Revelator, from the *person of Jesus Christ* and from the *personality of* the God of Israel revealed definitively in Christ. **The credibility of a dogmatic truth (content aspect) remains, moreover, always limited by our inability to understand all its components and implications, while it is easier to access a judgment about the sincerity and reliability of a revealing witness** (personalist aspect). God reveals with his personal Word and the truth of this word is inseparable from the truth of the person who pronounces it. This makes it better to understand why, when today we present credibility as a property of Revelation, we do so in a *strong* sense: it is a characteristic intrinsic to it, which belongs to it.

**A personalist renewal of credibility therefore places the reasons for belief within Revelation and no longer outside it**. Classical Apologetics gladly reflected on the "fact" of revelation, proposing to demonstrate its divine origin through objective-external motives, in order to distinguish Jewish-Christian Revelation from other alleged revelations. The legitimacy of such a demonstration, which is explicitly requested by *Fides et ratio* (n. 67), may, however, run the risk of separating Revelation from the Revelator, its giving of itself in history from the salvific message it expresses and transports.

Reflecting on the "fact" of Revelation is undoubtedly possible, but this is not strictly speaking a judgment of "credibility", but something preparatory to it. If the objective "motives" that are invoked to show the divine origin of Revelation - miracles and prophecies, or even other motives that one wishes to elaborate - concern the effects of a divine Omnipotence recognizable by a philosophy of metaphysical instance (capable precisely of distinguishing what would be an Absolute foundation of the world, unmasking false absolutes), and if they are known to us through a simple historical-documental approach to the testimonies of Israel or to Christian sources, then they should be more correctly proposed as **"preambles of faith".**

When we speak of "reasons" or "reasons" of ***credibility***, their theological-intrinsic meaning must be underlined. **Their appeal operates from faith to reason and not vice versa:** recognized with "the eyes of faith", according to the expression of Pierre Rousselot,8 **they strengthen the faith of believers** with the growth of their critical rationality, according to a dynamic process capable of encompassing the existential experience and the experiences gained through new knowledge.

For those, on the other hand, who have not yet accepted Revelation, knowing that there are "reasons" for credibility and that this credibility is an intrinsic part of the proclamation, accredits the Christian subject as a witness who is not naive, aware of the depth of the content he preaches, a believer who has not rejected the demands of a critical reason. The reasons for credibility therefore confirm the faith of those who already believe, guaranteeing them a connection with the universality of reason. Considered by those who have not yet made the option of faith, **they reassure the interlocutor that, if he or she comes to a response of faith, he or she will not have to renounce the exercise of reason: this guarantee certainly does not cause the act of faith, but it is no less its task to show it to be fully *human***.

In accordance with this renewed perspective, fundamental theology will be able to develop an adequate "Theology of credibility" which will begin with the study of the *self-testimony that God offers to man so that he may believe His Word*. This self-testimony can be found in the biblical context both in the logic with which God reveals Himself in salvation history, and in general throughout the Word of the First Testament, and in the often direct and explicit ways in which Jesus of Nazareth offers in the New Testament reasons to believe His words (similarly to what His disciples would later do). Such a discussion will probably have to appeal, from time to time, also to philosophical reason, whose conclusions are necessarily preparatory to faith and its intelligibility, leaving it clear, however, that no question of the reasons for credibility or the preambles of faith can ever be self-proposed as the foundation of faith.

In summary, ***the ultimate reasons for welcoming Revelation lie within Revelation itself and not outside of it****.*9 Whatever the terminology by which one wishes to indicate the **reasons for credibility, they too should be considered to be *given together with Revelation,* and therefore to be announced together with it, not as its preambles**. The credibility of Revelation must be highlighted by directing the attention of the interlocutor first of all towards Revelation and, starting from it, moving from Revelation towards the universality of reason. The personalistic character of Revelation leads us to recognize that ***the ultimate reasons for credibility are to be sought in the credibility of the revealed Subject*, *in the reliability of his testimony and in the coherence/fascination of the revealed content***. Even before the testimony given by others about the word of God, it is the very self-testimony of God, and of God in Christ, which is imposed on the study of the theologian and the recipient of the proclamation. If Revelation is understood as a personal-dialogical relationship and faith as faith in someone, then the *credibility of Revelation and the credibility of faith are, in fact, the* ***credibility of a person***, the Person of Jesus Christ, witness of the Father and in whom the whole Revelation is expressed.

##### 5.2.2 The role of *praeambula fidei* in a fundamental theology programme
The new approaches suggested by a contemporary Theology of credibility do not exclude the possibility of reserving to the *praeambula fidei* an explicit reference within a fundamental theology pathway. Indeed, they play a role of the utmost importance, that of showing human reason the "significance" of the content of Revelation. What we mean to say is that, **in order to understand the meaning of what Revelation transmits to us, it is necessary for human reason to know in an unambiguous way the content of concepts such as God, Logos, freedom, eternal life, soul, guilt, good, evil, etc.**. A discussion, also philosophical, of the preambles of faith has the purpose of showing that reason cannot judge these terms as insignificant, conventional, or worse a *non-sense*; instead they indicate a semantic area recognizable by philosophical thought (here widely understood), whose content is comprehensible and endowed with sufficient truth.

*[Embedded Image]*
 Text by *Fides et ratio*, no. 67

In order to understand Revelation and its credibility, reason must first possess a notion of *God* and this must be intelligible to it. It is necessary to have an epistemology open to an instance of *truth* and an anthropology that recognizes the truth of *freedom*. What is needed is a necessary grid of philosophical reference, spontaneous or reflected, which allows the subject, once he or she has approached the content of Revelation, to understand in it the *logic of fulfillment,* but also that of *scandal* and *paradox*. Recognizing the idea of an accomplishment will only be possible, in fact, for those who understand the historical nature of human existence and the irreversibility of a history that is not closed in itself, but develops as a meaningful process. A logic of promise and fulfillment can be accessed only after having ascertained the *historicity* - that is, the adherence to real events - of the religious traditions of Israel, and above all the historicity of the message and life of Jesus of Nazareth and of what the believing community has transmitted about him. Reason will be able to discern what a paradox or scandal represents for it only on the watermark of a metaphysical thought that shows sufficient truthfulness in judging events and what one would reasonably expect from events, and against the background of a moral thought for which the notion of justice has real meaning. Finally, the content of Revelation becomes significant only if the human person recognizes, in his or her conscience, the presence of a good to be done and an evil to be avoided, and thus sees in it the responsibility for *guilt and* the aspiration to *forgiveness*, caught in the face of an Absolute recognized not only as the foundation of the real, but also as the guarantor and ultimate referent of moral life.

An example of how the "significance" of Revelation can stand out along a theological itinerary that includes both the "movements" of Fundamental Theology, the biblical-dogmatic and the anthropological-apologetic, is offered to us by almost the entire first part of *Gaudium et spes*. The conciliar discussion - which chose the dialogical perspective in an intentional and programmatic way - carries out its arguments using a "reference grid" of epistemological and anthropological, historical and cultural character. The progressive exposition of the key elements of Revelation is in fact interspersed with the questions and conclusions that a reason not yet enlightened by faith formulates from the real, from human consciousness and from the reality of the world, according to arguments that in some cases recover even the best instances of religious phenomenology and thought.

From the point of view of theological systematics, **the preambles of faith then seem to be able to act as a "secondary object" of fundamental theology, with the role of providing meaning and intelligibility to its "primary object", that is, Revelation and its credibility,** understanding the latter always centered around the signs of salvation represented by Christ and the Church. We suggest the existence of a certain analogy between this articulation and what, when speaking of the transmission of Revelation in the Church, we have called the "primary" and "secondary" object of the Magisterium of the Church (see in this manual, chapter IV, n. 3). We have in fact recognized within the competence of the Church's Magisterium the existence of numerous philosophical truths of reason and motivated this extension by observing that all this serves "to correctly interpret and sanction" Revelation itself. The fact that the Magisterium can authoritatively affirm something about these truths by including them in its secondary object authorizes every theology of Revelation to include them in a sort of "secondary object" of its own: in fact, it would be unwise to say that there are themes for which the Magisterium of the Church can elaborate teachings on which, instead, theology has nothing to say.

A reason that grasps the preambles of faith as its own object, adequate in itself, does not yet operate within faith but is certainly capable of it, is a reason, as noted above, *capax fidei*. However, in order to meet the faith, it must strip itself of its *hybris*, propose and self-understand itself as a non-ideological reason, aware of its own ontological contingency and its own finite horizon, a reason that is not self-sufficient. It is a reason, we must never forget it, which must remain *open to being revealed as a reason created*. It is the reason that begins its journey from the realism of things and that has the humility to *let the otherness of reality speak for itself*. But still personal and free reason, and therefore capable of rejecting openness towards a principle of creation, in accordance with what the classical theological tradition has always pointed out, moreover with a solid biblical foundation (cf. *Wisdom* 13:1; *Rom* 1:21-23), **The recognizability of the preambles of faith, in essence, is clouded by sin and is strongly conditioned by the moral dispositions of the subject.** Realism requires us to bear this in mind and forgetting it would be naivety.10 In this sense, the Christian proclamation is not only called upon to *remind* human reason, not yet enlightened by faith in Christ, of its capacities: these capacities must in some way also be *revealed* and, to a certain extent, *healed*.

In order to justify a discussion of the preambles of faith in a programme of Fundamental Theology, one can also think that they are conceptually placed between the two modes of the one revelation, that is, between anthropological-natural revelation and historical-supernatural revelation, and therefore, in a certain sense, referable to a theological (and not only philosophical) perspective, at least to the extent that a theology of Revelation remains available to deal with a revelation of God in created nature. The position of the preambles of faith on this side of a principle of creation (or revelation) explains why they can operate in a meaningful way only in a humble and realistic reason, willing to receive the real as given, as given and therefore created.11 Although the deepest meaning and ultimate sense of the truths of reason that they bring us will be enlightened by the Word of God, and it is only through that Word that some may subjectively learn about it, the preambles of faith are objectively independent of a supernatural-historical revelation. They represent the presuppositions of every theologian and the necessary premise required of the recipients of the proclamation, constituting, as a whole, the *conditions of possibility of listening to the* Revelation, without which the latter could not even come to fruition in the subject. Ultimately, the willingness of reason to allow itself to be revealed as created is *in logical dependence on a revelation that has really given itself* and preceded it (natural revelation) and *is identified with the conditions of listening to the Revelation itself* (revelation understood globally).

##### 5.2.3 On the relationship between credibility, sign and testimony
The personalistic understanding of Revelation and its credibility has led fundamental theology to abandon a purely argumentative logic, to seek new forms capable of interpreting with greater success which motivations are at the basis of man's adherence to the faith, which favour his free response to the Word of God.

It is, after all, a research dictated by that "new sensitivity" shown today by the interlocutor, willing to listen more willingly to witnesses than to teachers, or to be guided **more often by feeling and experience than by formal inferences**. One of the first attempts to tap into the richness of the subject's vital world was that offered by J.H. Newman in his work *Grammar of Assent* (1870). In it the Oxford theologian analyzed the "certainty" with which we adhere to religious content that we cannot judge with formal inferences, introducing the difference between *notional* and *real* assent. Personal adherence to a religious content is the result of a real assent and does not depend on logical inferences that can be formalized. He then emphasized the operativeness of the "*illative sense*" (*illative sense*) by which we make a choice for "convergence by clues", none of which alone would be sufficient to determine assent.

In more recent times, attempts have arisen to base the adherence of faith on a certainty of conscience generated by an experience - the experience of the encounter with God, precisely - rather than on the intellectual certainty of a truth reached in a rational place. Such were the proposals for transcendental theology and narrative theology.

Karl Rahner's transcendental theology speaks to us of a transcendental athematic opening to Revelation, but this perception moves within the horizon of the "conditions of possibility" of a divine Revelation rather than that of its credibility. We are faced with an experience of a subjective type, which intercepts the meaning of the message for the interlocutor, but which, on its own, could not base the communication of the reasonableness of this message if not, precisely, only on its athematic and transcendental side.

In the case of "narrative" theology, it should be noted that it has played and continues to play an important role as a complement to a merely "argumentative" theology: it is self-understanding within the flow of a testimony, a flow that is certainly suitable to represent the logic of Revelation and its transmission in history. Yet, it too meets the limit of having to refer, in the final analysis, to the relationship between the witness who narrates and the truth told, and therefore needs a truthful foundation, at least to the extent that, in the encounter with the witness, the encounter with the truth does not itself become self-evident.

The revaluation of the personal-dialogical character of Revelation, with the corresponding attempt to proceed beyond purely argumentative logic, is also the cause of today's attention paid to semiology. Supported by a good biblical foundation, the logic of the *sign* (ostension, reference to an Author), is today preferred to the rationality of the *reason* (argumentation). The judgement that the signs enable is of a practical, synthetic order, susceptible of admitting a convergence, and operates within a context of meanings that cannot be formalized to a great extent. All these characteristics make a credibility entrusted to the practical judgment of the signs one of the ways of overcoming the *impasse* in which a theology of the act of faith, eager to interpret the relationship between God's gift and human freedom within a syllogistic-speculative framework, inevitably ran into.

In accordance with the definition of Thomas Aquinas, *id quod inducit in cognitionem alterius*,12 the sign refers to the presence and knowledge of something else, of someone if it is personal signs. If it is affirmed that a divine revelation possesses signs, then it is intended to emphasize that these are capable of indicating its Author, and that through them His salvation is made present and effective.

There is no doubt that Scripture makes extensive use of signs in order to mediate the action of the divine Word, to attract man's attention, to signify in an indirect way and to anticipate something that will happen in the future, as happens for example in many actions of the prophets. The signs refer to the Lord of Israel, and the Lord makes Himself present, He speaks, through His signs. The biblical "sign" (eb. *'ôt*) is like a *mark*, a stable confirmation that testifies to God's presence and saving action and promotes its memory in time. The works of Jesus of Nazareth, which manifest the *God who is with him and in him* and the *God who he is*, are also presented by the Gospels, by John in particular, as *his signs*.

The availability of the sign within the logic of revelation is favoured by its ordering to the communication of a meaning; its use within a logic of credibility is allowed by the relationship, free and involving, with which the sign attracts its recipient to let you glimpse a content that refers back beyond itself. In the sign we find once again, linked together, a *subjective* and an *objective* dimension. The first is necessary because the sign becomes recognizable within the lived experience, it appeals to the knowledge and experience of the subject, which could also vary from subject to subject, and even within the personal history of the subject. Some signs are directed only to specific interlocutors because they have something to say to them. At the same time, the logic of the sign cannot be totally subjective, because it is aimed at transmitting a demanding and important message, and therefore it must be easy to decode it, anthropologically eloquent and sufficiently shared.

But in a particular way - and here lies an important value of its function in the communication of God to man - the sign shakes and questions, urges the traveller along his path, offers him indications that demand from him a decision, *a stance*. In front of the sign, you can either believe in it and follow it, or you can pass over it, you can accept the meaning it carries and the knowledge it mediates, or you can freely run the risk of prescinding yourself from it. The sign is a *living* word, a word that indicates, but also a word that divides the soul, and for this reason can come to be a *sign of contradiction*. The sign thus manifests itself both to those who already move within the horizon of faith, and to those who do not yet share it; yet, only to those who read it within the act of their own belief is it recognized, truly and definitively, as a sign, while for others it may remain only a clue.

In this sense, the sign admits a scale of comprehensibility, from suspicion to clue to evidence, to which also corresponds a scale of appeal and personal commitment. Just as a sincere reason, neither ideological nor self-referential, and therefore in principle "capable of faith", is necessary to recognize the preambles of faith, so the sign can only be recognized within a virtuous, moral and religious context. For those who close themselves ideologically to any possibility of revelation and dialogue with the Absolute, the sign will not go much further than causing a possible suspicion, without being caught even as a clue. Those who share a context of truth of reason about human life and its openness to a Creator, will recognize in the signs of Christian Revelation sufficient clues to listen attentively to it, until they read to you, now in the dynamic of faith, the evident sign of God's saving presence in our midst and for us.

Even a credibility based on the logic of signs, and which prefers to speak of "signs of salvation" instead of "reasons of credibility", cannot totally disregard a rational itinerary and a philosophical context. Only when the interlocutor has an adequate grid of meanings, be they rational-philosophical or anthropological-existential, can he see in the sign "something great", something that questions and makes him understand what the consequences of following or rejecting him would be. Thus operate, for example, the sign of the logic of the Kingdom manifested in those who live its needs; the sign of the personality and authority of Jesus Christ, of his teachings and works, of his proexistence and his paschal mystery of death and resurrection; the sign of the charity/health of the Church and of the life of Christians. In the signs of Revelation the non-believer recognizes Something present, Someone worthy of listening and interest, a Word that communicates intelligible meanings and shows credible subjects.

A credibility based on signs is therefore a credibility that lately refers to a world of meanings, and therefore to the world of the person. But the sign, referring beyond itself, still remains a mediation, and it remains so even in its most eloquent expression, that of the humanity of the Incarnate Word.

Although signs and witnesses are indispensable because, thanks to them, others are attracted by truths that are meant and witnessed, and therefore prompted to take them seriously, they alone do not give a final answer to the question of truth, since "it is not the subjective commitment that is the decisive criterion of unconditional and universal validity, but ultimately the truth and the objective validity of the same reality or truth witnessed".13 Only in its last referral, the one who recognizes, in faith, the Incarnate Word as true God, the fullness and fulfillment of the Father's promise, does mediation give way to its source of truth, because the revealed content becomes one with the Revelator, credibility becomes self-testimony, and the need to argue disappears in the face of the divine glory that flaunts and abducts. The strategy, if one can speak of strategy, should then be that of placing the interlocutor increasingly in relation to Jesus Christ, the only mediator between God and mankind, so that the multiplicity of signs is actually understood as coming from the one Sign.

##### 5.2.4 The credibility of the lover as faithful to the promised word
Not only does historical relaxation characterize the form of the Jewish-Christian Revelation, but also, and above all, its nature as a **Word spoken out of Love**. Precisely because the **word pronounced by a personal subject and the expression of his condescending love, the divine word takes the form and value of a *promise***. **Those who love do not speak only to communicate, but to promise: the promise expresses the lover's gift to the beloved, the desire to make him share in what he does not yet possess, and sometimes even the intention to surprise him, to arouse his joy. But the lover's credibility is judged on the fidelity of his words, it is judged on the realization of what he has promised.**

The history of salvation, from its beginning in creation, shows that the fundamental logic of the credibility of the Word of God is that of a *dynamic from promise to fulfillment*. Revelation in the Word, the main way in which God communicates Himself to man, is **credible because *this personal word accomplishes what it promises***. From the origin of the religious experience of Abraham and then of Israel, up to the preparation of the coming of Jesus Christ and what is realized in Him, but in a certain way already starting from the situation of the ancestors before God, divine revelation presents itself with the logic of promise and fulfillment, a logic that while giving itself and recognizing itself in history, transcends it, because it is this Word that creates history, not being measured by it. **The word of God is judged credible because God accomplishes in history what he has promised, a fulfilment which he himself can help to recognize through a precise hermeneutics, as explicitly happens with the prophetic word, and in a summary and paradigmatic way with the Paschal Mystery of Jesus Christ.**

Such logic includes a classic idea of credibility as a *judgment* caused by signs, miracles or prophecies, but it places it more clearly within its own theological-fundamental perspective, that of a close correspondence between words and works as desired by the *Dei Verbum* of the Second Vatican Council (*gestis verbisque inter se intrinsece connexis*; DV, 2). The saving interventions of Yahweh (signs and miracles) express a fulfilment that his judgments on history (prophets) help to recognize as a promise. The credibility of the divine word therefore affects both senses of the mutual implication between words and deeds. Moreover, it is a radically available dynamic which can only be fully understood from a Christocentric point of view, since **Christ himself is the Promise and is the Consummation. Without his earthly event and his Paschal mystery, without his appearance as the Son of Israel and his revelation as the Son of God, without the hermeneutics of his Messianicity and the consummation of his sacrifice and resurrection,** we could not understand what God really promised and what he accomplished.

The dynamic promise/completion has a more general breath than a hermeneutics addressed to individual signs: thanks to its Christological perspective it is able to embrace the whole Revelation with a single overall gaze, that of a word that promises Jesus Christ, that is, the fulfillment of the Father's plan.

Reasoning in terms of credibility as a dynamic between promise and fulfilment has the advantage of presenting credibility with the **clear character of a self-testimony**. **God, in fact, cannot base on others or on others the motives that move man to believe his word, but he bases them on himself, as the Lord of history capable of realizing what has been revealed and announced.** This is the reason why Israel, when it intends to revive its faith and give reasons to return to believe in God, overcoming doubts and perplexities, remembers, with all the worship and solemnity of which it is capable, the promises it has received and the prodigious deeds with which it has seen them come true. **"Take care not to forget the things that your eyes have seen," says the editor of Deuteronomy, "and let them not escape from your heart for the whole time of your life: you will also teach them to your children and your children's children" (*Dt* 4:9). Revelation is the story of God's promises and the story of their fulfillment.**

The truth of Revelation is the way in which promise and fulfillment always coincide, without deception: **for God, "to speak the truth" is to say Himself, to say His Promise, to say Jesus Christ**. A saving truth which, like his Word, possesses a historical-eschatological distension. On the contrary, the openness of Revelation to history and its intrinsically historical dimension derive from the fact that God's promises can only be known within this temporal détente [relaxing of tension, over time]: those who are *on the way are* called to believe in its future fulfillment *at home*. Infidelity and betrayal are counter-revelation because they fail to achieve what they promised. But even idolatry is counter-revelation because it is incapable of making true promises. Faith in God's words is faith in the personal promises of love that these words contain; the fidelity required of man is fidelity in keeping the promises with which he too is called to correspond in a pact of love.

Faced with Revelation, human beings grasp the whole truth of their historicity, their freedom and their condition as hearers of the Word. The listening, the memory and the freedom of his decisions, according to what history has taught him by showing him the fulfilment of the promises he has heard, are both the coordinates of his anthropology and the measure of his dignity.

#### 5.3 The reasons for the credibility of Christianity: some cues from Theology and the Magisterium
##### 5.3.1 Understanding the reasons for the credibility of Christianity around the First Vatican Council
The manuals developed from the second half of the 19th century onwards inherited the theological-fundamental programme, which arose in the modern age, articulated as a *demonstration of religiosity – demonstration of Christianity – demonstration of Catholicism*. Two parts of it could be recognized: one comprising the methodological-fundamental aspects of an incipient theology of Revelation (although exposed with the method of neo-scholasticism) to which was already sometimes given the name of Fundamental Theology; the other of strong logical-philosophical content, called Catholic Apologetics. This second part experienced a significant development at the time, both because of the specific cultural juncture in which we are moving - marked by the drives of materialism, rationalism and fideism first, and then modernism - and because of the numerous interventions of the Magisterium on issues that closely concerned this subject.14

The indications contained in Pius IX's encyclical *Qui pluribus* (1846) and in the constitution *Dei Filius* of Vatican Council I (1870), the revaluation of Thomist philosophy carried out some years later by Leo XIII's *Aeterni Patris* (1879), and finally the disciplinary and doctrinal interventions directed against modernism, are translated by the Apologetics of neoscholastic inspiration in the elaboration of a specific treatment of credibility, centred on the proposal of an *analysis fidei* and on a philosophical-rational systematic of *rationes credibilitatis*.

Already in the first half of the 19th century explicit teachings of the Catholic Magisterium could be found about the fact that Christian Revelation possessed *evidence*, that is, reasons for its credibility. The first clarifications offered against fideism provided the occasion. To Louis-Eugène Bautain, his Bishop Le Pappe de Trévern asked him in 1835 to subscribe that "Proof drawn from the miracles of Jesus Christ, sensible and striking for eyewitnesses, has in no way lost its force and splendor as regards subsequent generations." (DH [2753](http://www.clerus.org/bibliaclerusonline/en/dxk.htm#cvo)). To this statement is added that the Resurrection of Jesus Christ possesses "definite proofs" (DH 2754). A few years later, in 1844, it was the Roman Curia that asked the French teacher *not to* teach that "reason cannot acquire a true and full certainty of the reasons for credibility, that is, those reasons that make divine revelation evidently credible (*évidemment croyable*), as are particularly miracles and prophecies, and above all the resurrection of Jesus Christ" (DH 2768).

The contents of these theses will be taken up again in 1846 by the encyclical *Qui pluribus*, where we encounter a more extensive discussion about the credibility of the Christian faith and the role of reason in the act of faith. Recalling the Pauline verse that God must be given a "homage according to reason" ([Rom 12:1](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Romans+12%3A1-2&version=NRSV): λογική λατρεία), the document states that there are "many admirable and luminous arguments (*argumenta*) according to which human reason must be perfectly convinced that the religion of Christ is divine" (DH [2779](http://www.clerus.org/bibliaclerusonline/en/lt.htm#xv)). It is significant to note that the first of the arguments reported there already has a Christocentric reference, when it is pointed out that faith is “confirmed by the birth, life, death, resurrection, wisdom, miracles, prophecies of its author and consummator, Christ Jesus " (*ibid.*). This is followed by references to the predictions of the prophets, miracles, the firmness of the martyrs and the witness of the Church in general, her life and the fruits of the doctrine she preaches.

A few years later, the First Vatican Council (1870) will deal with the relationship between faith and reason in the context of the credibility of Christianity. In chapter 3 of the *Dei Filius* Constitution, dedicated to the Catholic explanation of the faith, the Council speaks of "signs" (*signa*) of the credibility and divinity of Revelation. When the document deals with credibility, one seems to notice the choice to use the term "sign" instead of the term "reason/motive": in the strict sense, the reason/motive for believing is the authority of God, which Vatican I clearly introduces in its definition of faith (cf. DH 3008), because, of faith, God represents the cause, that is, the formal reason. Again according to Vatican I, the *signa* can be traced back to three (see DH 3009, 3012):

- miracles,
- the prophecies,
- the Church.

The Church, in turn, is itself the seat of numerous signs arranged by God for the credibility of the Christian faith to appear, as well as the subject of "marks" (*notae*) characteristics, which make it possible to recognize it as guardian and teacher of the revealed word (cf. DH 3012-3014). It is compared to a "banner raised in the midst of nations" (*veluti signum levatum in nationes*; cf. *Is* 11:12), that is*,* a subject of witness capable of showing all believers that "the faith, which they profess, rests on a very firm foundation" (DH [3014](http://www.clerus.org/bibliaclerusonline/en/lt.htm#ys)). There is no enumeration of the marks that clearly show its divine institution; nevertheless, although the traditional Apologetics proposed up to that moment many distinctive notes for the true Church of Christ, it is legitimate to believe that the Council referred to the four classical marks, towards which the numerous and diversified marks of the treatise *De Ecclesia* converged: that is, its catholicity, apostolicity, unity and holiness.

Of the signs that would make the credibility of the Christian faith appear clear (*ad evidentem credibilitatem fidei christianae... disposita*), making the Church as a whole "a great and perennial reason for credibility" (*magnum et perpetuum motivum credibilitatis*), a list is offered instead (cf. DH [3013](http://www.clerus.org/bibliaclerusonline/en/lt.htm#ys)):

- its marvellous propagation,
- its exceptional holiness,
- its inexhaustible fruitfulness in all good works, because of
- of its catholic unity and
- of its invincible stability.

It should be explicitly noted here that the expression "reason for credibility" is used only once by Vatican I, and in reference to the Church (also presented as *signum*): it will be neo-scholasticism that will make wider use of it, favoring its wide application in Apologetics at the turn of the two Councils.

Vatican Council I presents miracles and prophecies as signs that are properly recognizable by human reason, because they are suitable for the intelligence of all (*signa certissima et omnium intelligentia accomodata*). A canon associated with the chapter *De Fide*, reiterates that it is not possible to deny the existence of external signs (whose origin, that is, refers beyond the inner experience of the subject), nor should it be said that men are moved to faith in revelation exclusively on the basis of internal factors or private revelations (cf. DH 3033). With regard to miracles, another canon condemns those who believe that they cannot be known with certainty or cannot effectively prove (*rite probari*) the divine origin of Christianity (cf. DH 3034). At the same time, the Council's doctrine makes it very clear that the assent of faith is a free act that cannot in any way be traced back to a necessary conclusion of a rational order, because it is realized exclusively with the help of God's grace (cf. DH 3008, 3010, 3035).

*[Embedded Image]*
 *Dei Filius*, chapter 3, [DH 3009-3014](http://www.clerus.org/bibliaclerusonline/en/lt.htm#ys) and [DH 3033-3036](http://www.clerus.org/bibliaclerusonline/en/lt.htm#yx).

##### 5.3.2 The neo-scholastic analysis of the reasons for credibility and their diversification
On the basis of the doctrine exposed by Vatican I and the exhortation to revisit the school of Thomas Aquinas, openly presented by Leo XIII in *Aeterni Patris* (1879), theology undertook a re-proposal of the scholastic method and the logical-philosophical rationality that supported it, seeking its application in various treatises.15 Apologetics, which already traditionally looked to philosophical thought as its recognized interlocutor, largely embraced this methodology, especially to show the conformity of the fact of Revelation with reason (a divine Revelation is possible - God's Revelation has in fact given itself) and to attempt a rational analysis of credibility and the act of faith, that is, the study of the respective role of reason, freedom and grace. The context remained almost always the individual context, i.e. the subject faced with the problem of the reasonableness/possibility of one's own option of faith. This last approach was, after all, a consequence of that "Cartesian" understanding of rationality that became an implicit (and perhaps by now unconscious) background also for the treatment of the faith, of which the individual dimension was privileged by underestimating the ecclesial form.16

Among the authors who had the greatest influence on this approach are Ambrose Gardeil (1859-1931) and, in part, Réginald Garrigou-Lagrange (1877-1964). In his work *La crédibilité et l'apologétique* (1912), Gardeil intends to provide a *rational* demonstration of credibility, believing that it can be traced back to scientific criteria that, in a certain way, oblige the intelligence to take note of it. Rational analysis is aided by the contributions of history and the logic of inference, thanks to which reason can arrive with certainty at the truthfulness of certain conclusions. There are reasons that lead reason to formulate first a "judgment of credibility" (believing is possible) and then a "judgment of belief" (believing is dutiful).

The rational neo-scholastic treatment of credibility used to begin by exposing the content of the *praeambula fidei*, religious and moral truths, known by right reason, which alone do not cause faith but prepare it and are, precisely, like the preambles. A privileged source of doctrine on the preambles of the faith was Thomas Aquinas, deserving of having theorized the role of philosophy in theology in many places. Explicit, in this regard, what was stated in his Commentary on Boethius' *De Trinitate:*

Thus, in the field of sacred doctrine [theology], we can use philosophy in three ways. First of all to demonstrate the rational premises of faith (*praeambula fidei*), which one must necessarily know in order to be able to believe. Preambles of faith are, for example, the truths about God that can be proved by natural reason, such as that God exists and that He is unique; and so are other truths about God and the creatures that faith presupposes.17

The analysis then proceeded with the discussion of the *rationes credibilitatis*, i.e. those reasons that make it possible to believe, making it a reasonable act, traditionally divided into: a) subjective-internal reasons and b) objective-external reasons.18

In turn,

1. the subjective-internal motives were divided into
   - universal (conformity of Christian doctrine with the deepest aspirations of the human heart)
   - individual (experience of inner peace and joy caused by personal adherence to the faith);

1. the objective-external motives could be
   - extrinsic to Christianity as doctrine (evangelical miracles and prophecies) or
   - intrinsic to it (life and witness of the Church, sublimity of Christian doctrine when compared to other philosophical-religious doctrines).

The analysis was subsequently enriched by pointing out the aforementioned reasons as real and proper *rationes credentitatis*, that is reasons whose consideration should have been perceived by the subject as an imperative, something not only reasonable but also dutiful; with them the subject felt asked to formulate a free option and perceived the moral responsibility connected to his choice.

The final option, the one that from the judgement of believing definitively moved towards the act of faith made, was interpreted as caused by the concurrence of divine grace and human freedom, because of the lack of evidence of the truths believed; instead freedom and will, in theory, would not have been involved in the previous stages, because of the objectivity and rationality with which the subject was called to take note of both the preambles of faith and the reasons of credibility and belief. So a summary by Franz Hettinger:

Since revelation obliges us to accept it with faith, it must present itself as divine with a certainty excluding any reasonable doubt. Faith can therefore take over only if there is certain knowledge that we can (*credibilitas*) and must (*credentitas*) believe in revelation as a true and divine revelation. Consequently, revelation must carry in itself certain distinctions - criteria - by which we recognize it with moral certainty as credible and distinguish it from any false revelation.19

In the treatment of Garrigou-Lagrange one can notice the following formulations:

1. With regard to the "rational credibility of the mysteries of faith" this was theologically defined as "the capacity possessed by these mysteries (*aptitudo eorum*) to be believed, inasmuch as, through very certain signs, they appear to our reason as revealed by God". Explicitly criticizing the unorthodox understandings of credibility due to both fideism and rationalism, this is how the French theologian expressed himself:

Catholic doctrine about the notion of the rational credibility of the mysteries of the faith can be reduced to four propositions: 1) Objectively: the mysteries of the faith are rationally credible because the origin of the Christian religion can be duly proven (rite probari) by means of certain signs; 2) Subjectively: Before accepting the faith, human reason can know with certainty this credibility, that is, the divine origin of the Christian religion; 3) The faithful are not required to avail themselves of a scientific demonstration of credibility; 4) However, knowledge that is only probable, neither merely subjective, nor based solely on internal experience or private revelation, is not sufficient.20

1. With regard to the notion of "reasons for credibility", it is stated that they are "signs or characteristics (*signa vel notae*) that manifest as credible the revealed religion, or its divine origin".21 It seems clear that, in this context, the judgement of credibility goes directly to the "fact" of Revelation rather than to its contents. The term "reasons" is used because credibility is a *judgement*, i.e. a reasoned judgement. The reasons for credibility are distinguished from the reasons of faith (authority of God who reveals) and the reasons of belief (obedience due to what we recognize as divine).

A complete analysis of the *actus fidei*, so configured, was and is difficult to solve. By accentuating the evidence with which the subject perceived the reasons for credibility and belief, the act became less free; instead, diminishing its importance, the act ran the risk of appearing less human. Underlying it was a certain impersonal and objective vision of the idea of rationality, even when applied to elements ordered to spiritual realities, and a lack of attention to the psychological dynamics of the real man and his intellectual knowledge.22 The insistence on objective reasons, in order to protect oneself from subjectivism, did not correspond to the recognition of a contextual logic, nor that of an ecclesial breath, the reasons for credibility being almost exclusively linked to a faith that did not go beyond the exercise of the subject. As noted above, the subject *self* of faith still remained to a large extent *the Cartesian "I"*, correspondingly conditioning the theological understanding of the act of faith and its internal articulation.23 Finally, note the lack of an explicit Christological reference, which could only be traced indirectly in the fact that the evangelical miracles had Jesus as their author and that a good part of the prophecies were oriented towards the Redeemer.

The inadequacy of this approach was punctually pointed out by subsequent theology, which in some cases did not fail to accentuate the rational schematicity and anthropological reductivism of the neoscholastic vision. In defence of the latter, however, it should be said that the notion of "credibility" came from later criticism often included in the genre of "certain rational knowledge", while this term was simply meant to indicate the reasonableness of an adhesion judged greater than an opinion but inferior to evidence, and therefore never binding for the subject. In all the *analysis fidei*, moreover, the *rationes credibilitatis* never played the role of formal reasons of faith, always leaving the necessary space for freedom and, with it, the gift of grace. Finally, there was no lack of neo-scholastic authors who, also as a result of the debate established by the rise of personalism and existentialism, also considered the weight of subjective dispositions in understanding the objective value of the reasons for credibility.24

A conversion of the act of faith from logical-rational to personalist terms was carried out mainly by Jean Mouroux (1901-1973). The French philosopher highlighted how the option of faith was oriented towards Someone and not towards a simple objective content, recovering the relational richness also present in the reasons for believing and reopening the path towards a Christological recategorization of the credibility of faith.

With Pierre Rousselot (1878-1915) the reasons for credibility left the field of apologetics and began to be interpreted in an exclusively theological key. Judgement of credibility and act of faith are now considered indistinguishable in themselves: the reason for credibility would be perceived "with the eyes of faith" and would serve to reassure the subject of the full humanity and reasonableness of the act performed. The credibility of faith would not be the conclusion of an argument, but something that Revelation itself brings with it, showing it to the subject.

The theological-fundamental approach of Hans Urs von Balthasar (1905-1988) will develop this last perspective in a particular way, pointing out that the credibility of Revelation is associated with its beauty, its transcendence and its inner coherence, characteristics through which God challenges man by revealing his Glory.

We believe that the judgment formulated by post-conciliar Catholic theology with regard to the "rational" dimension of faith in Revelation, probably also because of the attraction aroused by the thought of theologians belonging to the Churches of the Reformation, Karl Barth in particular, was expressed in a manner that was perhaps too severe, contributing indirectly to impoverishing the very notions of rationality or reason, even when these terms could have indicated a broader semantic field, in a synergistic and not extrinsic relationship with the faith, respecting an anthropology more attentive to the experiential and existential dimensions.

In the criticism of an overly rational approach to credibility (with the preambles of the faith often also associated with this), however, with the effect, in our opinion, of depriving the theological discourse of those necessary philosophical pre-comprehensions that make possible any discourse on God and on man's openness/expectation towards the Absolute, thus weakening the universal scope of Revelation, and therefore also its intelligibility.

##### 5.3.3 Signs of credibility according to *Dei Verbum* and other documents of the Second Vatican Council
Taking up the best insights of personalism and Christocentrism, the ways in which the Second Vatican Council speaks of the credibility of Revelation are centered around three interrelated points of reference: Christ, the Church, the Christian witness. The most significant documents in this regard are, in addition to *Dei Verbum*, also *Gaudium et spes* and *Ad gentes*.

We know that chapter IV of the schema *De deposito fidei pure custodiendo*, which preceded the subsequent drafting of the *Dei Verbum*, had five different paragraphs dedicated to the "signs of Revelation".25 The first referred to external signs in general; the second spoke of miracles and prophecies; the third centered on the resurrection, in connection with messianic prophecies and the very words of Christ; the fourth was dedicated to the sign of the Church; and the fifth dealt with the meaning of internal signs and testimonies. Of this scheme, soon outdated, only the third paragraph was then developed, that relating to Christ, which flowed into no. 4 of *Dei Verbum*.

##### A first systematic approach to credibility
René Latourelle points out that the documents of Vatican II modify, above all, the lexicon of "theology of credibility". If Vatican Council I and the theology of the first half of the 20th century had employed terms such as *argumenta*, *facta divina*, *signa*, *credibilitas*, *motivum credibilitatis*, *testimonium*, to the point of using verbs such as *demonstratio,* Vatican Council II would essentially prefer the terms *signa* (*signum* in the singular, often using the plural to speak of the "signs of the times") and *testimonium*.26

If we take as reference the three reasons for credibility indicated by the First Vatican Council, namely miracles, prophecies and the life of the Church, it should be noted that in the documents of Vatican II the word "miracle" appears only 4 times, 3 of which in a context pertinent to our theme, and always in a proposition in which the miracle has Jesus Christ as its subject.27 The word "prophecy" does not appear in any of the 4 Council constitutions. Obviously in *Dei Verbum* there are references to the prophets, but the term will only be used twice in relation to the relationship they establish between the First and the New Testament:28 It is not on the content of the prophecies or the fact of prophecy that the Council dwells, but on *those who* speak to us in the name of God.

There are many references to the Church. It is possible to trace the idea that in it man meets the answer to his deepest aspirations. However, there is almost always a personalist transposition, from the Church to Christ, from the Church to God: "The Church knows well that only God, to whose service she is devoted, responds to the deepest desires of the human heart, which can never be fully satisfied by earthly goods" (GS, 41). If it is true that Christian doctrine responds to man's needs and questions, the Council stresses above all that it is Jesus Christ who reveals to man the *mystery* of man (cf. GS, 22): he is a Person, not a doctrine, who frees man and satisfies his aspirations. The moral sublimity of Christian doctrine, another sign of traditional Apologetics, has in Christ its paradigm, on a par with the testimonial works of charity or holiness of life.

In the Council texts, therefore, everything is seen from a Christocentric and personalist perspective: it is Jesus Christ who makes charity, Christian morality or the life of the Church a sign of faith's credibility. The Church is indeed a "sign raised up in the midst of the nations" (as Vatican I, Vatican II also proposes the quotation from Isaiah, cf. UR, 2), but a sign that leads to Christ, because it is in Christ, *lumen gentium*, that the Church has the raison d'être of her existence. His light is reflected in the Church, Christ's *visible* sacrament, especially the unity and peace which he has come to bring to the whole human race.29 In essence, we are witnessing a *Christological concentration* of the signs of salvation, because it is ultimately in Jesus Christ that the Father accomplishes his works and manifests his signs (cf. *Jn* 14:10; 12:29-30).

In his work *Le Christ et l'Église, signes du salut* [Christ and the Church as Signs of Salvation] (1971), René Latourelle proposes a first systematic approach to the conciliar doctrine on credibility, which we can summarize as follows.

First, the credibility of Christianity is traced back to the credibility of the person of Jesus Christ, to which three fundamental characteristics are associated. He is:

1. *fullness and fulfillment of Revelation*, manifesting in himself the entire plan of the Father;

1. *source of intelligibility* to understand any other sign (prophecies, miracles, holiness);

1. *key of discernment* to see clearly how man's deepest aspirations find fulfillment in evangelical doctrine.

Around Jesus Christ are therefore gathered signs and motives that the previous Apologetics would have called both internal and external, while inviting the interlocutor to note not the individual prophecies, but the internal coherence of the content of the whole Revelation, which points to Christ as its noetic and hermeneutic fullness.

Secondly, the Council proposes a second great sign, the Church, which in turn must be understood as a *sign of Christ* and corroborates the credibility of the faith in three substantial ways:

1. by its *preaching* and *sacramental action*, which show how the means of salvation desired by Christ himself remain in it;

1. with the signs of its *unity, holiness* and *historicity*, which are also proposed in their paradoxical dimension, that is, as tension between the earthly-human condition of Christians and the sublimity of the fruits that the Church is able to produce despite the limitations of that condition;

1. with *the witness of life*, particularly that of martyrdom, the latter understood not only as an event linked to a foundational era, but as a sign that always accompanies and will always accompany the preaching of the Gospel.

In a certain way there is a reconversion of the *tres viae* - *historical*, *empirical* and *notarum/marks* - used by previous apologetic theology about the Church: the *historical way* will insist more on the continuity of the message delivered than on the historical reconstruction of a precise foundation; the *empirical way* will be included within the category of witness, charity and martyrdom; the *notarum way* willbe presented with an eschatological hermeneutics in the perspective of paradox: that is, by emphasizing the tension between the earthly-human condition of the assembled Christians of the Church and the sublimity of the fruits that the Church is able to produce despite the limitations of that condition.

##### Christological and personalist nature of the signs of salvation
As already highlighted by Henri de Lubac, for Vatican II the Church is not just any sign, but a *sacramental* sign of Christ, not only indicates it but also makes it present, according to a salvific dynamic analogous to that of the sacraments. It is Christ who calls men and women to be part of the universal Church, he makes it possible for the Church to be a visible sacrament of salvation and union of all men and women with God, he who by his presence and works manifests the mystery of the Church from its foundation (cf. LG 2:1, 9, 5). In what the Church presents to the world it is he himself who shows himself because in his body, which is the Church, he makes himself present to us (cf. LG, 14).

Along the first part of *Gaudium et spes*, the internal reasons for credibility, as well as the objective sublimity of the Church's doctrine, are reread and developed in a Christocentric key. Christ is the new man, the only one who reveals man to man and by following him man becomes more man; in him the vocation of the whole human community to unity finds meaning and he is still the foundation of the dignity and respect due to every human person (cf. GS 22, 41, 24, 27-29, 32, 38). In his Paschal mystery the whole of human activity is elevated and finds its meaning, and it is around the mystery of the Incarnation that the Council shows how mutual relations between Church and world find their model, enabling the Church to provide the world with the answers it seeks (cf. GS, 38, 40-44), and then solemnly conclude that "The Lord is the goal of human history, the focal point of the longings of history and of civilization, the center of the human race, the joy of every heart and the answer to all its yearnings" (GS, 45).

When the Council speaks of the holiness of the Church or her doctrine, it never does so in an abstract or generic way. The documents speak of the person who proposes the doctrine, or Jesus Christ, and if they speak of the Church in the context of the visibility of the signs, it is because she is a sign of Christ's presence among men.
