The Church "grows visibly in the world" because she is "the kingdom of Christ already present in mystery" (LG, 3). The very nature of the Church, that of being visible but endowed with invisible elements, human and divine (cf. SC, 2; LG, 8), makes it a sign capable of indicating the invisible and the divine through the visible and the human. The Church is *in Christ* sacrament, sign, of the intimate union of the whole human race and of this with God (cf. LG, 1), and of the universal salvation offered to all peoples (cf. AG, 1). It is stated that she remained faithful bride to her Lord without ever ceasing to be a sign of salvation in the world for all men, while at the same time making it clear that she does not ignore how among her members, clerics and laity, there was unfortunately no lack of those who were not faithful to the Spirit (cf. GS, 43; LG, 15). Yet, "the Church has the task of making God the Father and his Son incarnate present and almost visible, renewing herself and purifying herself tirelessly under the guidance of the Holy Spirit" (GS, 21).

##### Christian witness and openness to dialogue
In continuity with Jesus Christ and the Church, the notion of sign is approached by the Council with the concept of witness, which the theology of Revelation has now been able to highlight as one of the leading categories of all salvation history. One of the most frequent expressions in all the documents of Vatican II, the word *testimonium* appears 74 times; more than 110 times if we consider it in its variants witness, testify, etc.

The Council's reference to the witness of life as a sign that attracts - and in negative to the bad example as an element that causes disunity, dismay and even rejection of faith - is constant. "For all Christians, wherever they live, are bound to manifest by the example of life and by the witness of the word the new man, whom they have clothed with baptism, and the power of the Holy Spirit, by whom they have been strengthened by confirmation, so that others, seeing their good works, may glorify the Father and understand more fully the genuine meaning of human life and the universal bond of communion of men" (AG, 11).

The obligation to bear witness to the Gospel as an attitude necessary to spread the faith affects all the faithful, lay people, religious and priests (cf. GS, 43, 39; PO, 3; PC, 25). It is known that the Council Fathers pointed to the lack of witness to the faith as one of the causes of contemporary atheism, a factor which hinders the development of the Church and the spread of the Gospel (cf. GS, 19; UR, 4).

When the witness of a living and adult faith is offered, that is, formed and mature, the children of the Church make Christ and the whole Trinity present. This happens especially through the highest sign, that of martyrdom (cf. GS, 21). It is in any case always from Jesus Christ, witness of the Father and source of all witness, that the coherence of Christian life takes meaning (cf. DV, 4).

We point out, finally, that the Council documents mention, in a pervasive and constant manner, a further attitude by which the Christian, who has accepted the truth of Christ and his revelation to men, presents himself to the world. It is the attitude of *dialogue* and *openness*. Although it is not directly part of a systematic approach to the signs of Revelation, dialogue is a necessary condition for the effectiveness of witness and corroborates it by manifesting the credibility of acceptance and love. Openness to dialogue distinguishes the Christian who respects the dignity and freedom of all people and wants to present to them the good and patient Christ so that they may be welcomed with a free conscience, linked only to the truth (cf. DiHu, 10-11). To dialogue is not to renounce the message of Jesus Christ, but to know how to present it starting from the knowledge of man and his expectations of salvation (cf. GS, 40, 92; AA, 14; AG, 11, 12;, UR, 11). Indeed, it is the Christian message itself that contains within it the foundation of the dignity and respect due to all men, that is, having been created in the image and likeness of God and having all been objectively redeemed, without exception, by the life and sacrifice of Jesus Christ, whose universality and historical-geographical effectiveness knows no bounds. The righteousness with which the Christian, through dialogue, makes present Christ's interest in all that is human, also comes to represent, as a further specification in the genre of witness, a reason that makes the proclamation of the Gospel credible.

##### 5.3.4 Evangelisation and contemporary context: challenges and opportunities
The connection between the apologetic dimension of Fundamental Theology and pastoral theology can be fostered by a better knowledge of the contemporary socio-cultural situation. This is characterized by trends, currents of thought and ways of life, sometimes contradictory, on which it is worth reflecting. Let's mark some of them:

1. The search for God and for the life of the spirit is not entirely dull, although in this search superficial, not infrequently irrational paths are often followed;

1. an implicit desire for God remains hidden many times under a widespread attitude of religious indifference, a position in constant ascent, and in a certain way a majority, especially in countries of Western tradition;

1. there is a "new anthropological sensibility" that moves to act on the basis of personal feelings, emotionality and empathy, turning less to the objective reasons of the action itself; the concrete-particular is preferred to the abstract-universal, the immediate (and the ephemeral) to what promises to be eternal;

1. Even in intellectual circles a certain change has occurred, from a position of atheism or cultured agnosticism to a position of relativism; theoretical-positive atheism seems to have lost philosophical strength and is often transformed into sophisticated propaganda;

1. the Catholic Church seems to be losing authority (as a consequence of the crisis of authority that has arisen in the West since the 1960s) and its teachings are no longer considered a moral point of reference for most of the baptized; in the relations between the Magisterium of the Church and the People of God there are lights and shadows; the Church encounters a certain difficulty in "communicating" to the world (misunderstood teachings, manipulated by the *mass media,* etc.);

1. We are witnessing a progressive shift, more or less from the same era, towards religious disengagement, according to some successive stages: Jesus Christ without the Church-God without Jesus Christ-religion without God-sacre without religion-mystical, or esoteric, without anything sacred;

1. In the Catholic countries of the West a sort of silent apostasy is underway; there is a progressive evolution from the position of "non-practising Catholics" to that of "non-believing Catholics": the sacraments - baptism and marriage - are asked for as signs of a cultural identity and a social tradition that one unconsciously wishes to preserve, but does not recognize (nor know) the main dogmatic truths;

1. However, a congenital attraction for authenticity, loyalty and sincerity, as well as a sensitivity for solidarity and help from those who suffer, which makes young people always able to recognize credible models and follow them, does not seem to have died out in the new generations.

To better understand the roots of the situation of unbelief in which many Western countries find themselves today, it is always useful to reread a well-known page of *Gaudium et spes* about the causes of atheism:

*[Embedded Image]*
 Vatican Council II, *Gaudium et spes*, n. 19

The contemporary situation also brings a further novelty. While it is true that every human being would require that the claim of faith be founded in a certain way, it can be seen, however, that the interlocutor of the twenty-first century, unlike in the past, does not follow a certain current of thought, nor is he oriented towards specific doctrines *only because he judges them credible*. The act of believing today is also oriented towards unusual or not at all taken for granted things.30

On the problematic nature of the term "credibility" when evaluated against the background of weak thinking and the contemporary culture of the ephemeral, many fundamental theologians of recognized authority have pronounced themselves. Yet to give it up would mean, for Fundamental Theology, resigning on a task and service that are specific to it. In addition to the weakening of the relationship with philosophy, the temptation to make such a renunciation has different causes: a shift of interest from proclamation and mission to mere dialogue; the search for contexts that facilitate inter-religious dialogue by reducing the truthful claims of Christianity; proposing instead of the certainty of knowledge the certainty of conscience, replacing the search for evidence of objective scope with an idea of credibility based only on subjective experience; or finally, the lack of relevance of a reflection on *true religion,* compared to what did not happen in the past.

When the evangelizing action of the Christian apostolate is directed to baptized persons whose behavior is desired to correct, orient, improve, because it does not conform to the teachings of Jesus Christ and the Church, it is frequent to hear an answer: our interlocutor is no longer certain that the truth is precisely what is proposed to them, that is, that of the faith in which they were perhaps traditionally educated. Years ago it was enough to remember the importance of a certain moral behavior, for people to recognize its convenience. This happened because society still had a sufficiently Christian *humus* [ground/soil]: it was enough to remember things. It's not like that anymore. The progressive weakening of a relationship with the Church - whose teachings are misunderstood and whose true identity misrepresented - represents for many the first stage of an itinerary that ends with a position of indifference, or an explicit rejection of the Christian faith. This confirms the idea that today's task of evangelizing cannot be separated from the task of uplifting man, of re-educating his religious and metaphysical sense, of encouraging his reason in search of truth and goodness, of restoring him to the fullness of a good human life.

But evangelizing action must also take into account the *personal* conditions of the interlocutor. It is essential to know at what level his intellectual life is situated, whether he has social concerns, sensitivities or personal passions of various kinds. It is also necessary to understand the reasons for his current non-belief: well-founded theoretical doubts, bad examples received or scandals suffered, prejudices of various kinds, stable dependence on erroneous or biased media sources, false images of God and the Church, progressive spiritual cooling due to lack of attendance by the ecclesial community, simple ignorance due to lack of knowledge and training.

In essence, it is a question of understanding whether the interlocutor is a person with a discreet or sufficient Christian formation, received in the past, and therefore only out of neglect has he abandoned the practice of the sacraments and Christian life, or whether, on the contrary, we are dealing with a person without a precise knowledge of Christianity and the contents of Revelation. In the first case, friendship, attendance, the example of the ecclesial community and remembering what once known may be sufficient to achieve the desired change; in the second case not.

Although there are no "techniques" to speak of God, there are certainly "paths" to enter the hearts and intelligence of men. Every evangelizing action is based on witness, especially the witness of Christian *hope*, *joy* and *charity.* Depending on the culture, sensitivity and experience of the interlocutor, one can look for an access that privileges a Christological approach, or an anthropological-existential approach, or even choose cultural-philosophical or biographical-testimonial paths.

Finally, there are some strategic areas to which both the effort of apologetic and pastoral development and the commitment to catechesis and the formation of the People of God should be directed. Let's highlight a few priorities:

- *Education*: strive to ensure that school education recovers depth and extension, access to the great traditions of thought of human culture, which, in themselves, are already an expression of the spirit, and are therefore ready to receive light and fulfillment from the proclamation of the Gospel.
- *Intellectual synthesis of faith and reason*: to elaborate convincing philosophical-theological syntheses so that the progress of knowledge will not be erroneously presented in conflict with the contents of Christian Revelation, but rather will be enlightened by it and placed in relation to Christ's capitalality on creation, with the centrality that his Paschal Mystery assumes in the sphere of human activities on earth.
- *Family*: to provide the family with intellectual content (cultural, anthropological and theological) and adequate formative-spiritual support (a pastoral ministry rooted in the family as the vital cell of the Church), so that it may recognize itself as a protagonist in the transmission and preservation of the faith, and be able to show and argue the fundamental importance of conjugal communion and family virtues for the building of society and for the progress of civil life, is supported by the Church to bear heroic witness to these contents, which are now against the current.
- *Presence of religion in civil life*: recover and develop convincing arguments so that not only religious freedom is respected, but religion itself is recognized as an integral part of the civil and cultural life of peoples.
- *Inculturation of the mass media*: to take up the challenge posed today by the mass media, so that the Christian proclamation can be transmitted also thanks to them, but in ways that enable the public to relate to true witnesses and to bring to them the true content of the Church's faith.
- *Enhancement and evangelization of scientific culture*: to elaborate philosophical and theological syntheses such that the contents of the Christian faith can be presented in a credible way also in the context of scientific knowledge, valuing the instances of the search for truth and the promotion of human dignity inherent in scientific culture and knowing how to direct its technological applications to the common good and the progress of peoples.
- *Formation of priests*: to care for the discernment of the priestly vocation and the integral formation, human and Christian, of priests, so that they may be an example to all God's people and prepare to support Christian communities at a time when confession and the practice of faith will be increasingly hindered, and Christians exposed to forms of martyrdom caused by marginalization and discrimination.

The new evangelization is "a team game". No one can win the battle alone. No ecclesial component can achieve it without the others. Everyone has his role as a living member of the Body of Christ, the body with which the Risen One presents himself to the world.

#### 5.4 God's self-testimony in the First Testament: the credibility of the divine word between promise and fulfilment
As we have already observed, **the dynamic between promise and fulfilment can be considered the fundamental logic of Christian credibility**, capable of embracing the whole of Revelation with a single, overall divine gaze. If Israel continually re-reads its history, reproposing the liberation from Egypt and the protection experienced during its pilgrimage in the desert - a religious experience which is fundamental for this people (cf. *Dt* 26:5-9) - it is not only to transmit its religious and cultural identity with the passing of generations, but above all to remember the fidelity of a God who fulfills his promises. One of the main functions of the Jewish memorial (eb. *zikkârôn*) is to return to the conviction that God has truly intervened in history by doing what he had announced, by carrying out works which the people cannot and must never forget (*Ps* 103:2: "Bless the Lord my soul, do not forget so many of his benefits"; cf. also *Ps* 78:1-4). **The credibility of the divine Word is the fruit of *God's self-testimony*: God bears witness for Himself**.

It is certainly true that a theological analysis of how divine fidelity and the fulfillment of what He promises obliges us to examine, sooner or later, what the biblical text means when it employs concepts such as "signs", "prodigious works", "miracles". However, starting from the concept of God's self-testimony rather than the exegesis and content of such terms, our theological understanding of *credibility* does not depend on a full understanding of the occurrence of those signs and miracles, on which exegetical or historical uncertainties may remain.

While it is true that a "theology of the credibility of revelation" must certainly refer to a "theology of signs", and even to a "theology of miracles", it is also true that a biblical foundation of credibility cannot be reduced to a mere historical-philological study of signs and wonders. The self-testimony that God gives of Himself in His works in history must be evaluated against the background of broader and more unified contexts.

In this regard, we believe that the credibility of the Word, and thus the credibility of faith in the one true God, can be recognized, in the First Testament, in the following areas of witness:

- the confirmation of the omnipotence, fidelity and authority of Yahweh, ratified by the presence of prodigious signs and salvific interventions in history, which bring about the fulfilment of a proclaimed word;
- the appeal to his position as Creator of heaven and earth, as proof and foundation of the truthfulness of his Word and the faith due to it;
- the way in which He, hearing and answering the prayers of the righteous, comes to the aid of those who turn to Him, as a demonstration of His presence among men and confirmation of His identity as the one true God;
- the attestation of the credibility of his Word through the fulfilment of signs announced by the ministry of prophets who speak in his name.

In each of these areas, the context in which the recipients of the Word often find themselves is one of doubt, hesitation, discouragement and trial. Man wonders whether God really exists, whether the words heard are really His, or whether they are only the echo of his imagination or, worse, the illusion arising from his despair. Man asks God for signs, and sometimes God Himself offers them in anticipation of human demands. But also, God provides arguments, invites man to reason with him, reveals to him the hidden reasons for his work so that man may understand the meaning of things and events. He invites him to believe in him, showing him not only what he is able to do, but also what he is able to endure, acquiring credibility in his eyes through his whole attitude in favour of man, *first of all his* faithfulness and his mercy. God accepts the *struggle that* man wants to engage in, and takes on the burden of establishing the credibility of his Word through the revelation of his personality and the works that are his own.

**5.4.1** **The prodigious signs with which the God of the Covenant intervenes in history**

The vocabulary of the "signs" offered by God as self-testimony of the truth of His word shows recurrent terms. The greatest frequency is found in the words "sign" (eb. *'ôt*; LXX: σημεῖον) and "prodigious work" (eb. *môpet*; LXX: τέρας), but there are also many "great deeds of God" (eb. *gedulôt*). The works that God accomplishes can rightly be considered "miracles" (eb. *pele'*; *nipla'ôt*), or signs that arouse admiration and amazement, even if it is not an end in itself, because God does not intend to surprise man, except to dispose his heart to listen and bear witness to what has been said.

The prodigious signs made by God manifest his omnipotence and his superiority over the false gods, providing man with elements to believe that Yahweh is truly present among his people and his word deserves to be welcomed with faith: "How long will this people treat me without respect? And until they believe in me, after all the signs [eb. *'ôt*] I've made in their midst?" (*Nm* 14,11. See also *Es* 8,18-19). The signs and wonders done by God on behalf of Israel are put in direct relation with the faith due to Him as the one and only true God: "Has a god ever tempted a god to go and choose one nation from another with trials, signs, wonders and battles, with powerful hand and outstretched arm and great terrors, as the Lord, your God, did for you in Egypt, before your eyes? You have been made a spectator of these things, that you may know that the Lord is God and that there is no one else but Him". (*Dt* 4.34-35). And again: "Fear the Lord your God, you serve him, remain faithful to him and swear in his name: He is your praise, he is your God, who has done for you those great and terrible things [eb. *gedulôt*] which your eyes have seen" (*Dt* 10:20-21; cf. *2 Sam* 7:23). In the *Book of Exodus* it is God Himself, turning to Moses, to make it clear that the meaning of His intervention against the Egyptians is to lead to faith in the one God: "[I have acted] to accomplish these signs of mine in their midst, and that you may recount and fix in the memory of your son and your son's son how I mocked the Egyptians and the signs I have accomplished in their midst: so you will know that I am the Lord! (*Ex* 10:1-2. See also *Dt* 6:20-25; *Gs* 4:4-7).

The "prodigious signs" with which God moves Israel to recognize its presence and salvific protection are numerous, especially in the books of the Pentateuch and in the historical ones, thickening in the context of the liberation from Egypt (*Exodus*). Their narration remains frequent in the subsequent war contexts that first marked the era of the affirmation of the kingdom of David and then the division of the kingdom of Israel, until the Babylonian captivity (especially in *Judges, in* the two *Books of Samuel, in the* two *Books of Kings*). Quantitatively less, however, such signs are not absent from the prophetic books; on the contrary, they accompany in a certain way the ministry of some prophets, particularly Elijah and Elisha, becoming a way of attesting to their mission among God's people. In sapiential literature, on the other hand, the divine wonders will be present above all as a re-proposal of a memory that never ceases to arouse wonder, moving man, especially in the *Psalms*, to gratitude and praise.

Much of God's wonders maintain a privileged link with the economy of the covenant and with what represents, expresses or accompanies it, since the vocation of Moses. The God of the covenant, Yahweh, before solemnly manifesting himself to him, attracts the attention of his servant by inviting him to see a "marvellous spectacle", that of a burning bush that is not consumed (cf. *Ex* 3:1-4). This makes God's interventions fit naturally into the logic of promise and fulfilment, the gift of the covenant being the hermeneutical place where promises are delivered. Jahweh will confirm his presence next to his people with the passage of the Red Sea and then the Jordan, with what happens inside and outside the Tent where the Ark is kept, with the theophanies connected to the delivery of the tables of the Law, with the numerous signs that guide the "pedagogy of the covenant" along the wanderings in the desert. The prodigies that accompanied the liberation from Egypt are recalled by Deuteronomy with a sufficiently significant formula, operating "with powerful hand and outstretched arm" (cf. *Dt* 4:34; 6:22; 7:19; 11:2; 26:8; 29:2; 34:11), which will last long in Israel's memory:

You have worked signs and miracles in the land of Egypt and until now in Israel and among all men, and have made a name for yourself as it appears today. Thou hast brought your people Israel out of Egypt with signs and miracles, with a strong hand and outstretched arm, and with great fear (*Jer* 32:20-21).

The wounds inflicted on Egypt and its Pharaoh with the intention of making the Israelite people understand the reasons are presented in the biblical text with a singular probative value and therefore called "signs" (eb. *'ôt*; *Ex* 8,19; 10,1; cf. *Ex* cc. 7-11). A summarizing text will present such deeds as "wonders" (eb. *nipla'ôt*) never accomplished by anyone: "Behold, I make an alliance; in the presence of all your people I will do wonders, which were never accomplished in any country or nation" (*Ex* 34:10).

The rich religious symbolism present in the first solemn theophany of the Exodus, where God gives Moses the tables of the Law (cf. *Ex* 19:16-25), does not use the previous vocabulary but draws on a common religious language, proposing to emphasize the authority with which the divine word is spoken, in order to arouse in those who listen to feelings of reverence and adherence to the faith.

The sign of the "devouring fire", which appears in the context of the offering of sacrifices, is of some interest. Already present from the sacrifice that Abraham prepares with four animals, placing each half before the other at God's command and then receiving a solemn confirmation of the covenant (cf. *Gen* 15:7-18), this divine intervention causes the recognition and glorification of God by those who are present: "a fire came out of the Lord's presence and consumed the holocaust and the fat parts on the altar; all the people saw it, sent out cries of exultation and prostrated themselves with their faces to the ground" (*Lv* 9:24; cf. also *Jn* 6:21; *1 Kings* 18:38-39).

From the "economy of prodigious signs" emerges the image of an all-powerful God, who exercises full lordship over history and is therefore able to be faithful to his promises by inspiring faith in his word. The Psalms of praise and thanksgiving are probably the place where one can see more clearly how it was precisely the works of God that rooted a deep faith in the people of Israel, constituting a complex of experiences that now represents the consolidated proof of the credibility of Yahweh, a credibility that must be transmitted through historical-religious memory to those generations who had no direct knowledge of it. *Psalm* 145 is valid for all (cf. vv. 1-13; cf. also *Ps* 78 and 135), where practically all the biblical vocabulary used to express the signs and wonders of God is reproposed in a few lines, reading his work within the canons of an unshakable truth/fidelity. In contexts like this, credibility, fidelity and stability essentially become similar terms, because all aspects converge in the one personality of Yahweh. From a personalist point of view, finally, it is also God's pedagogical action towards his people, as a personal subject, that speaks in favor of their credibility: God protects Israel and educates them to observe the Law, corrects and instructs them, leads them gradually to stages of progressive moral necessity, assists them so that they may remain faithful to the covenant and thus remain the repository of a plan of salvation in favor of all the Gentiles.

**5.4.2** **A God who works wonders: at the origins of the theological notion of “miracle”**

In the light of what has been examined so far one can now place the theological notion of "miracle", which in Sacred Scripture is expressed, as already indicated, with the Hebrew terms *pele'* (miracles, wonders) and *nipla'ôt (wonders),* both linked to the verb *pala'* (equivalent to "to overcome what one can understand or do"). Its biblical foundation lies in man's perception of the "wonderful" associated with God's works.

**The miracle in the saving history of Israel and its theological understanding**

The subject of the miracle is paradoxically man rather than God, in the sense that the terminology that expresses it mainly describes human reactions in the face of an unexpected event, which exceeds expectations, thus causing wonder and amazement. The theological interest of this semantic context lies in the fact that it emphasizes the unprecedented character of the divine work, a work judged on the whole background of salvation history and not only referred to specific individual events. Man remains as if *he had been* surprised by the resources of divine power and mercy, seeing in them a new guarantee of credibility, that of "other-than-human" action, which goes beyond his hopes and expectations, and which therefore cannot be understood as a mere human echo of his existential aspirations. This is a logic that the sacred text expresses unambiguously: "If this seems impossible in the eyes of the rest of these people in those days, will it be impossible in my eyes too?". (*Zc* 8:6; cf. also *Ps* 139:6; *Gb* 42:3; *Is* 55:8); "Why did Sara laugh when she said: "Can I really give birth while I am old"? Is there anything impossible for the Lord?" (*Gen* 18:13-14; echo in *Lc* 1:37). Its value as a *sign* due to an Author refers to the self-testimonial character of the miracle, while the fact that the miraculous works performed by God are a sufficient reason to place him "above all the gods" reveals the role of the miracle in the dynamic faith/credit: "Among the gods no one is like you, Lord, and there is nothing like your works. All the people you created will come and bow down before you, Lord, to give glory to your name. You are great and you do wonders: you alone are God" (*Ps* 86:8-10. See also *Ex* 15:11).

Not very frequent in historical books, most of the occurrences of the terminology that expresses the "miracle" is found in the psalter and in the sapiential, demonstrating its use in a summary key and its marked anthropological dimension (praise, prayer, memory). To be celebrated as prodigious miracles are above all the saving works of Yahweh (cf. *Jer* 21:2; *Mi* 7:15; *Ps* 9:2; *Ps* 78:12; *Ps* 106:7.21-22; etc.). "Wonderful" is also the law and the alliance (cf. *Ps* 119,18.27.129).

Although Scripture had spoken more often of "signs and wonders", with the passage of time it is the theological notion of *miracle* (from the lat. *miror*, wondering) that progressively affirms itself, including both the various "wondrous works" performed by God and collected in the First Testament and, subsequently, those performed by Jesus in the New Testament. It should be remembered that the original scope of the notion of miracle has always been religious, although the term has manifested over time a variety of understandings and lexical uses that have inevitably migrated its meaning to other contexts. In theology, "miracle" means something out of the ordinary, which refers to a sphere of possibilities and activities that go beyond what man is used to knowing and experiencing in his daily life. Hence its natural connection with a sphere of forces and possibilities that belong to something or someone "other-than-human", and thus the **understanding of the miracle as a divine intervention in the world of men**. **There are three dimensions classically associated with miracles: an ontological one (God is the author, as a subject who has power over the being of things), a semiology (a sign containing a certain message) and an anthropological one (directed to man, arousing his attention and wonder)**. From the point of view of its *recognition*, it always implies a certain relationship with the epistemology of the laws of nature.[31](about:blank)

Attentive to the biblical context in which they take place, theology has appropriately pointed out that God's interventions in history and nature are never confined within the dimension of pure omnipotence. They are linked to a message, to a teaching, to the establishment of a new relationship with God: it is in fact primarily to these contents that the miraculous event has and intends to direct. The miracle is certainly connected with faith, which on some occasions seems to be the very condition of its recognition, a manifestation of man's adherence to God's saving works. Although the relationship between faith and miracle remains difficult to understand and represents for theology a theme of difficult solution, the narration and recognition of miracles take place within a horizon of faith, or at least a horizon of humility and openness to the mystery, because it is the experience of an encounter with the divine; if this were not the case, the sacred author would stop at the simple recording of an unusual fact, without connecting it with divine revelation and action. For this reason, the author of the miracle is always God, even when miracles are performed by men, as in the case of Moses or the prophets: it is God who works through them. The miracles take place in an atmosphere of faith, prayer, trust in the covenant, a climate in which the prophet, the righteous, the servant of God is involved and the protagonist, even if some of those who witness what is happening may not initially share it. The miracle, in essence, is a *sign* placed between God and man, a sign that can be accepted or rejected, a sign that invites one to adhere to the word of God, without forcing man to accept it and make it his own.

In this respect, the miracle closely accompanies the phenomenology of religion and somehow shares its outcomes. That is, it can be an expression of genuine openness to transcendence and the possibility of divine revelation, comforted in this by a corresponding judgment of philosophical reasonableness; or it can degenerate into a credulity separate from the exercise of rationality, anxious to find the divine in what is not divine, or worse, trying to subject it to its own ends, imitating its works in counterfeiting and the practice of magic. If the biblical notion of miracle mainly refers, as we have seen, to the idea of prodigy and portentous work, as a condition for the recognition of divine intervention that breaks into the spaces and times of the ordinary, the theological-anthropological notion of miracle keeps intact its connection with the idea of wonder and amazement. In more general terms, it can even interpret man's amazement at nature, at the unfathomable richness of reality and especially at life, thus indicating the experience by which man gains access to deeper layers of intellect and contemplation of being, showing himself capable of recognizing the divine even in what is ordinary.

**Exegetical criticism of the notion of miracle**

As with the exegesis of the miracles of Jesus (which we will deal with in the following pages), the question has also been asked about the realism with which to understand such events. A perplexity about the realism to be attributed to biblical narratives came in the exegetical sphere at the end of the nineteenth century, because of the philosophical criticism of the miracle formulated by various authors of modernity and the Enlightenment. In its most radical aspects, this criticism flows into the program of "demythization" of Sacred Scripture, of the NT in particular, whose protagonists were first Adolf von Harnack (1851-1930) and then Rudolf Bultmann (1884-1976). The narratives of God's miraculous interventions would be literary forms that cover, and in part hide, the original context of Israel's religious experience and, as such, should be identified and removed. A very influential orientation of biblical theology of the twentieth century had already led to an understanding of the covenant that absorbed in itself the relationship between man and God established by creation, advising against too close an association between miracle/prodigy, as a sign of the covenant, and natural phenomena as such, thus avoiding the difficult idea of overcoming or suspending the laws of nature. Moreover, the ontological dimension of the miracle had already been explicitly disregarded by various exponents of modernism, considering it an obstacle for a biblical exegesis in dialogue with scientific thought.

Bultmann's program of demythicization of the NT was aimed at recognizing in the stories of miracles the literary forms of extra-Biblical traditions, especially of the Hellenic sphere, which narrated prodigious healings, probably due to other causes. If for the First Testament, at least for the books that revolve around the religious experience gained during the Exodus and the settlement in the land of Canaan, one cannot speak of a Hellenic mythological influence, the fact remains that various literary forms of a mythological nature were available in the surroundings of Israel, and these could have used them to narrate the prodigious works of Yahweh, thus making the Bulgarian program, in principle, applicable.

The proposal of the German Protestant theologian, in line with his existential exegesis, was to distinguish between *Mirakel* and *Wunder*. To the first term should be attributed the traditional notion of "prodigious event", while the second would express the idea of a "wonder" that the subject experiences when he observes, with the eyes of faith, a certain event that is existentially significant for him. *Mirakel* would have no interest in faith because it would be something to which we cannot access the truth, something of which it is much more reasonable to doubt than to believe; *Wunder*, on the contrary, would express the intensity of our particular relationship with God, which leads us to recognize His action around us, by means of facts not necessarily prodigious, but equally suitable for questioning our faith and revealing a particular message. The miracle ceases to be a concrete event of an objective nature, to become a horizon of subjective and personal understanding. Such a "subjective recognition" would also, according to Bultmann, have the advantage of being able to lead human reason to see in everything the provident action of God. This dynamic would be applicable to the wonders of Israel's saving history and to the Old Testament narratives, in which the anthropological and semiological dimensions would be those privileged by Scripture, without these implying a corresponding ontological dimension.[32](about:blank) Bultmann's attempt was basically aimed at eliminating the need to oblige contemporary man to believe in narratives he considered incredible, directing his sensitivity towards the recognition of subjectively and existentially significant "signs".

This Bulgarian approach does not, however, escape one question: can the entire religious experience of the Jewish people, not least the one by which they judge the credibility of the words delivered to them by Moses and the prophets - ultimately by God - be caused by what we might call "ordinary divine providence", perhaps favored by the resourcefulness and shrewdness of the members of Israel, without the need to invoke any truly "extraordinary" divine work? Can the "extraordinary" be confined only to the gratitude and wonder with which the protagonists save themselves from existentially decisive, but ultimately "ordinary" situations, as wars, migrations or famine are at bottom? In fact, the distinction between *Mirakel* and *Wunder* closely copies the distinction typical of Bultman exegesis between the Jesus of history and the Christ of faith, lending itself to the same limitations: Just as a faith in Christ deprived of all foundational ties with the history of Jesus would not make sense, so a wonder that would not find its origin in miraculous historical events (whose existential understanding can certainly depend on the disposition of the subject) would end up dissolving into a generic feeling of wonder, whose appeal no longer starts from reality, but only from inner experience, when not from simple states of mind. Too deep-rooted and too intense is the religious experience of Israel gained *from the prodigious works of Yahweh,* to believe that psychological, emotional, traditional or mythological factors were solely responsible for its rise. And too (extraordinarily) numerous are the prodigies narrated, regardless of the comprehensible literary forms that have certainly covered the narrations, to believe that they should all be read within the framework of ordinary providence and its corresponding existential perception. The feelings expressed by the psalmist confirm this: "How many wonders [eb. *nipla'ôt*] have you done, you, Lord, my God, how many plans in our favor: no one can compare to you! If I want to announce and proclaim them they are too many to count" (*Ps* 40:6).

The affirmation of an ontological dimension of the miracle must then be distinguished from the question of the choice of the philosophical framework to be used to explain the modalities of God's intervention in nature. The difficulty of philosophical (and scientific) reason to understand adjectives such as "direct", "immediate" or "external" intervention of God should not be ignored, since they are, among other things, qualifiers that are certainly not the best to represent the relationship between God and creation, since it is a continuous and meta-historical relationship, but no less ontological. God is certainly distinct from the created world but not for this reason external to it, and there is an action of which God the creator is certainly the author and the first Cause even if it is carried out through the mediation of second causes. The elaboration of the corresponding interpretative framework must be entrusted both to ontology and to the philosophy of nature, carried out in the light of the theology of creation, with respect for scientific epistemology but without showing any subjection to it, since the sciences remain de facto not competent in the ultimate ontological causes of being and of the nature of entities.[33](about:blank) The miracle is part of man's religious experience and precisely for this reason it also belongs to the religious history of Israel: it is an event, both subjective and objective, in which the subject recognizes without hesitation the effective and operative presence of the divine, feels called to it, welcomes it with gratitude: the Creator places his "signature" on things and man is able to identify it in an unambiguous way.

**5.4.3** **The omnipotence of God as Creator attests to the credibility of His word.**

It is not customary to see in the condition of Yahweh as Creator one of the reasons given to the people of Israel to exhort them to give faith to the Word received. According to a generally acquired view, the primary experience of the knowledge of Yahweh is realized during the history of Israel's liberation from Egypt and during the exodus to the land of Canaan, and it is in the light of this experience that his reality as Creator of heaven and earth would later be understood. The prodigious works of God, and therefore the reasons for believing in Him, are essentially the signs given along the path to the Promised Land, the settlement in it and the historical events that followed it, all signs easily available to a corresponding, subsequent Christological reading. Yet, a complete analysis of the biblical texts, certainly starting from the deportation to Babylon, but with clear indications also in previous eras, reveals sufficient elements to affirm that Israel believes in Yahweh *because* Yahweh is the creator of heaven and earth. Let's take a brief look at the reasons.

Although the words that express God's wondrous works are frequently used to describe God's saving interventions in the history of Israel, much more so than in reference to the cosmos and nature, a more comprehensive contextual analysis shows that the works of creation are also remembered to establish the credibility of God's word. Created nature is an appropriate place to manifest God's self-testimony because it too participates in the word/work logic and promise/completion dynamics that characterize the whole Revelation. The creation is therefore proposed by the First Testament as a masterpiece and original work of Yahweh which gives authority to his word. This is how an entire Psalm expresses it, showing the reason why God's teachings are worthy of faith: *testimonia tua credibilia facta sunt* (*Ps* 93:5):

The Lord reigns, He cloths Himself with majesty: He cloths Himself with strength. The world is stable, it will not falter. Stable has been your throne forever, since eternity you are. They lifted up the rivers, Lord, they lifted up the rivers their voice, they lifted up the rivers their roar. More powerful than the roar of rushing waters, more powerful than the waves of the sea, powerful on high is the Lord. Truly worthy of your teachings! Holiness suits your house for the duration of days, Lord (*Ps* 93).

Thanks to the idea of firmness and stability commonly associated by Scripture with creation, credibility (*credibilis*, or *fidei dignum testimonium*) can then flow into the notion of fidelity, finding in it the same conditions of possibility as a logic of promise/completion. The reference to the created world does not shift God's self-testimony to something else, but rather offers the *locus* where God can express self-faithfulness to Himself as an all-powerful Creator and the subject of a provident plan. **The word of God is worthy of faith because this same word is capable of sustaining the world (cf. *Ps* 33:4-9) and the stability of his judgments is comparable to the stability of the laws which he impressed on creation (cf. *Jer* 31:35-36).**

Since salvation history is capable of binding together creation and covenant, gratitude for a creation received and gratitude for a freedom regained (cf. *Ps* 136), the memorial of the prodigious deeds performed by Yahweh, which Israel recalls with particular extension and solemnity in support of the credibility of the divine words, can begin not infrequently with a reference to **creation, as the first of the works performed by "He who is alone doing wonders** (eb. *pele'*)". (*Ps* 136.4). In chapter 40 of Isaiah, the beginning of the solemn announcement of the future liberation, rich in messianic allusions (cf. *Is* 40:1-48:22), finds in the exhortation to look at creation a point of support so that Israel does not doubt that it will be the object of the imminent divine consolation; as usual, the scenario of creation will serve as a background to judge the falsehood of idols, easily unmasked by the comparison with the creative omnipotence, proper only to Yahweh (cf. vv. 18-26).

The memory of creation becomes a strong point for manifesting faith in the power of God, as in the opening verses of some of the most solemn prayers formulated by the righteous when, desiring to remain faithful to the God of the Fathers, they must expose themselves to risk. This is how Judith expresses it: "God of my father, God of the inheritance of Israel, Lord of heaven and earth, creator of the waters, king of all your creatures, hear my prayer! (*Gdt* 9:12); Mordecai: "Lord, Lord, king who rules the universe, all things are subject to your power and there is no one who can oppose you in your will to save Israel. You have made heaven and earth and all the wonders that lie beneath the firmament. You are the Lord of all things, and there is no one who can resist you, Lord" (*East* 4:17b-c); Hezekias: "Lord of hosts, God of Israel, who sits on cherubim, you alone are God for all the kingdoms of the earth; you have made heaven and earth. O Lord, put out your ear and listen" (*Is* 37:16-17); Jeremiah: "Ah, Lord God, with your great power and your strength you have made heaven and earth; nothing is impossible for you" (*Jer* 32:17). Note, in the latter case, the strong parallel with the analogous expression - powerful hand and outstretched arm - which recalls the prodigies accomplished in the context of liberation. Moreover, in the well-known narration of the *Second Book of Maccabees*, the exhortations made by the Jewish mother to her children to accept martyrdom valiantly, once again offer cosmological references with the aim of strengthening faith in the God of the Fathers (cf. *2Mc* 7,21-23 and 28-29).

A great proof of God's self-testimony in creation, delivered in order to arouse full confidence in Him, is that offered to us by the conclusion of the *Book of Job,* in a narrative context that goes beyond the history of Israel to place itself in a sapiential tradition of much wider boundaries. As a guarantee of the existence of a divine Providence capable of guiding the whole of creation, and to which even man must entrust himself at the moment of trial and contradiction, the sacred author presents one of the most beautiful descriptions of the wonders of nature and of the wisdom with which God guards and nourishes his creatures, watching over them (cf. *Gb* cc. 38-42). That the awareness caused by the most careful consideration of creation has a probative value for Job, and does not follow only aesthetic canons, is demonstrated by the conclusions he comes to: "Then Job began to say to the Lord: I understand that you can do everything and that no project is impossible for you. Who is the one who, being ignorant, can obscure your plan? I really have exposed things that I don't understand, things that are too wonderful for me, things that I don't understand. [...] I only knew you by hearsay, but now my eyes have seen you. Therefore, I repent and repent over dust and ashes" (*Jb* 42:1-6; see also 40:3-5). **When man, feeling pain and abandonment, questions the existence of his Creator, it is to creation that he must look. If made with sincere eyes, such a look *shows* a valid reason to believe in God, to trust in His promises and, even more radically, to no longer doubt His existence. This last "reason for credibility", to call it in some way, has as broad a scope as the appeal made by the Word of Wisdom as a whole, including its philosophical, religious and moral resonances.**

We can then conclude that, for Israel, to believe in Yahweh *because* He is the Creator does not mean to follow a speculative philosophical path, on the basis of which to deduce the existence of God as the Founding of the real, then placing this conclusion in relation to the knowledge of His Lord, as this is provided by His interventions in saving history. Israel is rather solicited to a kind of spontaneous philosophy, which is at the same time an exercise of authentically religious thought, which leads him to realize that **if God created heaven and earth, if God administers His Providence over all creatures, then it is reasonable to believe His word, even when promises are late in coming true or discouragement and disappointment seem to cast doubt on the fidelity and truth of Yahweh. The existence of the Creator seems, in essence, more readily available to reason than is sometimes the existence of other arguments to sustain faith in a Promise that is apparently thwarted by exile, abandonment or pain; knowledge of the existence of *the* Creator can then be a reason for the credibility of Yahweh and his Word,** nourishing the expectation and hope of the people and individuals. It protects from idolatry, directing Israel and its interlocutors towards the one true God, the Creator of heaven and earth.

**5.4.4** **God intervenes in response to those who call upon Him or question Him**

In the context of the Babylonian captivity, Azariah, one of the young men condemned to martyrdom for their faith in Yahweh, according to the account proposed by the *Book of Daniel,* solemnly declares: "Such is our sacrifice before you today and be it pleases you, because there is no disappointment for those who trust in you". (*Dn* 3.40). The conviction that God *does not disappoint*, that is, he intervenes in favour of the righteous who invokes him, crosses the books of the First Testament in a way that is too widespread and reproposed in various ways to go unnoticed: "Know this, the Lord does wonders [eb. *nipla'ôt*] for his faithful: the Lord hears me when I invoke him" (*Ps* 4:4; cf. 25:2), says the Psalmist. **Prayer, the place of dialogue between God and man, also participates fully in the logic of promise/completion which founds and structures the credibility of Revelation: God promises the praying person assistance and salvation, while those who pray base their faith on the help given by the faithful and merciful God, a support which he himself has witnessed in the past or which has come to him**. **God's interventions in favor of those who invoke Him and seek Him are also a manifestation of His self-testimony, because thanks to them the existence and effective action of Yahweh is confirmed, thus healing the disbelief of those who denied or doubted Him.**

From the passage of the Red Sea to the temptations of discouragement in Massa and Meriba, from the fall of the walls of Jericho to the revolt of the Maccabees, the prodigious interventions of the God of Israel answer, directly or indirectly, the question that man returns to ask himself at crucial moments in his history: "is the Lord among us, yes or no?". (*Ex* 17:7; see also *BoG* 6:13). God's saving interventions in favor of those who invoke Him fulfil in essence the divine promise never to abandon His own. For "blessed is he who trusts in the Lord" (cf. *Ps* 84:13; *Pr* 16:20), more than those who trust in human security. The latter are often based on an accessible and evident support, while the task of the righteous is to rely on a word believed and accepted with faith, even if circumstances are adverse and might reasonably doubt its truthfulness: "He who trusts in the Lord is like Mount Zion: he does not waver, he is stable forever". (*Sal* 125.1).

It is in particular the Psalms that celebrate the wisdom of those who trust in the Lord. And the Psalms are also the place where the reasonableness of the faith of the suffering and persecuted just person emerges, whose prayer is always heard (cf. *Ps* 30, 31, 40, 107, 118, etc.). Thus the Messianic *Psalm* 22, which reproposes in dramatic tones the sufferings and hopes of the righteous, his humiliation and salvation, his death and resurrection: "Praise the Lord, you his faithful, give him glory all the lineage of Jacob, I fear him all the lineage of Israel; for he has neither despised nor disdained the affliction of the poor, his own face has not hidden from him but has heard his cry for help" (vv. 24-25).

Emblematic in this regard are the charms of the suffering Servant of Yahweh, whose complete reading reveals a dynamic of pain and consolation, of apparent defeat and final redemption (final result in *Is* 53:10-12). Jeremiah's prayer (cf. *Jer* 20:7-18) is very rich, an expression of intense feelings of uncertainty and desolation, but at the same time a place of trust in one's liberator: "Lord of armies, who feels the righteous, who sees the heart and mind, may I see your vengeance upon them, for I have entrusted my cause to you" (v. 12).

Such an attitude on the part of man, an explicit sign of recognition and a judgement of credibility towards the divine word, can only be understood in the light of a love of fidelity and mercy through which God himself has testified in the past that he was close to those who invoked him (cf. *Ps* 13:6; 21:8; 32:10; 44:7-9; 52:9-11; 118:8-9; cf. also *Prv* 3:5; 28:25; 29:25, etc.). A love in which even a non-Israelite like Job in some way trusts, as shown by his careful behavior not to deny God or curse him, despite the tribulations he endures, because he is inwardly certain that God's goodness and justice, previously known to him, are worthy of faith: "if we accept good from God, why shouldn't we accept evil?". (*Gb* 2.10).

In this regard, the narrations of prodigies performed by God to defend the righteous who wish to remain faithful to him appear sufficiently significant. This is the case of Elijah who invokes the true God before the many prophets of Baal and in whose favour Jahve brings down fire on the altar erected for him in the sacrifice prepared on Mount Carmel (cf. *1 Kings* 18:20-40). Thus, in the second exile, the episodes of Daniel in the lions' den (cf. *Dn* 6,17-29) or that of the three children in the burning furnace (cf. *Dn* 3,19-100), narrate prodigious interventions with which God saves his chosen ones in the trials to which their persecutors had subjected them. These are events in which the divine wonders have as their purpose to reaffirm who the true God is, going so far as to convert, as in the case of Nebuchadnezzar and Darius, the hearts of the oppressors themselves. Beyond the literary genre or the historical value to be associated with such narratives, they convey a precise conviction of the people of Israel, that God gives faith in his promise not to abandon the just suffering.

Sometimes God's self-testimony comes in response to the prayers of the weak and oppressed, to show through works that He will confirm through them the credibility of His word. Even if at times such works do not manifest themselves with the external character of wonders, they are equally perceived by those concerned as proof of God's action in history and proof of His presence among His people. The context of prayers invoking God's protection is often that of a solemn memory of the history of liberation and salvation experienced by the Chosen People, a confident profession of faith in the promises made to the Fathers. Thus the pleas of Mordecai and Esther to thwart the plot woven by Aman against the Jews of Mesopotamia (cf. *East* 4,17a-z); the prayer of Judith before facing Holofernes (cf. *Gdt* 9,2-14); that of Hezekias king of Judah to ask God for justice against Sennacherib king of Assyria (cf. *Is* 37:16-20); the heartfelt invocation of Jeremiah before the invasion of the Chaldeans into Jerusalem, which will end with the divine judgment condemning Zion as having fallen into idolatry, but will at the same time promise its subsequent benevolent restoration in the context of a new, more spiritual covenant (cf. *Jer* 32:16-44).

God does not show his credibility only by meeting the prayer of the suffering Israelite who trusts in him, saving him from his enemies and his tribulations. Trusting in the one true God is also the attitude of the wise man. God's teachings are credible because they are a guarantee of success in moral and social life, and such are revealed to those who live them uprightly. Even the books of wisdom know the exhortation to "trust in the Lord with all one's heart" (cf. *Pr* 3:5; 28:25; 29:25), because "he who trusts in the Lord will not be disappointed" (cf. *Sir* 32:24); "fearing God" is a principle of knowledge, a guarantee of success in life, a sure outcome in one's own work (cf. *Pr* 1:7; 9:10; 14:2; 15:33; 16:6; *Sir* 19:18; *Qo* 7:18), because "the fear of the Lord prolongs the days, but the years of the wicked are shortened" (*Pr* 10:27; cf. 19:23). To affirm in different contexts that Wisdom goes to those who desire it and recognize it as the most important good is after all a way of witnessing, in the language of sapiential literature, that God Himself comes to those who seek Him with all their heart, with a search that is already prayer (cf. *Wis* 9:9-12; 15:3; *Sir* 1:9-10; 25:10-12). Just as the persecuted Israelite is certain that Yahweh will intervene by being close to those who suffer because of him, so the wise man is certain that once possessed the fear of God and his Wisdom, with them God will make himself present to him, granting him all other things: "Wealth and power lift up the heart, but even more the fear of the Lord. With the fear of the Lord nothing is lacking, with it there is no need to seek another help" (*Sir* 40:26-27).

**5.4.5** **The 'signs' and the testimony of the prophets**

Prophecy is certainly one of the privileged places to approach the logic of credibility. We are not referring now to the fact that prophecies have historically occupied an important place in legitimizing the divine origin of a revelation, especially in the usual articulation between Old and New Testament, realized around the Christological fulfillment of the Word. Rather, we are referring to the fact that, within a logic of credibility between promise and fulfilment, even in the Old Testament context of prophecy we can find some "proofs" that God seems to want to offer his people so that they may believe his Word. Beyond the debate provoked by 20th century theology about the apologetic value of an "argument of prophecies", the **re-reading of Old Testament prophecy in terms of God's self-testimony recalls the relationship between prophetic ministry and two essential categories of credibility: sign and witness.**

The **prophet proposes signs and his life is itself *a sign***. The action of the prophet and his preaching are studded with signs that refer to the Other, whose presence is meant to indicate effective in the midst of the people, to communicate a word of consolation, but also of exhortation and judgment. The prophet is in himself a sign: his preaching and his works, his life and death, could not be understood if this man had not been totally conquered by the Word of Yahweh, if he had not given his entire existence to Him.

In this sense the prophet is something more than a sign, because he is a *witness*; he does not indicate the presence of Yahweh and his law in a merely objective or extrinsic way, but he makes the saving Word present with his whole person, with the testimony of his life. The prophet bears witness to the Word by his holiness, by his commitment to preach it in spite of the consequences it brings with it, by his zeal for the works of the Lord, and in a special way by defending the weak, the poor and the widow. **The prophet becomes a solemn and authoritative witness especially when the Word he has received is delivered by him as an interpretation of the past and an announcement of the future, thus offering a precise testimony of the lordship of Yahweh over history.** Both the economy of the prophetic signs and the economy of the personal witness of the prophet are dispensed according to a logic that is always that of the dynamic relationship between promise and fulfillment; and this is both because Prophecy is after all a Promise, **since it is a word pronounced in history and on history, whose fulfillment it announces, and because the Word "is fulfilled" in the life of the prophet, becoming himself, with his preaching and his existence, the *sign of a fulfillment***.

As he recalls the gift of the covenant and the demands of the law, the prophet often recalls the signs and wonders with which Yahweh showed compassion to his people so that, mindful of this, Israel might strengthen its faith and convert (cf. *Jer* 32:20-22; *Bar* 2:11-13; *Mic* 7:15-16). But the prophet also performs "signs and wonders", extraordinary events which are cited as proof of the veracity of the word of God delivered to him: the chastisements against the house of Eli (cf. *1 Sam* 2:33-34); the drought caused by Elijah's prayer (cf. *1Kings* 17,1) and the miracles he performed in Sarepta, including the resurrection of a dead man (cf. *1Kings* 17,14-15 and 23-24); the dispute on Mount Carmel and the severe refutation of the prophets of Baal (cf. *1Kings* 18,20-39).

In the case of the prophet Elisha, there is a veritable cycle of miraculous interventions (cf. *2 Kings* 2:19-22; 4:16-17; 4:32-37; 5:1-15; 13:20-21; etc.), the aim of which, however, seems more to be to offer a popular narrative framework that exalts the deeds of the prophet (cf. *2 Kings* 8:4: "Tell me all the wonders [*gedulôt*] performed by Elisha") than to give credibility to specific teachings of Yahweh.

On some occasions, the sign realized by the prophet has the explicit purpose of gaining credibility in the eyes of his listeners, like the sign given by God to King Hezekias through the mouth of the prophet Isaiah: "I will deliver you and this city from the hand of the king of Assyria; I will protect this city. From the Lord be this to you as a sign [eb. *'ôt*] that the Lord will keep this promise He has made to you. Here, I turn back ten degrees the shadow on the sundial, which has already gone down with the sun on Acaz's clock". And the sun went down ten degrees on the scale that had descended" (*Is* 38:6-8). There are also other signs that are provided to serve as a criterion for ascertaining that what has been announced has now been accomplished: thus, for example, the sign of an altar to the Lord that will be erected in Egypt as proof of the future conversion of this country (cf. *Is* 19:19-20); the signs that will accompany the definitive conversion of Israel to the true God (cf. *Is* 55:12-13; 66:19), or the signs that will show the coming of the Messianic era (cf. *Is* 9:1-6; 11:1-16).

Finally, the prophet presents the sign of his symbolic actions. With them he wants to make clear to all the relationship between Yahweh and his people: the weight of his past actions and the events that await him, the judgment of Yahweh and the mercy that will follow, the betrayal of his beloved wife Jerusalem and the forgiveness of the bridegroom, the constant need for conversion and the call to meekly accept God's action (cf. *Ez* cc. 4-5; *Ez* 12,1-7; *Hos* 1,2-8; *Jer* 19,1-13 and 18,2-10). The explicit recourse of the prophets to an "economy of signs", even in the diversity that this term assumes in the meaning, prediction or confirmation of the word announced, suggests at bottom the idea that human intelligence is invited to rely on sensitive elements in order to better examine the content of what is proposed to it and, above all, to grasp whether, beyond the word and in the foundation of the word, there is God as author. Although the prophets received a Word which alone would have been sufficient to manifest its truth and power, the sign is equally invoked, almost demanded, by man; the sign remains available to offer its mediation and referral, demonstrating that the appeal is addressed to the whole man, and that God really wants to help him to understand.

Without prejudice to the difference between prophetic preaching and the vaticinium - which is well shown by the different moral profile of the *Nabi towards the different* exponents of the divinatory arts - it should be noted that Sacred Scripture speaks of *prediction* as one of the ways in which Jahweh confirms the truth of his word: "The first facts, behold they have come, and the new I foretell them; before they come, I will make them known to you" (*Is* 42:9). Prophetic literature is studded with predictions - times of war and peace, times of famine and opulence, deportations and return home, victories and defeats, abandonment of God and restoration of His love - whose subsequent coming true is cited as proof of the omnipotence of Yahweh, the stability of His word and the effective action with which He carries out His plans of conversion and salvation (cf. *Jer* 34:1-5; 44:24-29; *Am* 4:6-12; *Ez* 24:1-14). After all, history is the test chosen by God Himself to ascertain the truth of the word of the prophet, and thus the truth of what He has communicated: "Perhaps you could say in your heart, 'How will we recognize the word that the Lord has not said? When the prophet speaks in the name of the Lord and it does not happen or come true, the Lord has not spoken that word" (*Dt* 18:21-22). The great formulations of the faith of Israel and the frequent, solemn repropositions of the memorial of God's actions present a hermeneutics of past events, indicating the truth of what Yahweh had announced through his prophets (cf. *Dn* 9:3-19).

It then contributes directly to giving credibility to the word delivered by Yahweh, the witness of life and personal conduct of the prophet himself, the moral strength of his preaching, his choice to proclaim the word of God to the point of accepting its most extreme consequences. The evaluation of the moral profile of prophets as men of God is not only available a posteriori and in an abstract sense: the moral conduct of the prophet, or rather his *testimony of life,* was also considered by his contemporaries as a criterion for discerning the true from false prophets. The people somehow "recognize" the credibility of those who speak in the name of God (cf. *Ez* 13:22). Unlike the false prophet, who is condescending and yielding to the wishes of influential evildoers, and sometimes to those of the entire people, the true prophet is an inconvenient character who does not seek the favor of others (cf. *Ez* 13:1). If there is always the possibility that a false prophet will produce signs to attract others behind him, this can also have the character of a "trial" (cf. *Dt* 13:2-5), and the people faithful to God have the capacity to expose the deception. The true prophet denounces evil wherever it appears, not excluding the class of religious leaders or the king's house, as shown by Jeremiah's invective against the priests of the temple (cf. *Jer* 7:1-27) or Nathan's courage in deploring King David's immoral behaviour (cf. *2 Sam* 12:1-15). The prophets of Israel know how to take the defense of the poor and destitute, the exploited and the persecuted, remembering the vanity of riches and blaming the immorality of idolatry and violence, as shown very well by the ministry of Amos and Abacuc.

The lives of Jeremiah and Daniel, Elijah or Samuel, are as many stories of fidelity to a word received and made their own, whose truth is confessed with the testimony of life: they are therefore presented as *credible* subjects, but subjects made such by a Word that all works in them, and therefore once again an expression of Jahweh's self-testimony.

**5.5** **The person of Jesus of Nazareth, the center of the credibility of Christian Revelation**

The gain of a Christocentric-personalist perspective in understanding the credibility of Revelation leads in essence to the assertion that **instead of *reasons to believe in Christ, one* should rather speak of a single reason for this faith. This reason is Christ himself**.[34](about:blank) In the economy of signs of credibility, Jesus Christ offers in Himself the sign that He is truly what He says He is.[35](about:blank) The reason for faith in Christ is Christ himself, because the credibility of Christianity is ultimately the credibility of Jesus.

In traditional apologetics, this Christological centrality was less marked, not because his Person was not mentioned, but because his mediating function was preferably emphasized. From a Christocentric perspective, **the works of Jesus** are significant for the faith not only because they manifest, through **the performance of miracles**, the living presence of God; they are highly significant above all **because the work of Jesus of Nazareth expresses an attractive and credible humanity.** In his humanity, Jesus always shows himself inclined to mercy and forgiveness; in him coexist sweetness and fortitude, charity and justice, detachment from material goods and their righteous use, the most passionate love and the most perfect chastity. **Man recognizes in this humanity what he feels called to and what he would like to live, the answer to his questions, his desires, his deepest anxieties.**

However, this offer of meaning given by Christ to man is never totally normalized by man's expectations, nor does it find meaning only in the light of those expectations. As we shall see, the ultimate horizon of the believing conscience is not a single demand for meaning, but openness to the gift and to the new and exceeding logic of the Paschal Mystery. In this section we will try to follow an itinerary that leads us to recognize the credibility of Christian revelation in the very heart of the mystery of Jesus Christ, the Word incarnate, crucified and risen.

**5.5.1** **Extra-Biblical historical testimonies about Jesus of Nazareth**

The Church's Magisterium, theology and the pastoral perspective of the new evangelization have long suggested placing the person of Jesus Christ, his credibility and his Paschal Mystery of salvation at the centre of the proclamation. This is tantamount to bringing the apostolic kerygma back to the center, "Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, who died for our sins and rose from the dead", but without worrying anymore, as in past decades, about whether or not this means doing a kerygmatic theology. The core of the announcement is and will always remain this, and so the Church transmits it throughout history. Sometimes, however, there is less reflection on the fact that the grammatical subject of the sentence is not Christ, nor the Messiah, nor the Son of God, but *Jesus of Nazareth*. Far from wanting to introduce any separation between the Jesus of history and the Christ of faith, we just want to observe here that the whole proposition is based on the intelligibility of a subject, and if this subject was not known, or we did not have access to it, or did not know how to know it, the whole kerygma would lose sense, because our interlocutor would not *know who we are talking about*.

**Information about Jesus from paganism**

Primitive extra-Biblical sources about Jesus of Nazareth and his followers are scarce.[36](about:blank) For almost two centuries, the pagan intellectual milieu had underestimated the socio-cultural significance of the new *sect,* especially in regions of the empire far from Asia Minor where it had emerged, justifying to a great extent the meagreness of comments. Although at the beginning of the second century the teachings of the Master of Galilee could already be perceived as a *philosophy* by intellectuals converted to Christianity, as is the case, for example, with Justin, they did not yet constitute a doctrine examined by the Stoics or the Platonic schools. With a few rare exceptions, the pagan intellectual environment gradually became aware of the relevance of the Christian message only between the end of the second and the beginning of the third century.

Among the testimonies that come from sources independent of preaching or the writings of Christians, there is certainly that collected by Publius Cornelius Tacitus (55 ca.-120) in his *Annales*, written around 116-117. It was a work destined to tell the history of Rome from the year 14, the date of Caesar Augustus' death and the beginning of Tiberius' empire, until the year 68. In the fifteenth book, the Roman historian tells that the Emperor Nero held the Christians of Rome responsible for setting the fire that destroyed large areas of the capital in July 64. The emperor's accusation was followed by harsh repression, the cruelties of which are described in detail; probably imperial police action rather than organized persecution like those that would take place in later centuries. To be captured were first "those who confessed" and then those who came from them reported as sympathetic to their cause, which in this case is certainly not the origin of the fire, but an *odio humani generis*, which suggests the accusation of irreligiousness of which Christians, as Christians, began to be accused in the empire. The name of Jesus does not appear, probably because that of Christ was already directly connected with the name of his followers, but the historical references to Tiberius, Pontius Pilate and the capital of Judea are clear enough to establish the identity of the founder of the movement in question.

*[Embedded Image]*
 *Annales*, Chap. XV, No. 43-44.

A second important testimony is the correspondence between Pliny Caecilius the Second, known as Pliny the Younger (c. 61-113), governor of Bithynia and Pontus, and the Emperor Trajan. Between 111 and 113 the official consulted the emperor on how to proceed against the Christians in those regions.[37](about:blank) Pliny informs him that in Asia Minor worship is spreading to a certain Christ, worshipped by his followers as if he were a god, and adds that they did not intend to worship the image of the emperor by offering incense and wine in libation as prescribed. We also read to you Christians "to be accustomed, on a given day, to gather before the dawn (*ante lucem convenire*) and to sing alternately a hymn to Christ as to a god, to oblige one another with an oath not for criminal purposes, but committing oneself not to commit robbery, adultery, not to lack faith, not to refuse, if requested, the return of a deposit".[38](about:blank) Pliny also mentions only Christ and not Jesus, in reference to the Christians who bear his name, qualifying the new sect *superstitio prava et immodica*. Pliny asked for instructions on the content of the punishments to be inflicted on Christians and the rules for their application. Trajan, confirming the absence of precise laws on the matter, prescribes to punish those who refuse to worship the emperor, but does not oblige them to systematically seek out Christians, nor to give credit to anonymous accusations.

*[Embedded Image]*
 *Epistulae*, X, No. 96 and 97

A third reference that most likely mentions the name of Christ is due to the Latin historian Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus (ca.70-150), in a text in his biography of the Emperor Claudius (41-54). The author reports that during the years of this empire clashes took place in Rome between Jews and Romans because of a certain Chresto (*Chresto impulsore*). Suetonius believes him to be present in Rome, which on the one hand would tend not to identify the character in question with Christ Jesus, but on the other is fully compatible with the way in which Christians habitually spoke of Jesus, believed, yesterday as now, to be present among them. Although there is no doubt about the authenticity of the document, there is a disparity of opinion about the real correspondence between the name *Chrestus* mentioned by Suetonius and Jesus of Nazareth.[39](about:blank) However, it should be noted that the *Chrestus/chrestianos* variant is also present in some editorial offices of *Tacitus*' *Annales*.

There is also a letter in Syriac language from a man called Mara bar Serapion, whose date is uncertain, but could reasonably be in the 2nd century.[40](about:blank) The author complains to his son about the situation resulting from the Roman domination. Mara expresses the hope that in the end divine justice will prevail over the oppressor, exhorting his son to always seek truth and wisdom. The letter proposes a parallel between Socrates, Pythagoras and Jesus, not unheard of for certain Christian literature of a sapiential environment. In reality Mara does not mention Jesus, but speaks of the "king of the Jews". Quoting the unjust end of these three noble characters, Mara expresses herself in these terms: "What advantage did the Athenians gain from having killed Socrates, for which they were rewarded with famine and plague? Or what advantage did the inhabitants of Samos derive from having burned Pythagoras, since all their land was covered with sand in an instant? Or what advantage did the Jews have from the slaying of their wise king, for from that time onward the kingdom was taken from them? The reference to the destruction of Jerusalem allows us to deduce that the "wise king" in question is Jesus, whose royal title may have been made known by the preaching of Christians or even by traditions linked to the *titulum crucis*; this association is reinforced by the continuation of the text, in which he is presented as the "author of new laws".[41](about:blank)
