Among the pagan witnesses, Luciano of Samosata (120 ca.-180 ca.) and Galen of Pergamum (130 ca.-199), who both refer, more or less indirectly, to Jesus Christ. Also of note is a testimony of Galen on the life of Christians.[42](about:blank) Certainly more numerous are those authors who inform about the presence and personality of the followers of Jesus, soon to be called "Christians", as reported by Luke (cf. *Acts* 11:26) and as the Roman Senate itself seems to do around the year 35.[43](about:blank) Among them, before the end of the second century, were Epictetus, Cassius Dione, Marcaurelius.

**Information from Judaism**

As far as the Jewish sources are concerned, a commentary on the Babylonian Talmud is known, which informs how, before an anniversary of the feast of Passover, Jesus of Nazareth was hung on the cross because by his magic he had seduced and led Israel astray.[44](about:blank)

Particularly debated is the reference to Jesus present in the *Jewish Antiquities*, written between 93 and 94 by the Jewish historian Josephus Flavius (37 ca.-100 ca.). The work mentions facts in relation to Christianity, speaks of the movement that arose around John the Baptist and the capital condemnation of James, a Christian from Jerusalem, known as the brother of Jesus called Christ.[45](about:blank) In a more extensive passage of this same work, called *Testimonium Flavianum, we* mention the title of Messiah attributed to Jesus, the miracles he performed and his apparitions as a resurrected man after his death on the cross.[46](about:blank) Because of the implicit Christian faith that it seems to profess, however, historical-literary criticism has suggested that it contains some interpolations. However, there are recent studies that reduce the extent of possible corruption of the text, due to findings of other versions of this same passage.[47](about:blank)

The information on Jesus reported in Justin Martyr’s *Dialogue with* *Tripho*, where a judgment circulating in that environment about the resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth, which coincides with the one collected from the Gospel of Matthew (cf. *Mt* 28:11-15): "You have chosen men and sent them all over the earth to proclaim that an ungodly and unjust heresy had arisen because of the error of a certain Jesus, a Galilean, and saying that they had crucified him but his disciples had taken him away at night from the tomb where he had been laid when he had been nailed from the cross, and now they were deceiving men by claiming that he had awakened from the dead and ascended to heaven.[48](about:blank) The same accusation is reported by Tertullian, who refuted it in his apologia addressed to the Latin environment.[49](about:blank)

**Summary remarks**

Although scarce, the proposed testimonies reasonably lead to some conclusions.

1. Excluded are those interpretations of the character Jesus - more frequent in the past but not absent even in our days - that have denied or deny his real historical existence in Palestine at the time of Tiberius Caesar's empire. History is not competent to confirm or deny whether Jesus, as he proclaimed himself, is really the Son of God, but it testifies that faith in him was preached by his followers in a public way and in years sensibly close to the *events of Jerusalem, that is,* the crucifixion to death of Jesus and the beginning of the expansion of a religious message announcing him risen from the dead.

1. Historical analysis shows the existence of a very close link between Jesus Christ and the religious movement that was linked to him, the *Christians*. This link did not express abstract or theoretical adherence to a doctrine received from the preachers of a gnosis, but was established on the basis of facts preached by witnesses. The link between Christians and Jesus of Nazareth did not arise from the later rediscovery of a previous spiritual message that was now being reworked, but presents itself as something historically original. It also implied a willingness to suffer martyrdom, a behavior that could only result from a reasonable continuity between the witnesses of the events in Jerusalem and those who committed themselves to live as Christians, spreading the Gospel.

1. While the extra-Biblical sources do not offer us elements to outline a portrait of the "historical Jesus", they do offer us a well-founded access to the "historicity" of the subject in question and therefore also suggest information on the historicity of his teachings and his messianic self-proclamation, because reflected in the behavior of his followers.

Ultimately, what Christians say and what they do, how they behave and what they endure, can only be understood on the basis of a historical link with Jesus, through the mediation of witnesses who have been close to him.

**5.5.2** **The historicity of the Gospels and the historicity of the *Gospel***

Once the historicity of the subject of the apostolic kerygma has been established, Fundamental Theology must clarify how to access the teachings and personality of Jesus, which is only possible by examining the Gospels and other documents written by Christians and received by the Church. However, access to the history of Jesus and his teachings through the Gospels would be impossible without giving importance to the role of *tradition,* which *gives* us, through its experience, the meanings without which the words and teachings of Jesus would remain lifeless words. By his followers, the Christians, Jesus of Nazareth was loved, remembered, told, preached. The gospels and other writings of the NT are the final witnesses, collected and prepared by the believing community, of this love, this memory, these stories and this preaching.

**Historicity, authenticity, inspiration**

Like biblical exegesis, fundamental theology is interested in identifying criteria of authenticity and historicity to trace back to the original core of Jesus' message. The Christian faith is born from the response to a word that is believed to be truly resonated in history, heard and preached by witnesses who, by accepting it, have changed their lives. The *historicity of the word* thus understood, however, does not coincide, although it certainly intertwines, with the demands of historicity, authenticity and antiquity proper to exegetical work. The *historicity of a teaching of* Jesus of Nazareth is compatible with the fact that this teaching comes to us through the narration of events that may not have had a "historical record" in the actual course of events, or through the testimony of a believing community that has chosen the words and images considered most appropriate in this regard. Thus the *historical reality* of the contents preached by Jesus about the Kingdom of God can also reach us through a literary genre that is not necessarily historical or through a biblical sense that is not necessarily literal. True and historical teachings of Jesus, never collected literally from the words of the *four gospels,* and therefore for exegesis never belonged to the historical Jesus, can come to us through the *Gospel* of the words and behavior of the believing community, also based, in the final analysis, on what has been learned from the canonical writings.

Something like that could also be said about the relationship between historicity and antiquity. The antiquity of the canonical gospels certainly speaks in favor of their historicity, but a saying of Jesus transmitted from the oldest layer of the hypothetical source Q is not only for this reason more historic than a saying mentioned several decades later by the editor of the Fourth Gospel. The adjective "authentic", often used in the discussion of the historicity of documents, refers both to the concordance between the declared author and the real author, and to the correspondence between the period in which the writing places its author and the period in which it was actually written. Authentic also opposes "counterfeit". The latter term indicates a text that intentionally wants to imitate a historical context older than that of its actual composition, or the corruption of a text that no longer conforms to that originally intended and produced by its author. Where there is no willingness to deceive, the practice of pseudoepigraphy (using the name of another author, different from that of the writer), not extraneous even to canonical books, or the uncertainty in attributing a specific writing to a specific author, do not oppose the truth of the narrated facts or the transmitted teachings. For Fundamental Theology **the "authenticity" of a writing refers first of all to the request that its author, or its authors, be considered by the first Christian communities as credible witnesses of the Gospel that is transmitted there**, even if from an exegetical point of view that writing could be judged not authentic for reasons of pseudoepigraphy or uncertainty in the authorship to be attributed to the writing.

Even the notion of "inspiration" is not directly translatable in terms of the "historicity" of a certain narrative; the historicity of a text does not derive from its property of being inspired. An allegorical-literary reconstruction of the Gospel of Luke would be as inspired, in terms of Catholic doctrine, as the description of the death of Jesus on the cross, although the characters and places of the Lucanian reconstruction could be the result of literary composition; in this case too, the content or teaching which, through that reconstruction, the evangelist intends to communicate to his readers remains historical, that is, *historically linked to the original and original message of Jesus.* Fundamental theology, in essence, is interested in the historicity *of the Gospel*, which, as the Council of Trent expressed, "after having been previously promised by the Prophets in the sacred Scriptures, first made known through the mouth of Our Lord Jesus Christ, Son of God, and then by his apostles, was entrusted to him with the mission of proclaiming it to every creature as the source of every salutary truth and every rule of morals" (cf. DH 1501).

The criticism of the historicity of the Gospels, especially in the forms developed from the Enlightenment and rationalism - but not entirely absent even today - is basically a criticism of their *truth*. On this ground, fundamental theology must argue in its historical-propedeutical moment, showing that the knowledge through which we enter into relation with the word of Jesus Christ, so that it may resonate in *our* history, is not fragile, it is not a naive discourse or an incongruous presentation. **In essence, reasons must be offered which conclude that believing the Jesus known through the Gospels, believing His word and committing oneself to it, cannot be qualified as *unreasonable* behaviour.** The Gospels claim to communicate events that have truly intersected human history, and as such must be able to be recognized on some level, though pregnant with the mystery that they carry. What the editors of the Gospels intended to transmit is the testimony of the *true story of* Jesus of Nazareth: "He who has seen bears witness and his testimony is true (ἀληθινὴ αὐτοῦ ἐστιν ἡ μαρτυρία); he knows that he speaks the truth, because you also believe" (*Jn* 19:35). It is the value of this testimony that must be exposed and defended.

**The debate on the historicity of the Gospels**

Already in patristic times there was no lack of those who questioned the historicity of the Gospels, pointing out the presence of discrepancies in their narrations. The response to these criticisms developed two different strategies: to search for the composition of differences through the reconstruction of a unitary history, or to accept differences by explaining them on the basis of real differences, both in the personality of the hagiographers and, above all, in the recipients of the writing. Thus Tazian (2nd century), starting from the differences between one evangelist and another, wrote the *Diatessaron* around the year 150, bringing together the four gospels into one to defend their concord and to draw from them a coherent and univocal story of Jesus' life. Augustine (354-430) instead tried to give an answer to the problem of evangelical discords, with the work *De consensu evangelistarum* (400), affirming that it was not necessary to read the gospels to obtain the same words pronounced by Jesus (*ipsissima verba Christi*), but rather to seek the overall meaning that flows from his teachings.

Since modern times, criticism of the gospels' historicity can be traced back to three main currents:

- Enlightenment-rationalist thinking, responsible for promoting the idea that the Gospels were artificial and deceptive documents;
- the positions of "liberal theology" promoted by theologians of the Churches of the Reformation and those of Romanticist idealism, which supported the mythical-literary origin of most of the stories told, thus proposing new readings of the life of Jesus (*Leben Jesu Forschung*), of which an attempt was made to reconstruct with difficulty a story stripped of faith;
- the hermeneutical perspective that emphasized the "kerygmatic" genre, that is, of proclamation and support for the faith, and for this reason unintentionally historical, of the Gospels, also advocating a necessary "demythologization" of Scripture.

The first examples of rationalist reading of the gospels were those proposed, respectively, by the works of the English deist Thomas Chubb (1679-1747), *The true gospel proclaimed by Jesus (*1738), the German deist Hermann Samuel Reimarus (1694-1768), *The purpose of Jesus and that of his disciples,* published anonymously by G.E. Lessing (1729-1781) in 1778. For the first, Jesus would have been convinced of the imminent coming of the Kingdom of God and on this false expectation he would have staked his entire existence. For the latter, however, Jesus of Nazareth's project would have been different from that of his disciples: he would never have thought of founding a new religion, nor would he have performed miracles, nor announced his death and resurrection. In both cases his disciples, having invested all their political-religious ideal in Jesus of Nazareth, were disappointed in their expectations after the death of their Master. They therefore devised a fraud by stealing his corpse from the tomb, proclaiming his resurrection and thus his ascension to Heaven.

The second current (later called "First research on Jesus", *Leben-Jesu-Forschung*) can be traced back to David Friedrich Strauss (1808-1874), author of a *Life of Jesus* (1835) which inaugurated the interpretation of the gospels in a mythological key. Starting from some simple historical facts, the believing community would have covered the deeds of its founder with a supernatural superstructure. The supernatural narratives present in the Gospels would not be conscious and deliberate invention of the first disciples, as wanted by Reimarus, but collective and unconscious elaboration of the believing community developed over time, and therefore comparable in everything to popular legends. The historical fact is not, for Strauss, relevant: it does not matter so much to know what Jesus was in reality, historically, as to access his spiritual message. Authors such as F.C. Baur and E. can also be assimilated to this current. Renan; the latter composed an influential romantic *Life of Jesus* (1862).

The third current is traced back to the position of Rudolf Bultmann (1884-1976). Following the program of Adolf von Harnack (1851-1930), who in his work *The Essence of Christianity* (1900) supported the essentially "kerygmatic" character of the gospels, Bultmann stated that we have access only to *preaching about Jesus* by the Church, but not to the *history of Jesus*. The superstructures that this preaching brought with it involved subjecting the NT to a process of demythization, which he exposed first in his work *Jesus* (1926) and then in the *Manifesto of Demythization* (1941). The "mythological" content of the Gospels, which in different ways and at different levels had been supported by various authors of the First Research, had therefore to be removed. The end result of this work would not be to go back to a historical substratum, which Bultmann considered impossible, but quite the opposite, that is, to circumvent the problem of history. The exact reconstruction of this would no longer be necessary for a living and existential relationship with the spiritual message of Jesus of Nazareth. By demythologizing the texts we do not reach the story, but we can only filter the existential message. In accordance with the Lutheran perspective, for Bultmann it is not we who must question the Word of God, but it is the Word of God that questions us, with an effectiveness that overcomes the problematic nature of its exact historical reconstruction, which is in fact now impracticable for us.

From 1953, Bultmann's position was criticized by his disciples, Ernst Käsemann in particular. Käsemann tried to recompose the separation between the Christ of faith and the Jesus of history, but he recognized the existence of an irreducible and positive tension between them: although the gospels were written in the light of faith in the Risen Christ, the Easter events and what followed them could not be understood without real historical roots. This, according to this author, encouraged a "Second Search" or a "New Search" on Jesus. For Käsemann too, historical research alone is insufficient to make us know who Jesus of Nazareth really was, but unlike his teacher, historical research is relevant to the faith itself, because **only by holding on to the history of Jesus can we affirm that salvation comes *from this history****.* With Käsemann, therefore, exegetical criticism can continue with three points: **a) if there were no connection between the Christ of faith and the Jesus of history, Christianity would become a myth;** **b) if the early Church had had no interest in the "history of Jesus", it would not be explained why the Gospels were written; c) for our faith to be the same as that transmitted by the Apostles, the certainty of the identity between the earthly Jesus and the glorified Christ is required**.[50](about:blank)

What was the position of Catholic Apologetics, between the 19th and 20th centuries, in the face of such criticism? To defend the historicity of the Gospels, at least until the first half of the twentieth century, we tried to respond by developing three arguments:

1. to show the "integrity" of the text, that is, the faithful correspondence between the text that has come down to us and the original one;

1. to defend "literary authenticity", i.e. the well-informed character of the original editors of the Gospels;

1. support the "historical truthfulness", showing that the evangelists could not only tell the truth, but also in fact said it, and with all sincerity.

With the development of historical-literary criticism, especially the methods of internal criticism of texts, this approach, based essentially on external criticism, unfortunately proved insufficient. There were, in fact, objective limits to an exact reconstruction of the first written texts, but the presence in the Gospels of literary forms that differed from a simple historical account of events should also be recorded.

As a result of the debate established, especially in relation to the exegesis of the reformed theologians, Catholic theology has matured the need to **accompany the study of "external criticism", through which one seeks a verification of the reliability of the texts by examining the data relative to the author, time, place and circumstances, with that of "internal criticism", on the basis of which one can verify the historical value of the initial data, examining the intrinsic characteristics of the material reported**. While external criticism and literary analysis make it possible to go back to the "antiquity" of the text, internal criticism is called upon to weigh the historical reliability of the reported data, thus accessing the level of "authenticity" (mainly centered on the subject of the editorial staff), satisfied which can be accessed a judgement of "historicity" (which is more relevant to its content).

**Criteria for assessing the authenticity and historicity of Gospel narratives**

On the criteria for verifying the *authenticity* of the Gospels, and subsequently accessing their historicity, there is a certain consensus among the exegetes, although not always unanimity on their specific number and content. Here are some of the main ones:

1. The "comparative method of continuity/discontinuity": the narratives must be sufficiently similar (plausible) to what one would expect from the context and circumstances (historical, geographical, cultural, etc.), but at the same time sufficiently dissimilar to show an originality capable of bringing out the narratives in the background represented by the possible simple re-proposals of narratives already known or easily composable, both in the Jewish sphere and in the circle of the primitive believing community.

1. The "multiple source attestation". The presence, in a number of independent sources, of Jesus' teachings and episodes concerning him, is more in favor of their historical authenticity than content that boasts few witnesses or only one. The criterion points first of all to the *antiquity* of the material, because it is recognized as present in a deep tradition, and secondly to the deduction of its *historicity*. The criterion is used with reference not only to the multiplicity of *sources*, but also to the variety of *forms*. It is not only the common testimony of sources considered independent (e.g. the source Q, the source of Mc, the Lc and Mt's own parts, the source of Gv, the Pauline corpus, etc.),[51](about:blank) but even if the same content has come to us through different literary or editorial forms, that is, through parables, called isolates, teachings linked to the narration of some miracle, disputes with exponents of the Jewish religious milieu, etc., the same content can be found in the same way.

1. The analysis of the person of Jesus, using a criterion also called the "style of Jesus", evaluates both linguistic aspects (philological-literary analysis of his sayings) and psychological aspects (personality, coherence and sincerity of life). The analysis of Jesus' human psychology has two advantages. On the one hand, it guides our access to the historicity of the narratives by relying on the historical *truth of* the character, as this is revealed by the consistency/unity of his way of behaving and his life style; on the other hand, it offers us the possibility to explore the reliability of the witness, directing us to the credibility of his person, and therefore to the conformity between the truth of his words and works and the truth known to the subject.

1. The analysis of the credibility of the witnesses at the origin of the texts, i.e. the believing community. In this case the catechetical, liturgical and vital dynamics of the first Christian communities are studied, trying to understand both the personality of witnesses and the relationship between oral traditions and the preparation of written texts.

The first two criteria have a typically exegetical character, just as, in the third criterion concerning the style of Jesus, the one concerning the philological-literary aspect of the statements reported. The aspect inherent in the personality of Jesus and his psychology, together with the fourth criterion, which concerns the historical-testimonial role of the believing community, have instead a more specifically theological-fundamental character. Let us briefly go into the meaning of some of the previous criteria.

The relationship between continuity and discontinuity "establishes a comparison between Jesus and the Judaic and/or Christian world, noting together the harmony (continuity) and the contrast (discontinuity) between the two terms of the comparison. Thus, on the one hand, we come to grasp the adherence of Jesus to the world around him and, on the other, the decisive detachment from it: conformity with the environment is the background to this unique and highly original personality. The relationship of continuity-discontinuity runs therefore on the thread of "distinction-complementarity": if discontinuity allows us to grasp and experience the uniqueness of the phenomenon of Jesus, it is with continuity that we can place that phenomenon in his world and in his time".[52](about:blank)

The criterion based on the analysis of the credibility of witnesses develops three important characteristics possessed by the members of the believing community, considered in a broad sense as the author of the Gospel writings, and in any case as the community to which the individual hagiographers belonged. These are authors, as historical analysis itself can show in an objective way, who were at the same time ***disciples*, *apostles* and *witnesses*** (i.e., more precisely, martyrs). As disciples, it is reasonable to consider them interested in faithfully recording the teachings of a Master whom they admired and venerated, reflecting continuously on them; as apostles, they were invested with the mission of proclaiming precisely those teachings, to which a clear saving value was associated from the beginning, which increased their commitment to preach them, orally and in writing, truthfully. As martyrs, they showed with their lives that they considered true and worthy of faith the content of a message for which they were willing to die.[53](about:blank)

**The position of the *Dei Verbum* and the Pontifical Biblical Commission**

On the theme of historical access to Jesus, the subject and foundation of the credibility of Revelation, the declarations of the Magisterium of the Church and some related bodies should also be recalled. The Council Constitution *Dei Verbum* (1965) presents a concise but significant summary of what it calls, with patristic expression, the *fourfold Gospel*: "The Church believes and has always believed in every place that the four gospels are of apostolic origin" (DV, 18); the four gospels, of which "[she] affirms without hesitation the historicity, faithfully transmit what Jesus the Son of God, during his life among men, actually worked and taught for their salvation" (DV, 19); the hagiographers, then, "drawing both from their own memories and the testimony of those who "from the beginning were eyewitnesses and ministers of the word", wrote with the intention of making "the truth" known to us (cf. *Lk* 1:2-4) of the teachings on which we have been instructed" (DV, 19).

With regard to the formation of the written texts, it is finally affirmed that they were composed "by choosing some things from the many handed down by word of mouth or already in writing, drawing up a summary of the others or explaining them with regard to the situation of the churches, finally preserving the character of preaching, but always in such a way as to report true and sincere things about Jesus". (DV, 19). In these conciliar texts, two poles to be held constructively together should be noted: on the one hand, the full recognition of actions such as: *choosing*, *synthesizing*, *explaining according to the recipient*, *preaching*; on the other, the conviction about the *apostolic origin of* the gospels and about the willingness of their editors to report through them *true and sincere things about Jesus*. If what is said about the first pole leaves the the theologian the freedom to reconstruct specific paths that the proclamation, memory, tradition or testimony may have followed, what is said about the second pole is intended to set a firm point about the origin and purpose of the evangelical writings.

While in the Council Chamber the outline of the *Dei Verbum was* being discussed, the Pontifical Biblical Commission published the document *Instruction on the historical truth of the Gospels* (1964), expressly dedicated to this theme.[54](about:blank) While recognizing the usefulness of historical-critical methods, in particular the literary criticism method known as the "history of forms" (*Formengeschichte*), there is a significant call for prudence in its use, because of the philosophical-theological preconceptions and assumptions that often accompany its use.[55](about:blank) The judgment will be confirmed in substance, but mitigated in expression, in the following document *The Interpretation of the Bible in the Church* (1993), where it is made clear that historical-critical methods, as scientific methods, are not in question: instead, their use is one that does not take into account the particularity and nature of the text, sacred Scripture, to which they are applied, for example by underestimating the role of a spiritual tradition and context in which the text was written and within which it is to be read and interpreted.

*[Embedded Image]*
 PCB, On how to understand the historicity of the gospels

**Summary view**

In the light of the above, we can provide some answers to questions about the historicity of the Gospel narratives:

1. There are historical testimonies outside the gospels about the presence of a rapidly expanding spiritual movement, created as a result of events that took place in Jerusalem, of which Jesus of Nazareth was the protagonist. In fact, we are dealing with a person who historically existed and a religious message historically preached that has its author in him.
2. In contrast to the hypothesis of a "mythological superstructure" that would have been associated with the figure of Jesus of Nazareth, there are some important factors: the rapidity with which both the traditions of Jesus' oral sayings and the first written testimonies about him were formed; the rapidity with which the apostles and those who preached the Christian kerygma displayed a precise theological understanding of the mission of Jesus Christ (universal redeemer, mediator of cosmic significance, Son of God), manifesting an unusual originality with regard to the Jewish religious culture from which this message had arisen.

1. There are appropriate criteria, based on the internal criticism of the evangelical sources, which make it possible to qualify as reasonable a judgment of historicity/truthfulness for the substance of the teachings and works attributed to Jesus of Nazareth, although there are not sufficient elements for a complete and timely reconstruction of them.

1. With regard to the historicity of the Gospels, this should not be considered as a request to be confronted with a chronicle or a chronology of Jesus' life, nor is there an obligation to consider all the episodes narrated there as all events that really happened. The purpose of the Gospels, however, remains always the preaching of historical facts, which are considered by the evangelists as the first content to be transmitted, even if sometimes their narratives can be enriched by various literary forms.

1. Finally, it is possible to establish a historical access to Jesus of Nazareth and his teachings by reflecting on the positive role played by the believing community. The latter does not act as a barrier to such access, but as a live subject of the transmission. It can reasonably put us in contact with a *Jesus remembered and witnessed*. The "substantial" historical truth of the Gospels is based on the credibility of the subjects of the tradition, who were simultaneously ***disciples*, *apostles* and *martyrs*: the authors at the origin of the oral or written traditions about Jesus therefore deserve a pre-judgment of trust, as does the intentional purpose that guided the formulation and diffusion of those same traditions, namely to transmit *the truth about Jesus*.**

##### 5.5.3 The Psychology of Jesus
Employing a **Christocentric-personalist perspective** in the study of the credibility of Revelation also implies the need to take into account the human psychology of Jesus of Nazareth, as it emerges from the NT narratives. This is also necessary to examine the third (c) of the **criteria of authenticity and historicity of the gospels** set forth in the previous section. In order to carry out such an investigation, theology clearly needs the **help of the human sciences**, but this does not mean that it renounces its epistemological specificity. The person of Jesus Christ, in fact, is always considered according to the perspective of faith, also because the sources used remain those drawn up by a believing community. It is not a question of putting the divinity of Jesus Christ in parentheses, nor of trying to derive it directly from the psychological investigation of his humanity, but rather of **examining whether Jesus of Nazareth presents himself as a person worthy of faith, credible, or whether the sources suggest basing the credibility of his teaching on other factors**. The Fundamental Theology developed since the 1930s by theologians such as Karl Adam and Romano Guardini shows that this path can be followed with profit.56

##### The psycho-physical state, the look, the good mood, the firm will
First of all, it should be noted that the Gospel narratives offer sufficient cues to believe that Jesus enjoyed excellent physical health. He makes frequent journeys through Galilee and a number of marches between Galilee and Judea; he loves life in the open air and staying in natural places of certain scenic value (high in the hills, gardens, viewpoints of the whole city of Jerusalem; cf. *Mk* 1:35; 6:32; *Lk* 6:12-13; *Jn* 4:35), in many of which he gathers to pray. He shows good physical endurance and the habit of leading the day at a rather intense pace (cf. *Mt* 8:20; *Mk* 3:20; 6:31). What emerges is the figure of a healthy person, whose teaching and training of a restricted discipleship in the rabbis' manner has not made us forget at all the contact with the crowds and ordinary life, work in the fields or the economic administration of crops, human relations of kinship, the customs of good neighborliness or domestic work, as witnessed by the subjects chosen for the parables used to train the crowds and the richness of the details that appear in their narrative. The only elements in contrast are the two comments that judge him "out of himself" provided respectively by Mark and John, the first addressed to him by his family and the second by the Pharisees (cf. *Mk* 3:20-21; *Jn* 10:20). The two contexts are, however, rather specific: in the first case, a concern of his relatives for the intensity of his rhythm of life, which led him to have no time to take food; in the second, the judgment is expressed in the context of an explicit confession of filial witness to God-the-Father, evaluated as blasphemy.

An element of interest in approaching a person's psychology is **the character of their** ***gaze***. In referring to the encounters between Jesus and his interlocutors, the evangelists (Mark in particular) frequently use expressions such as "staring into the gaze", "staring around", "looking around", etc., wanting with this to **emphasize a careful way of observing and looking at those who are in front of him or who accompany him, beyond simply seeing or crossing their eyes** (cf. *Mt* 19:26; *Mk* 3:5; 3:34; 5:32; 8:33; 10:21.23; 14:67; *Lk* 6:10; 19:5; *Jn* 1:42). In particular, the gaze of Jesus, certainly not detached from his whole person**, shows a certain attraction, as witnessed by the episodes of vocation to discipleship.** Equally significant is Jesus' gaze that moves Peter to conversion, even though it is directed in a context - that of the long sleepless night and the physical and psychological wounds - that inaugurates the first hours of the Passion (cf. *Lk* 22:61). **The intensity and strength of Jesus' gaze lay in favour of a firm will, an underlying sincerity and an uprightness of mind**. It is not possible to sustain the gaze on someone with such intensity if one is not certain of the truth, present in one's own conscience, that through that gaze one wishes to communicate.

In Jesus' human psychology one can recognize the **presence of good humor and a possible tendency to irony**. Thus some episodes seem to manifest it, if *read in* this perspective. He uses popular proverbs that reveal a certain ironic content when applied to the specific context in which he uses them (cf. *Lk* 5:39; 7:31-35; 17:37). Some exchanges of jokes between Jesus and Peter, and the facts that follow them, are equally compatible with a certain ironic sense: from the episode of the night encounter on the waters of Lake Tiberias, to that of the tribute to the Temple, or the washing of feet at the Last Supper (cf. *Mt* 14:28-31; 17:24-27; *Jn* 13:8-10). In the way he relates to his interlocutors, on some occasions Jesus uses figures and examples that could intentionally make them smile, such as when he asks them how the kingdom of Beelzebubble could exist if the demons fight among themselves (cf. *Mk* 3:22-26), or when he directs new questions to those who question him instead of answering what is asked of him (cf. *Mt* 21:24-27; *Lk 20:*3), or when he finally asks himself witty questions (cf. *Lk* 20:41). A reinterpretation in an ironic sense is even possible in the comments that follow the episode of the encounter with the rich young man and his failure to follow Jesus, at least in the editorial staff that makes him Mark (cf. *Mk* 10:17-27). To the disciples amazed by the hyperbole employed by the Master, to teach them that it is easier for a camel to enter the eye of a needle than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of God, Jesus answers that this curious eventuality is certainly possible for God, making them regain their trust. But it is actually the game of glances reported by Mark in this episode that directs an ironic reading in this sense: **the hyperbole is pronounced "looking around" (v. 23), but the unexpected solution that making a camel pass through the eye of a needle is possible for God is suggested "looking his disciples in the face" (v. 27), as when one wants to capture someone's attention, arousing in him a certain expectation before communicating something decisive**. He also speaks in favour of an open and **jovial character, or in any case capable of joking**, what the three synoptics narrate about the presence of children around Jesus and their trust in letting themselves be blessed and caressed by him (cf. *Mt* 19:13-15; *Mk* 10:13-16; *Lk* 18:15-17; cf. also *Mt* 18:2-5), **since it is usually the amusing and expansive/warm people who attract the attention of the little ones and are able to entertain themselves with them.**

Jesus of Nazareth manifests a very resolute will and the precise awareness of having to carry out a mission. In particular, some expressions show, by the strength of his tone and the radicality of his content, that his is a clear and determined conviction, both in his personal conduct (cf. *Mt* 10:34-36; 16:22-23; *Mk* 10:45; *Lk* 12:49; cf. also *Lk* 2:49), and in his requests to those who wish to follow him (cf. *Mt 10:*37-39; 18:8-9; *Mk* 10:21; *Lk* 9:60-62; 14:26-27; 16:13). His judgments are not tender and, when he points the way to the good, he does not spare invective, in a style reminiscent of the language of the prophets (cf. *Mt* 23:13-39; *Lk* 11:39-52; 13:32). With an equally resolute will he handles situations which, in other people, could easily have generated feelings of hesitation or even discouragement (cf. *Mk* 12:13-17; *Jn* 8:3-11; *Jn* 6:54-67). Particularly explicit, in this regard, were his reactions during the episode of the expulsion of the merchants from the Temple, which marked a discontinuity with a tacitly approved practice for which no one felt any more scandal (cf. *Jn* 2:14-19). **In the psychology of Jesus of Nazareth there exists the clear conviction of being someone who came with the explicit mission of manifesting the truth: "This is why I was born and why I came into the world: to bear witness to the truth. Whoever is of the truth, hear my voice"** (*Jn* 18:37).

Jesus of Nazareth **has a personality that attracts and imposes itself**. Those who frequent him adm**ire him, but also fear him, a fear that could reasonably be described as reverential** (cf. *Mt* 9:7; 17:6; *Mk* 1:27; 5:35; *Lk* 7:16; *Jn* 3:2). The attraction and admiration aroused are **the fruit of an authority that seems reasonably linked to the witness of life with which he accompanies his teaching**. A paradigmatic example of this can be found in the *Sermon on the Mount*, as narrated by Matthew's writing and the effects it has on his listeners.

*[Embedded Image]*
 K. Adam, A brief analysis of Jesus' psychology

As the Gospels agreed, He must have been a man accustomed to fatigue, resilient, healthy, robust. And already for this reason He distinguished himself from other famous founders of religions. Muhammad was a sick man with hereditary affections, shaken in the nervous system, when I raised the prophet's flag. Buddha was inwardly undone, unnerved, tired of life, when he withdrew from the world. There is no clue that Jesus was touched by any illness. All the sufferings that struck him were caused by his office: they were hardships and sacrifices imposed on him by his messianic mission.

His body must have been extraordinarily hardened and resistant to fatigue. This was already proved by his habit of going out in good time to his work. "In the morning he got up very early and went to a deserted place to pray" (Mk 1:35). "At dawn he called his disciples around him and chose twelve of them" (Lk 6:13). The same sense of freshness and healthy vigour flows from his love for nature. In a special way he loved the mountains and the lake. After a hard day's work he would gladly climbed some isolated heights.

[...] Moreover, this life of wanderings was full of hard work and hard work. Mark frequently points out: "They did not even have time to eat" (cf. Mk 3:20; 6:31). Until late at night the sick came and went (cf. Mk 3:8). And evil adversaries, Pharisees and Sadducees, came and went with the sick. Then it was beatings and answers, word against word, spirit against spirit. Nerve-wracking discussions, struggles and dangerous dangers arose.

Added the exhausting explanations to the disciples, made heavy by their misunderstanding and pride. **Any sick, or barely delicate, temperament would have to give in or succumb. Never and nowhere did Jesus withdraw, even in the most grueling, most dangerous situations. He sleeps peacefully lying on his pillow, in the midst of the storm that upsets the lake of Genezareth; when the disciples woke him up, as soon as he woke from his deep sleep he immediately found himself and dominated the situation. All this shows how far he was from having an excitable, nervous temperament; instead, he was always master of his senses: in short, he was perfectly healthy.**

K. Adam, Jesus the Christ (1938), Morcelliana, Brescia 1995, 83-85.

##### The affections and feelings of Jesus
The 4 Gospels use 21 different verbs to indicate Jesus' feelings. **Most of Jesus' feelings reported by the synoptics highlight his compassion and suffering: compassion for man, for his material and spiritual needs; suffering in the face of evil, in its various forms: hypocrisy, obstinacy, unbelief, ignorance**. **In John, the whole emotional-sentimental life of Jesus revolves around terms that indicate love, friendship, loving, indicating the relationship of love between the Father and the Son as the nucleus from which they emanate.** The authors/editors of the Gospels agree that **the strongest emotional feelings such as love, but also indignation, fear and anguish, are manifested primarily to the twelve who accompany him and to those closest to him.**

In the synoptics the recurrence of the feeling of ***compassion* ("to have or to feel compassion", gr. σπλαγχνίζομαι) is very significant, which places Jesus in a relationship of empathy with his interlocutors, showing a real and sincere interest in them** (cf. *Mt* 9,36; 14,14; 15,32; 20,34; *Mk* 1,41; 6,34; 8,2; 9,22; *Lk* 7,13). This is what Jesus feels for the crowds that follow him, hungry for bread but also for doctrine, for the lepers he meets, for Nain's widow because of the death of her only son, for the two blind people who go to meet him, for the epileptic boy. The authors/editors report this feeling both by talking about his healings and his teachings. An effective biographical summary is offered by the Gospel of Matthew: **"Jesus went through all the towns and villages, teaching in their synagogues, proclaiming the Gospel of the Kingdom and healing every illness and infirmity. Seeing the crowds, he felt compassion for them, because they were tired and exhausted like sheep who had no shepherd" (*Mt* 9:35-36).** The same verb is used by Jesus in the narration of various parables: to indicate the exemplary attitude of the Good Samaritan, whose mercy he praises towards the beaten and wounded man (cf. *Lk* 10:33); to describe the compassionate love of the father who mercifully welcomes the prodigal son (cf. *Lk* 15:20); to show the indulgence of the master with one of his debtors, who will later prove to be ruthless towards one of his servants (cf. *Mt* 18:27). Again within the picture provided by the synoptics, but with some connection also to episodes reported by John, Jesus' compassion manifests itself and is also related to other feelings: thus the insensitivity of the Pharisees in the face of the sick who need care even on the Sabbath, arouses in Jesus anger and sadness (cf. *Mk* 3:5); the presence of the merchants in the temple strongly outrages him because of his zeal and his love for the house of the Father, provoking an angry reaction, which appears immediately dominated, at least in the three synoptics, judging by the subsequent immediate dedication to healing and teaching (cf. *Mt* 21:12-15; *Mk* 11:15-17; *Lk* 19:45-47; *Jn* 2:13-17); Jesus is still outraged at the way his disciples did, who do not want to let the children - probably brought by their parents (cf. *Mt* 19:13; *Lk* 18:15) - approach him, so that he can caress and bless them (cf. *Mk* 10:10-13).

**Jesus' compassion is also manifested in his *crying***. Luke reports this on the occasion of the Master's outburst for the non-conversion of Jerusalem, in anticipation of its imminent destruction (cf. *Lk* 19:41-44; cf. also *Mt* 23:37-39 and *Lk* 13:34-35). On the occasion of the death of his friend Lazarus, "Jesus burst into tears" (*Jn* 11:35), a cry broken by hiccups, without moaning, unlike Mary's weeping at her brother's tomb (cf. *Jn* 11:33). In reality, the whole episode of Lazarus' death and resurrection is pervaded by intense empathy. Jesus twice expresses a feeling similar to a "tremor" (cf. *Jn* 11:33 and 11:38) and manifests a profound disturbance at the sight of the sorrow of Mary his sister and that of the Jews (cf. *Jn* 11:33). Jesus weeps because he loves Lazarus and because he is emotionally involved in the climate of sorrow and the tears with which the sisters and the Jews mourn the premature death of their spouse.

John insists on Jesus' feelings of love. **Compassion is a fruit of love and only the one who loves can share in the pain and joys of others**. The evangelist explores with greater intensity the deep core from which Jesus' attitude in favour of mankind seems to spring: the mutual love of the Father and the Son. Of the 29 times in which John speaks of Jesus' feelings, 18 times these are feelings of love, concentrated above all in the so-called farewell speeches. It is love towards his disciples, towards those who welcome him and keep his word, it is his love towards the Father, it is the love of those who give their lives for his friends (cf. *Jn* 13:1; 13:23; 13:34; 14:21; 14:31; 15:9-10.12), it is love towards "the disciple he loved" (cf. *Jn* 19:26; 20:2; 21:7; 21:20). It is love of self-giving which has "joy" as its fruit (cf. *Jn* 15:11; 17:23), born of obedience which goes as far as sacrifice. In John, Jesus reveals himself as the personification of the Father's love, and it is the profound love which binds the Father to the Son to "expand" towards his disciples and all men. **This love is manifested in the fulfilment of the Father's will, in witnessing to his word and his love for the world. This love determines in Jesus the firm will to serve (cf. *Jn* 13:13-14; cf. also *Mt* 20:28; *Mk* 10:45), to sacrifice himself (cf. *Jn* 12:23-24), to die (cf. *Jn* 12:32-33), sharing human life to the point of bringing it, in the labour of the Cross, upon himself.**

**On the theme of feelings and affections, although with a different vocabulary, the Synoptic Gospels and John agree, because compassion, mercy and love cannot remain separate, nor manifest themselves independently.**

##### Jesus of Nazareth in the face of pain and death
In the analysis of the credibility of a witness, the encounter with death represents a fundamental junction, because **it reveals the personal adherence to the subjective truth that he confessed and followed**. It is in the encounter with death that a witness **confesses an accomplished meaning to his existence.** The encounter with death characterizes the historical story of Jesus of Nazareth with a wealth of details far greater than any other narrative context of the Gospels: ancient oral and written sources on the passion and death of Jesus, from which the authors have drawn extensively.

The way Jesus of Nazareth deals with suffering, both physical and moral, shows a singular character profile. In addition to his remarkable physical endurance, due to his good health and his age, **he shows the ability to remain a master of himself, he maintains a good intellectual lucidity and a great emotional control, aspects of a psychologically strong and balanced personality**. **In the psycho-physical test to which he is subjected, Jesus manifests the reactions of a true man when subjected to a situation of intense *pathos*. Human weakness and passivity coexist with a fortitude and lucidity revealed by the security with which he decides what must be done and recognizes all its implications** (cf. *Mt* 26:45.52-54.64; *Mk* 14:41-42.49.62; *Lk* 22:53; *Jn* 18:5.11.36-38; 19:11).

**The logic that sustained Jesus' behavior during his public life is also present at the moment of his passion and death**. By his way of confronting events, Jesus overcomes them and masters them, not by fighting them or seeking through them his way out, but by leading them back to a deeper sense that he shows he knows and possesses. This basic psychological mastery ensures that the *agony* of Gethsemane, even in the realism of a truly experienced suffering and struggle (cf. *Lk* 22:44), should not be interpreted as a psychological collapse, nor as a religious crisis generated by fear of the future. Rather, the event presents itself as the manifestation of an awareness of the profound implications that the hour, now arrived, brings with it.

A central aspect of understanding the psychology and credibility of Jesus of Nazareth is that **the suffering experienced by him is not an expression of aversion, bitterness or condemnation *against* anyone, be they Jews, weak and traitorous disciples, persecutors or events, but rather a demonstration of the intensity of that hour which demands to be lived to the full.** In these hours Luke and John still place teachings of the Master aimed at conversion and forgiveness, mercy and piety (cf. *Lk* 23:28-31.34.43; *Jn* 19:26-28). Everyone agrees on one thing: Jesus of Nazareth does not die protesting or cursing, he does not resist persecutors or condemn them, but he fully identifies himself with what is happening and what he had announced several times in his public life.

From the top of the cross, Matthew and Mark depict him still able to invoke the name of God aloud with a supreme lament, "My God, my God, why has He forsaken me?". (*Mt* 27:46; *Mk* 15:34), and then he emits a loud cry as he breathes. Luke and John prefer to express this last cry in an exclamation that has the taste of a prayer, "Father, into your hands I deliver my spirit" (*Lk* 23:46), and "All is accomplished" (*Jn* 19:30). The feeling of abandonment experienced by Jesus from the gallows, whose meaning can certainly be the subject of lively exegetical debate, does not alter the picture of the sincerity of Jesus' feelings in facing his passion and death, but rather enriches it.57

##### The witness Jesus of Nazareth
All the above elements confirm **the firmness of will and determination in a balanced and non-violent subject.** The way Jesus faces death is in accordance with the maturity and self-control that had already characterized his life and feelings. **The witness Jesus of Nazareth, therefore, dies as he lived, with consistency of life and with feelings of compassion**. He dies, in particular**, *keeping what he promised,* that is, "to give his body and shed his blood for his own and for all". (cf. *Mt* 26,26-28; *Mk* 14,22-24; *Lk* 22,19-20) and "lay down your life for your friends" (cf. *Jn* 15,13), in accordance with what would be required of a credible witness.**

In summary, from an approach to the human psychology of Jesus emerges the picture of **a psychologically healthy, humanly mature person. Jesus of Nazareth is neither an ecstatic nor a dreamer, he is neither violent nor an angry. He is certainly determined and resolute in pursuing the mission of which he feels himself protagonist, but his attitudes and his speeches have nothing of the possessed, nor do they recall the typical manifestations of a maniac**.

**The point at issue, and the fact that it really demands an explanation, is that this balance and maturity coexist with an explicit statement about his own identity: Jesus self-proclaims himself Son of the eternal Father.** This person, psychologically sound, declares himself to be the Son of God equal to the Father, sent by the Father, who came from God's womb into the world, whose existence he himself claims to precede that of Abraham and precede the very creation of the world. **Although extraordinary and shocking in their content, it must be recognized that these unheard-of statements find in the psychology of Jesus of Nazareth a reason for credibility that imposes on reason attention and interest in this man and his life.**

##### 5.5.4 The *signs* of Jesus: the call to believe motivated by works and miracles
In continuity with what is stated in the Old Testament, also for the New, miracles are above all a "sign" of God and a sign that refers to God, even if presented as extraordinary and prodigious works. With these works Jesus manifests the living and merciful presence of God on behalf of mankind, especially the suffering; on these works rests the public revelation of his identity as Messiah; with them he intends to demonstrate his authority as the Son whom the Father has sent into the world. After the apparitions of the Risen One, and in the light of Paschal faith, in these same works his disciples will read in a definitive way the revelation of the divine nature of their Master, bringing together the witness of miracles in the apostolic kerygma, preaching that Jesus *is the Son of God*. In this way the Gospel of Mark (cf. *Mk* 1:1), known as the gospel of miracles, is set out at the beginning and in a programmatic form, and the first conclusion of John's gospel is equally explicit: "Jesus in the presence of his disciples made many other signs that have not been written in this book. But these were written because you believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God" (*Jn* 20:30-31).

##### The biblical testimony about the works accomplished by Jesus and their purpose
The works performed by Jesus as a testimony of his divine mission are indicated in the books of the NT generally using four recurrent terms: "miraculous power" or "act of divine power" (gr. δύναμις), "sign" (gr. σημεῖον), "prodigy" (gr. τέρας) and "miraculous work" (gr. ἔργον). As already in the First Testament, **the miracle, even when presented as a miraculous work, always remains a "sign" of God and a sign that refers back to God**.

The Gospels report numerous signs and miracles performed by Jesus of Nazareth. One of the aims of these works is undoubtedly to attest to the divinity of his mission as Son and Messiah sent by the Father, since through them those present are induced to believe in him, and **their portentous character frequently arouses acts of praise and gratitude to God** (cf. *Mk* 2:12; *Lk* 7:15; 18:43; 19:37). However, an important motive of Jesus' miracles is undoubtedly to meet the needs of those who turn to him with faith; not infrequently Jesus himself prevents their demands, acting on his own initiative.

Matthew and John speak at length about the "works of Christ" and the "works of God" (gr. ἔργα), those which Jesus does on behalf of the Father and which the Father works in him. Paul will insist more on the works of divine power, centered on the gift of justification obtained from the redemption of Jesus Christ.

The idea of a messianic revelation through signs is especially present in John. The evangelist proposes seven signs-miracles (gr. σημεῖα) narrated in orderly sequence, accompanied by important speeches of messianic witness, ideally retracing in the first part of his Gospel some prodigious events that accompanied the Exodus of the chosen people. The following are narrated: the transformation of water into wine at Cana (cf. *Jn* 2:1-11); the healing of the son of a royal official (4:46-54); the healing of a sick man in the pool of Bethlehem (5:1-18); a multiplication of loaves (6:1-13); Jesus walks on water (6:16-25); the healing of the born blind man (9:1-39); the resurrection of Lazarus (11:38-44). **Both Matthew and John quote surprisingly explicit phrases with which Jesus affirms the purpose "to move to faith and conversion**" of the miracles he performed: "Then he began to reproach the cities where most of his wonders had taken place (gr. δυνάμεις), because they had not converted: "Woe to you, Corazìn! Woe to you, Betsàida! **For if at Tyre and Sidon the wonders that were in your midst had happened, they would have been converted long ago, dressed in sackcloth and sprinkled with ashes"". (*Mt* 11:20-21); "If I had not done among them the works that no one else has ever done, they would have no sin" (*Jn* 15:24); and again: "believe me: I am in the Father and the Father is in me; if nothing else, believe it for the works themselves" (*Jn* 14:11).**

A comparative analysis of the four gospels reveals no less than 40 different narrations of miracles performed by Jesus, apart from a dozen or so apparitions of the Risen One. It is possible that some of the same events have been reported by the different evangelists with such differences that they appear to be different events; in this case the overall number should be reduced by a few units. **The "weight" of the presence of these narratives within the editorial material used by the evangelists is, however, very relevant: they cannot therefore be considered as something circumstantial.** The case of the Gospel of Mark stands out, in which the accounts of the miracles of Jesus occupy 31% of the global text and reach 47% if the last 6 chapters concerning the passion of Christ are excluded. Their presence is also significant in the *Acts of the Apostles*, where about thirty or so miracles and portentous events of various kinds are narrated.

The evangelical miracles of healing are certainly the most numerous. Among them are 3 resurrections of human beings already dead (the daughter of Jairus, the son of the widow of Nain and Lazarus of Bethany). However, there is no lack of numerous miracles performed on or in relation to elements of nature: the transformation of a large quantity of water into table wine, at least two different multiplications of breads in favor of several thousand people, fishing for fish in miraculous proportions (and circumstances), episodes in which Jesus intervenes to quell a storm, walks on water, or makes a boat land instantly on the shore. These are interventions that are carried out with extraordinary safety and effectiveness.58 Except in rare cases in which "additional" operations are commanded to complete what he does (cf. *Jn* 9:7), healings always have an immediate character and concern a variety of pathologies: sudden recovery from strong fevers, stable recovery of sight, hearing and speech, interruption of chronic bleeding, suspension of epileptic seizures, instant healing from leprosy, recovery of motor function following paralysis or congenital malformations. Among the miracles of healing we should probably also include exorcisms on demons, if we remember that the mentality of the time attributed to certain diseases, particularly epilepsy, to the invasive presence of evil spirits.

Contemporary exegesis cannot exclude that some of the miracles narrated may be "post-Easter rereadings" of the divinity of Jesus Christ, to whose full awareness the disciples came only after his resurrection.59 In some cases, as in the case of the miraculous fishing told by John's second closing, which theologically re-reads - now on the horizon of the missionary Church - what Luke places at the beginning of public life, or as in the picture that Luke paints on the road to Emmaus - an icon of the Eucharistic Church - to propose such a hermeneutics would not be unreasonable. Nor can we exclude that in a limited number of cases the account of some of the prodigious works of the Master of Galilee is an editorial construction aimed at offering a literary context in which to transmit both the testimony of the messianic nature of Jesus Christ, as this had emerged from the attendance of the disciples, and the faith in his divinity professed by the Church. In some miracles, such as the transformation of water into wine at Cana, the resurrection of Lazarus, or the healing of the demonized in Jerash, exegesis has highlighted the existence of several layers of overlapping editorials, which had the task of making explicit and more accessible to the recipients what was transmitted in more sober and essential oral forms.60 However, beyond the meticulous search for the historicity of the individual narratives, the miracles of Jesus reported by the evangelists are too numerous, and the narrative plot of the teachings with which they are interwoven is too dense to interpret them all within purely rhetorical or theological-literary frameworks.

As for the purpose for which signs and miracles are performed, the Gospels themselves offer us precise indications. **Their role in confirming the messianic mandate of Jesus of Nazareth is explicit:**

John the Baptist sent us to you to ask you: "Are you the one who must come or must we wait for another?". At that same moment Jesus healed many from sickness, from infirmity, from evil spirits and gave sight to many blind people. Then he gave them this answer: "**Go and tell John what you have seen and heard**: *the blind regain their sight*, the lame walk, the lepers are purified, the *deaf* hear, the *dead rise again, the* good news is announced to the poor. And blessed is he who finds no cause for scandal in me!" (*Lk* 7:20-23; cf. *Mt* 11:2-6).

In Mark's Gospel, the high concentration of miracles seems to be explained in the opening statement: "Beginning of the gospel of Jesus Christ, Son of God" (*Mk* 1:1). Especially in the Gospel of John, the signs accomplished are aimed at moving those present to faith in him, the Son sent by the Father (cf. *Jn* 5:36; 10:25-26; 10:37-38; 11:41-42; 14:11; 15:24). **Nicodemus, one of the leaders of the Jews, will recognize frankly: "Rabbi, we know that you are a teacher who has come from God; for no one can make the signs you make if God is not with him". (*John* 3,2). The tone used by Jesus is clear and explicit: the works and signs made by him in the midst of men are *sufficient reason to believe* in him.** The admonition quoted by John is enough for all: "If I do not do the works of my Father, do not believe me; but if I do them, **even if you do not believe me, believe the works, that you may know and know that the Father is in me, and I in the Father" (*Jn* 10:37-38).**

To an even greater extent than was already the case with the signs and wonders of Yahweh in the First Testament, even **the signs of Jesus narrated by the New Testament are necessarily realized in a horizon of faith: the faith that Jesus can accomplish such works is actually faith *in Jesus*, that is, in his divinity and in his origin from the Father. The demand for faith as a condition for achieving healings is constant (cf. *Mt* 8:13; 9:2; 9:22.28-29; 15:28; 17:19-20; *Mk* 9:19; *Lk* 7:6-10; 8:50; 17:19; etc.).** The miracles performed by the Apostles and narrated in the *Acts* also continue to be miracles of Jesus, for which faith in Jesus is required (cf. *Acts* 9:34; cf. *Acts* 3:6). **The context of faith in which the miracle takes place presents it as "a sign that God is in our midst and is for us", not as a mere miraculous event.** The eyewitnesses of the signs made can remain incredulous and not manifest their faith in Jesus (cf. *Mk* 6:5-6). It is not to the miracle as such that those present are called to lend faith, but to the messianic role of Jesus Christ. **The disputes between Jesus and the Jews reported by John are an eloquent example of how this recognition can be denied despite the signs he had made: "though he had made such great signs before them, they did not believe in him". (*Jn* 12:37)**; denial that flows into a dialectic that forms the background to the whole fourth gospel, that between the light that has come into the world and the darkness that has not received it (cf. *Jn* 1:5.10-11).

Finally, an important goal that accompanies most of Jesus' miracles should be pointed out: **a sincere compassion for the needy whom he encounters on his path, especially for sinners.** Often neglected by many theological analyses,61 this purpose also belongs to the economy of signs of credibility, because **it manifests the credibility of a love of mercy.** The compassionate and merciful love of Jesus of Nazareth seems to be situated on the prolongation of the revelation of Jahve's love for his people and shares its traits. It manifests itself in the many healings of the sick, often preventing their own will to be healed, in the three resurrections of the dead, two of whom were young people, but also in dialogues with people commonly described as sinners, and generally with everyone, rich and poor, men and women. Jesus stands in the midst of the crowds with the sensitivity of a person capable of realizing the particular situation of each one. He cares about their spiritual and bodily life, taking care to provide them with food. This is an interest and a love that is reciprocated by practically all those who frequent him, as witnessed by the great crowds who gathered to listen to him and were willing to follow him in his movements. This attitude of Jesus of Nazareth, which basically concerns a particular aspect of his psychology, constitutes a new, specific sign of the credibility of his person and his doctrine.

##### Historicity and realism of the evangelical narratives about the signs and works of Jesus
A first element that speaks in favour of the historicity of the signs and miracles performed, or at least a large part of them, is that their narration appears to be closely intertwined with the exposition of precise teachings and with the description of the reactions of those present, also an occasion of training by Jesus. For this reason it would be very **difficult to make a sort of separation between the "preaching of Jesus" and the "works of Jesus",** attributing to them an unequal historical or hermeneutical value: **miracles and preaching form such a unified web that if we lose the former we would also lose the latter.** An illustrative example in this regard is **the healing of a paralytic reported by the three synoptics (cf. *Mt* 9:2-7; *Mk* 2:3-12; *Lk* 5:18-26), in which Christ's teaching about the divine power he has to forgive the sins of men (and the corresponding faith in that power) is deliberately associated with the fulfilment of a miracl**e: "Why do you think so in your heart? What is easier: to say "Your sins are forgiven you," or to say "Rise and walk"? Now, so that you know that the Son of Man has the power on earth to forgive your sins, I say to you," he said to the paralytic, "stand up, take your little bed and go home. He immediately rose up before them, took the little bed on which he was lying and went to his house, glorifying God" (*Lk* 5:22-25). **Nor does the narration of miracles seem to have a rhetorical-literary function in order to emphasize or legitimize such preaching, since the evangelists clearly testify that the legitimacy and authority of Jesus' teachings was easily based on other reasons, linked to the credibility of his person**. **Thus the Sermon on the Mount, which contains the core of the Gospel law, does not need miracles to be ratified and transmitted: listeners are sufficiently impressed by the *authority* with which he spoke (cf. *Mt* 7:28-29; cf. also *Lk* 4:15). Putting the "Jesus of miracles" in parentheses, believing it to be necessary to sustain confession in the Christ of faith, inevitably leads to losing the whole figure of Jesus Christ**.62

The fact that the works and signs accomplished by Jesus and witnessed by eyewitnesses had an importance not inferior to the teachings transmitted by him can be seen from the decision made by the eleven at the time of replacing Judas Iscariot: "It is therefore necessary that, among those who have been with us all the time the Lord Jesus lived among us, beginning from the baptism of John until the day he was taken up from us into heaven, one should become a witness, together with us, to his resurrection" (*Acts* 1:21-22). Even if from this passage one does not want to deduce that Matthias, drawn in place of Judas, had seen the Risen One like the other eleven (one must become with us...), which is plausible, one can certainly deduce that it was the attendance of Jesus, the taking part in his life, public and private, listening to his words and seeing his works, is considered a sufficient condition *to understand that Jesus is the Christ*, and to understand at the same time that the testimony of the eleven about his resurrection from the dead is worthy of faith and truthful. To draw upon the mystery of Christ, the apostles knew no other way than that of his life and his historical activity,63 And it is from them that they can preach the fulfillment of the Scriptures.

It would again depose in favor of a judgment of the historicity of evangelical miracles the fact that one of the most evident differences between the canonical writings and the "apocryphal gospels" lies precisely in the anomalous presence, in the latter, of prodigious facts whose aims seem to distance themselves from those previously reported. Unlike the miracles narrated by the canonical gospels, those reported by the apocryphal gospels, instead of emphasizing the salvific dimension and the call to conversion usually associated with the miracle, reported rather prodigious actions carried out without adequate motivation or only to arouse wonder. The miracles of the apocryphals were often given a forcibly symbolic and translated meaning, or they lingered in emphatic and miraculous descriptions, distancing themselves from the sober and historical-narrative style of the gospels welcomed by the Church.

Still recalling the expression of the apostolic kerygma "Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, who died for our sins and rose from the dead" (cf. (cf. *Acts* 2:22-24; *Acts* 10:36-43; *1 Cor* 15:3-5), it must be recognized that miracles occupy in this proclamation the important role of attesting to the true identity of the subject, that is, his divine sonship, on which depended, in the apostles' intention, the truth of the redemption and its universal effects for the human race, as confirmed by the dogmatic development of the first centuries. If the Gospels were written to bear witness to the divinity of Jesus Christ, without which one would not understand the preaching of a universal redemption capable of replacing the Covenant established by Jahweh with the Fathers, then the **narratives of miracles constitute an intrinsic part of this witness; it does not seem possible, therefore, to be able to leave them aside without losing at the same time the entire content and salvific scope of what the Apostles intended to transmit**.

The analysis of the historicity of miracles can benefit from the same criteria used with regard to the Gospel narratives as a whole, in particular **the criterion of multiple attestation and that of continuity/discontinuity.**64 Miracles are spoken of in different sources of the NT and according to different literary forms: from detailed and extended accounts to summary and synthetic summaries; with relatives' quotations within episodes of different kinds, or with reports expressly dedicated to them. In the *Acts we* find phrases that suggest that, regardless of the sides for or against the recognition of Jesus as the risen Messiah, the fact that Jesus of Nazareth had spent some years in public, working healings and miracles in the midst of the people, was something well known to all (cf. *Acts* 2:22: "as you well know"; *Acts* 10:38-39). **The criterion of conformity or continuity - that is, it attributes greater value to narratives that show continuity with the historical-contextual environment in which they are believed to have arisen - seems well verified by the link between miracles and "preaching of the Kingdom", which was considered by all to be the prophetic activity par excellence (exemplary, in this regard, the pericope of *Mt* 4:23-25, in addition to the messianic self-proclamation of *Lk* 4:16-21).** The miracle is very often associated with the demand for inner conversion and is therefore imposed as a salvific work on the body and soul; it is followed by the invitation to proclaim the works of God, to glorify Him and bear witness to Him with one's life, **all elements which place the activity of Jesus in continuity with that of the Masters of Israel (cf. *Mt* 11:20-24; *Lk* 10:13-15).** At the same time**, the miracle represents the irruption of something new, which interrupts many of the usual patterns among the Jews and cannot therefore be interpreted as a simple literary construction which arose from the community in which Jesus lived and worked. In fact, his approaching the lepers to cure them (a disease considered impure by the Jews), the numerous healings carried out on the Sabbath day, the authority with which Jesus carried out these works, in his own name and by virtue of a virtue that belongs to him alone, are elements of discontinuity.**

As observed with regard to the compassion and mercy manifested by Jesus during most of his miracles, an analysis of the **"style of Jesus" in his thaumaturgical activity can also be used as a further criterion of authenticity. His works arise from a sensitivity to human suffering, not from the desire to make showy gestures: his activity is directed towards the good of the person and not towards obtaining public recognition**. Even when miracles are performed with the declared purpose that those present believe in his divine origin from the Father, their ultimate purpose remains the conversion of hearts to the new logic of the Kingdom of God (cf. *Mt* 12:28), not the human glory of Christ. These are rather sober narratives, at times even bare, within which Jesus moves according to his usual personality, without the need to transfigure himself (when he does so it will not be to perform miracles: cf. *Mt* 17:1-8 and parallels). Significant, in this regard, is Jesus' reproach of the unusual "proposal" of some disciples to punish with a shower of fire from heaven (the image is taken from the First Testament) the cities that had not accepted their preaching (cf. *Lk* 9:54-55). Similarly, he blames the attitude of those who, in order to decide to believe, seek only signs and wonders (cf. *Jn* 4:48). "The discretion surrounding Jesus' thaumaturgical activity is in harmony with the context and meaning of his miracles. **There is no search for himself, never a miracle that he performs to highlight his person. Christ rejects the sensational and amusing performances that Herod had requested of him**. He demands silence from the miracle seekers. When the people are exalted, Jesus hides. After the multiplication of the loaves, he asked his disciples to leave to escape the messianic fever that took hold of the crowd.65 To those who ask him for a sign "out of context", wishing to give it the burden of proof of the truth of his words, he will respond by speaking of *the sign* par excellence, that of his Resurrection (cf. *Mt* 12:38-39; *Jn* 2:18-22). The same attitude will be maintained at the supreme moment of death: he who has worked miracles for others will not accept the challenge of working miracles for himself, coming down from the cross (cf. *Mk* 15:29-32).

With regard to the realism of the narratives and the relationship between subjective experience and objective happening, it should be noted that those who, while witnessing his miracles, did not want to believe in Jesus, did so not out of a lack of conviction about the "truth" of the events observed: the healings are not considered "tricks", nor is Jesus accused of malice. The criticisms of those present follow other directions: this man, the adversaries say, "is not from God" because the power to cast out demons was given to him by the devil himself, or because he performs miracles on the Sabbath, in contravention of the Law. Those who "do not believe", it is not that they do not believe in his miracles: they actually stop at them, not going beyond the prodigy and not grasping what it reveals about the subject who performs it and his saving mission. The episode of the healing of the born blind reported in the fourth Gospel (cf. *Jn* 9:1-39) is a very special realism, still capable today of arousing a significant appeal for rationality of a scientific nature. The miraculous person is subjected to meticulous recognition: first by verifying that he is the beggar known to all, and not someone who simply resembles him; then by questioning the person concerned about the precise manner in which the healing took place; finally, his parents are questioned to obtain information about the congenital and not transitory nature of the infirmity. Once the reality of the event is established, with a new interrogation, the debate moves on to the identity of Jesus, on how a sinner who does not respect the Sabbath could have accomplished such a miracle.

Regarding realism and the existence of an objective foundation for the miracles performed by Jesus, one last element should not be overlooked. The four Gospels are unanimous in noting that many crowds followed Jesus (cf. *Mt* 4:25; 7:28; 9:36; *Lk* 4:42; 5:3; 9:11; 11:29; *Jn* 6:5-7), often willing to march long and sacrifice in order to attend his preaching and works (cf. *Mt* 4:25).
