It is reasonable to suppose that this occurrence of crowds, rather than the depth of the doctrine taught - which they also admired - or the actual understanding of the messianic signs that he offered, was in truth due to the actual occurrence of sensitive and concrete wonders, to which many had been present as eyewitnesses (cf. *Jn* 6:26; *Mt* 16:9-11; *Lk* 5:15). Such a hypothesis seems the only one capable of justifying the rapid spread of his fame and the growing desire, sustained for at least a few years, to follow his movements en masse. It does not seem easy to succeed in gathering itinerant crowds, evaluated in several thousands (*Mt* 14,21; *Mk 6,*44; *Lk* 9,14; *Jn* 6,10: five thousand; *Mt* 15,38; *Mk* 8,9: four thousand), in the total absence of at least some real episodes, able to attract them and to justify their well-founded hope of receiving goods of some kind.

In summary, there are sufficient elements to believe that the narration of the signs and miracles of the Gospel satisfies a condition of *historicity* and that this belongs to the core of the message that the apostles and evangelists wanted to convey about the identity of Jesus of Nazareth, as known and experienced by them. We can also affirm that a judgment of *realism* regarding the occurrence of at least a significant part of the facts described is reasonable. This is suggested by the following elements, over and above the criteria for evaluating the historicity of the Gospels, already seen previously:

- the high frequency of the narratives, their style and their purpose that distance them from a fabulistic type of literature;
- the close narrative correspondence between the episodes of the miracles and the preaching of Jesus;
- the necessary support that the miracles offered to the preaching of the apostolic kerygma and its rapid spread;
- the reactions of Jesus' contemporaries, both to the reception of miracles and to their rejection;
- the popularity that the works of Jesus of Nazareth achieved, attested to by the huge crowds that followed.

The miracles of Jesus transmitted to us by the Gospels were works accomplished with the purpose of moving to faith and conversion of heart, to prepare those who witnessed them to accept the Kingdom of God already recognized in him, the Son sent by the Father, while revealing God's merciful love for all men, made visible in the Word made flesh. Their role within the preaching of the apostolic kerygma was (and still is today) that of attesting to the true identity of the subject of the kerygma itself, an identity on which the figure and the universality of redemption depended, according to a dogmatic teaching soon gathered from the baptismal formulas and the first Councils. Without the affirmation of the *divinity of* that subject it would not be understood why the apostles were able to preach, in Jesus Christ, a *universal* redemption, declaring in it definitively fulfilled the Law delivered by Jahve to Moses and the Promise made to the Fathers.

##### 5.5.5 Jesus' call to the prophets and the fulfilment of the Scriptures
Alongside miracles, the traditional Catholic Apologetics proposed prophecies as one of the two main reasons for credibility external to the subject, destined to confirm the divine origin of Revelation. As for the miracle, also for the "sign" of the prophecies, a rereading is necessary today that highlights their Christological reference and clarifies their authentic meaning in the context of the reasonableness of the faith. **The appeal to prophecies, in fact, would make no sense if not within the fundamental logic of Christian credibility, that of the *dynamic between promise and fulfilment*.** An apologetic use of prophecy as a simple sign of the prediction of the future, considering it alone a divine sign, would have no meaning; just as the appeal to the miracle as a simple sign of surprise would have no meaning. The relationship with time, certainly intrinsic to the sign of prophecy, is fully revealed only against the background of history *as salvific history,* and not on the basis of a simple chronology recognized subjectively significant.

Just as miracles admit a Christological concentration because they are *signs of Christ* and signs oriented to direct the attention of the subject towards his salvific mission, so too the prophecies point in their entirety to the *Promise par excellence,* the Incarnation of the Word, progressively revealing its features. In a certain way they are all *prophecies of Christ*, both because he is the subject of Scripture as a whole and because their place in the dynamics of salvation history finds meaning only if there is a fulfilment of history, and this fulfilment is Christ himself. From the understanding of the Christological event as the fullness and fulfillment of the whole Revelation necessarily follows the Christocentric understanding of the prophecies and their concentration around the Word made flesh, the definitive Word of the Father.

In addition to being used by Jesus himself during his earthly preaching, the sign of prophecies becomes a strong point of apostolic preaching and of the first evangelization, and then enters with vigor into patristic literature, both as the object of the catechesis of the Fathers and as the theme of their theological reflection. This widespread use naturally descends, from the very beginning, from the reception of the First Testament as a necessary place for a correct hermeneutics of the Christian event, on the basis of a method that the evangelists, and after all Jesus himself, inaugurate.

Apostolic preaching is *preaching of an accomplishment*. It is certainly so in the context of Judaism, where the fulfillment of Scripture could be understood in an easier and more direct way: as we know, this mode of preaching identified a precise strategy of evangelization, also because of the privileged (but not exclusive) place that Christians chose to carry it out, the synagogues, at least for about thirty years after the resurrection of Jesus.

But the *logic* of the fulfillment of the Scriptures will be destined to extend also to pagan environments and in the different cultures of the gentiles, progressively assuming a new and wider hermeneutics. What has now been fulfilled in Christ are not only the Scriptures, but all the aspirations of humanity and those of every single human being. Even certain aspects of pagan myths and prophecies are courageously re-read in a Christian key, not because the word of the Gospel is placed in the context of writings or traditions which were certainly foreign to it, but to testify that the prophecy and the expectation of Christ were already present in peoples and cultures as seeds of the Word, as fruits of an early action of the Spirit whose role in creation and history is now beginning to be recognized ever more vast and profound.

##### The logic and argument of the fulfillment of the Scriptures according to the Gospels
A first element of interest is the call to fulfil the prophecies and Scriptures that the evangelists attribute to the preaching of Jesus himself. **The clearest example of this is the solemn messianic self-proclamation witnessed by those present in the synagogue of Nazareth, to whom Jesus read the famous passage of Isaiah (cf. *Is* 61:1-2):** after the reading, "He rewind the scroll, handed it back to the janitor and sat down. In the synagogue, everyone's eyes were fixed on him. Then he began to say to them, "Today this Scripture has been fulfilled which you have heard. All gave testimony to him and were amazed at the words of grace coming out of his mouth" (*Lk* 4:20-22). This messianic conscience will be proclaimed again on the occasion of his public life, always with reference to the fulfilment of the Scriptures, not without the idea of a tension between acceptance and rejection, expressed by the image of the stone discarded by the builders and then become a cornerstone (cf. *Mt* 21:42-44; *Mk* 12:10-11; *Lk* 20:17-18). The use of the appellative "Son of Man", very frequent especially in the synoptics (cf. *Mt* 24,30; 25,31; *Mk* 8,38; 13,26; 14,62; *Lk* 6,22; 19,10; *Jn* 9,35; etc.), should also be considered as a messianic declaration.), by which Jesus qualifies himself in reference to the eschatological vision of Daniel (cf. *Dn* 7:13), especially in contexts of an apocalyptic character, which are intended to highlight his role as judge who brings to completion the history of man and the cosmos.

Jesus quotes the Scriptures very frequently, using the usual didactic division between Law and Prophets, and he does so with unexpected wisdom, not without sometimes arousing a certain surprise in his interlocutors (cf. *Jn* 7:15-16; cf. also *Lk* 2:47). Most of the time, Jesus cites the Scriptures as support for his preaching, in the manner of the rabbis and by analogy with the prophetic ministry itself. There are, however, explicit references to the Scriptures made in order to show the credibility of his mission, as for example in the case of the disputes with the Jews reported by John: "You scrutinize the Scriptures thinking that you have eternal life in them: it is precisely these that bear witness to me" (*Jn* 5:39). And again: "For if you believed Moses, you would also believe me; for he wrote of me. But if you do not believe his writings, how can you believe my words?" (*Jn* 5:46-47). The mention in the *voice of the Father*, which the evangelists point out on three occasions (cf. *Mt* 3:17; 17:5; with parallels. in the synoptics; *Jn* 12:28-30), should also be understood as a testimony that "what the Father had revealed" must now be seen in continuity with listening to the Son, because it represents in a certain way his fulfilment (cf. also *Heb* 1:1-2). In this light should also be read the episode of the Transfiguration (cf. *Lk* 9:30-31), as a great icon of the fulfillment of the Law (Moses) and of the Prophets (Elijah), at the centre of which is the Incarnate Word. As for the return of Elijah as a sign of the imminence of the Messianic times (cf. *Ml* 3:22-23), remembered by some of those present at the event of the cross perhaps as a sign of his protection of the afflicted (cf. *Mt* 27:47), Jesus himself offers an authoritative interpretation of it by revealing the meaning of the mission of the forerunner, John the Baptist (cf. *Mt* 11:14).

The references to the fulfillment of the Scriptures, through Jesus' own words, acquire a growing rhythm in the context of his passion and death. And this from the announcement of the facts to Nicodemus (cf. *Jn* 3:14-15) and to the unbelieving disciples (cf. *Mt* 16:22; cf. also *Jn* 12:16), as well as to the twelve at the Last Supper: "I know those whom I have chosen, but the Scripture must be fulfilled: He who eats my bread has lifted up his heel against me. I tell you this now, before it happens, because when it happens, believe that I am" (*Jn* 13:18-19; cf. also *Jn* 17:12). The frank and essential reference - almost an exclamation of sigh - reported by Mark, "every day I was among you in the temple teaching, and you did not arrest me. Let the Scriptures therefore be fulfilled!" (*Mk* 14:49), finds more space in Matthew and Luke (cf. *Mt* 26:52-56; *Lk* 22:36-37), to become in the latter, in the words of Jesus, the expression of a re-reading of all the events that have taken place up to that moment: "for everything that concerns me is now coming to completion" (*Lk* 22:37). In John, the supreme event of the cross is read as the conclusion of a total fulfillment: "Everything is fulfilled" (*Jn* 19:30).

The permanent and founding value that the post-Easter rereading of the events of Jesus' passion, death and resurrection, made in the *light of the fulfillment of the Scriptures,* assumed for the whole believing community and for its incipient evangelizing mission, is masterfully illustrated by Luke in the scene of Emmaus:

He said to them, "Fools and slow of heart to believe everything the prophets have said! Was it not necessary for Christ to suffer these sufferings in order to enter into His glory?". And, beginning with Moses and all the prophets, he explained to them in all the Scriptures what referred to him (*Lk* 24:25-27).

The evangelist himself solemnly emphasizes the relationship between the fulfillment of the Scriptures and the universal preaching of salvation in the context of the apostolic mission that closes his Gospel:

Then he said: "These are the words that I said to you when I was still with you: all the things written about me in the law of Moses, in the Prophets and Psalms must be fulfilled. Then he opened their minds to understand the Scriptures and said to them, "Thus it is written: Christ will suffer and rise from the dead on the third day, and in his name the conversion and forgiveness of sins will be preached to all peoples, beginning with Jerusalem. You are witnesses to this" (*Lk* 24:44-48).

In addition to the use that Jesus himself makes of it, the editors of the Gospel narratives speak widely of the fulfillment of the prophecies. It is well known the wide use that the Gospel according to Matthew makes of it, for which prophecies are a constant glossa to the description of the facts of Jesus' life. Tradition has given us the reason for this, pointing out that its author was directed to a predominantly Jewish audience. Therefore, from the very beginning, the writing intends to show the close relationship between the life and works of Jesus and numerous passages from the *Book of Isaiah*: "All this happened so that what was said by the Lord through the prophet might be fulfilled: *Behold, the virgin shall conceive and bear a son: to him shall be given the name Emmanuel,* which means *God with us*". (*Mt* 1.22-23). The years of childhood are related to four precise prophecies (cf. *Mt* c. 2), while in the narration of the episodes of public life there are dozens of explicit references to the fulfillment of prophecies, often introduced by the phrase "according to what was foretold/announced by the prophet..." (cf. *Mt* 3,3; 4,14; 8,16; 11,9-14; 12,17; 12,39; 13,14; 13,35; 21,4; 21,42; 27,9; etc.).

John, like Matthew, also places among the first pages of his Gospel a clear reference to the fulfillment of messianic prophecies. He does so in the words of Philip who, turning to Nathanael, a true Israelite and therefore a prototype of those who awaited the Messiah with a sincere heart and were now called to believe, announces to him: "We have found him of whom Moses was written in the Law and the Prophets, Jesus, son of Joseph of Nazareth" (*Jn* 1:45). As was to be expected, even in the comments with which John accompanies his exposition of the facts, the call to the fulfilment of Scripture finds its emotional and narrative culmination in the Paschal Mystery of the Passion (cf. *Jn* 19:24,28), death (cf. *Jn* 19:33-37) and Resurrection (cf. *Jn* 20:8-10) of Jesus Christ.66

The high number of occurrences and the concentration of the call to the prophetic Scriptures around the events linked to the Paschal Mystery of Jesus Christ, make one rightly believe that the fulfillment of the prophecies not only represents an original content of Jesus' preaching, but also constitutes a central element of the apostolic kerygma. The apostles do not and cannot disregard the announcement of this fulfillment. It is of little importance to distinguish, exegetically, whether the link to the prophecies is proposed by the evangelists in relation to the credibility or intelligibility of the events that have occurred. That is, whether the reference to the prophetic Scriptures served to believe in Jesus of Nazareth or to understand what had happened to Jesus of Nazareth. The two things are inevitably intertwined, because once you "understand" what happened you cannot help but believe what those events really meant. The reasonableness of faith in Jesus is proposed on the natural extension of faith in the word of God, already accepted and meditated on by those who knew the Scriptures.

##### The fulfillment of Scripture in the preaching of the Apostles
From their very first pages, the *Acts of the Apostles* also convey specific references to the fulfilment of the Scriptures. Well rooted in the discourses of Peter (cf. *Acts* 1:16; 3:19-24; cf. also *Acts* 4:27-28), the explanation of the prophecies is seen at the heart of their proclamation about Jesus of Nazareth, Messiah and Redeemer: "To him all the prophets give this witness: whoever believes in him receives forgiveness of sins through his name" (*Acts* 10:43. cf. also *Acts* 13:27-35.41-42).

In analogy with what we read in the scene of Emmaus, in *Acts* Luke places the explanation of the true meaning of Jesus' passion, death and resurrection - understandable only in the light of the Scriptures - on the lips of deacon Philip, in the scene of his meeting with the Ethiopian, an official of Candáce, according to a catechetical logic that ends with the administration of baptism:

*
*[Embedded Image]*
 Acts of the Apostles 8,31-35*

Stephen's speech before the Sanhedrin will offer an extended and heartfelt rereading of the whole salvific history of Israel now seen entirely in its radical orientation towards the revelation of Jesus as Christ (cf. *Acts* 7:2-53). The conclusion of the episode with the martyrdom of the young deacon shows that the courageous confession of the fulfilment of the Scriptures was now considered part of the testimony to be given to the world.

The explanation of the Scriptures in order to show their fulfillment in Christ is also present in Paul's preaching, often taking on the nuance of careful study, proper to the rabbinical context to which Paul belongs: "Walking along the road through Amphiopolis and Apollonia, they came to Thessaloniki, where there was a synagogue of the Jews. As was his custom, Paul went there and for three Saturdays discussed with them on the basis of the Scriptures, explaining them and arguing that Christ should suffer and rise from the dead. And he said, "The Christ is that Jesus whom I proclaim to you"" (*Acts* 17.1-3). And again: "When they had arrived there [Berea], they entered the synagogue of the Jews. These were of nobler sentiments than those of Thessaloniki, and they welcomed the Word with great enthusiasm, examining the Scriptures every day to see if things were really like that". (*Acts* 17:10-11). We find the same methodical attitude in Apollo (cf. *Acts* 18:24-28). The fulfilment of the Scriptures is still at the heart of Paul's trial before his countrymen, after his arrest in Jerusalem and before the Apostle decides to appeal to Caesar's Roman court (cf. *Acts* 26:21-23). Even once he had arrived in Rome, he would immediately gather together the Jews who lived there, presenting for days the facts concerning Jesus of Nazareth, based on the Law of Moses and the prophets (cf. *Acts* 28:23-24). As for the twelve, also in the Gospel proclaimed by Paul the role of the fulfillment of the prophecies is central and indispensable: "I stand here as a witness to the humble and the great, saying nothing other than what the Prophets and Moses declared was to happen, that Christ should suffer and that, first among the risen from the dead, he would proclaim the light to the people and to the nations" (*Acts* 26:22-23).

The New Testament epistolary develops in continuous dialogue with the Scriptures of Israel. Unlike the preaching collected by the Acts of the Apostles, this dialogue does not so much play the direct role of sustaining the reasonableness and credibility of faith in Christ Jesus as, in the first place, that of providing a great hermeneutical horizon for understanding the First Testament in the light of the New, according to what Paul emblematically expressed to the Corinthians: "Until now, when Moses is read, a veil is stretched over their hearts; but when there is conversion to the Lord, the veil will be lifted" (*2Cor* 3:15-16). The reason that makes faith in Christ credible is therefore the light that it dims, a light capable of finally making people understand what they already possessed, but did not know in all its splendor. The New Testament *locus* that best expresses this grandiose overall vision is undoubtedly that of the *Letter to the Romans* and the *Letter to the Hebrews*.

##### The re-reading of the main prophecies oriented to the salvific event of Jesus of Nazareth
As with the *signs*, prophecies should be understood in their Christocentric direction and not as a mere announcement of future things, intended to arouse attention or cause surprise in their actual occurrence.

Beyond the fact that Jesus himself applied a number of prophetic passages to Himself, and that the evangelists and other New Testament hagiographers used the direct or indirect citation of numerous prophecies as narrative support for the description of the events reported, ecclesial Tradition made a systematic rereading of the First Testament, highlighting those groups of prophecies that seemed to have an explicit relationship with the salvific events of the Incarnation and the Paschal Mystery of Jesus Christ.

They are, in any case, biblical pages whose full understanding can only be achieved in faith and by faith. Yet, both the insistence of the Magisterium prior to the Second Vatican Council to consider prophecies as adequate signs for the credibility of faith (cf. DH 2768; 2779; 3009; cf. however also DV 7 and 15), and the widespread use made in this sense by the Church's Tradition, suggest that fundamental theology, in its reflection on credibility, does not omit to offer any summary.

We therefore summarize, by way of example, the main biblical places handed down to us in this regard by Tradition:

1. the prophecies linked to the Promise given to Israel, about a blessing and a descendants capable of embracing all the Gentiles, especially as set out in the *Book of Genesis*, as then fulfilled in the universality of the redemption brought about by Christ and in the propagation of the Christian message (cf. *Gen* 12:2-3; 15:5; 27:27-29; 35:10-12);

1. the main Old Testament prophecies progressively oriented to the prediction of the appearance of the expected people, of the promised Messiah (cf. *Gen* 49,8-10; *Nm* 24,17; *Ez* 34,11-15; *Mic* 5,1-4; *Zac* 9,9-10);

1. the prophecies about the nature of the Messiah and his divine sonship (*Ps* 2; *2Sam* c. 7);

1. the prophecies concerning the passion and death of Jesus (cf. *Ps* 22,2.8-9.15-19; 31,6; 55,13-15; 69,8-12.22; *Wis* 2,18-20; *Is* 53,2-10; 52,14; *Zc* 9,9; 11,12-13; etc.);

1. the prophecies about the resurrection of Jesus Christ (cf. *Ps* 16:10; *Is* 53:11-12; 52:15).

They should also be added to them:

1. the predictions that Jesus makes about the outcome of his earthly event, namely his passion, death and resurrection (cf. *Mt* 16,21; 17,12; *Mk* 8,31; 9,31-32; *Lk* 13,32-33; 17,25; 22,37; etc.), including those referring to the behaviour of the disciples or some of them in particular (cf. *Mk* 14,27.30; *Mt* 26,21-25);

1. the predictions he makes about the future of the Jewish people, the desolation of Jerusalem and its temple (cf. *Mt* 21:43; 23:37-38; 24:15; *Lk* 13:34-35; 21:20.24; *Mk 13:*2; 13:14; etc.);

1. those which describe the reactions of Jews and Gentiles towards him, the spread of the Gospel by his disciples, and the events which will accompany his universal proclamation (cf. *Mt* 10:17-25; *Jn* 12:32; *Mk* 16:17-18; etc.);

1. and, above all, those contained in his eschatological discourses (cf. *Mt* 24:4-14; *Mk* 13:5-37; *Lk* 21:5-36).

It cannot be excluded that the predictions of Jesus reported by the evangelists about his passion, death and resurrection, and about the events that accompanied his historical realization, are in part due to the post-Easter editing of the events in Jerusalem. The narration of these predictions would then have had as its purpose to show the awareness possessed by the Master about everything that was about to happen, of which his disciples had been witnesses and which, in its psychological and existential richness, could not be expressed otherwise. However, the precise recording of the disciples' lively reactions to some of Jesus' predictions about his imminent future, testifies to the fact that at least some of them were formulated in the precise historical context in which they were narrated.

##### 5.5.6 The Resurrection of Jesus is the sign par excellence and the reason for the Christian proclamation.
The Resurrection of Christ is the center and the reason for the Christian proclamation. The Church was born as a community of witnesses to the resurrection of Jesus Christ: to be baptized are those who believed in the proclamation of the risen Christ. Faith in the resurrection of the dead is faith in the resurrection of bodies and not mere faith in the immortal condition of the human soul. Beyond the different anthropological perspectives of the ancient world, here lies the main originality of the Christian message on eternal life, in whose proclamation the nascent Church found serious difficulties both in the Greek-Roman context and in a part of Judaism.

##### A decisive sign
As we have observed, contemporary theology has made a Christocentric reconversion of the classic reasons for the credibility of faith. The Second Vatican Council confirmed this orientation by speaking of "signs of salvation" and identifying them substantially with Christ and the Church: Christ as the primary sign and the Church as the *sign of Christ*. The New Testament Revelation indicates an itinerary that leads from the economy of signs to the sign par *excellence* given by God to mankind, that of the Resurrection of Jesus Christ.

Already the testimony of Matthew's gospel invited us to see in the prophecy gathered from the *Book of Isaiah* an explicit reference to the Incarnation, recognizing it as *the sign* that God would give to men (cf. *Is* 7:10-14). **Within the economy of signs, when Jesus proposes himself as the *decisive sign*, he essentially refers to a sign par excellence, that of his *Paschal Mystery*: "Then some scribes and Pharisees said to him: 'Master, from you we want to see a sign'. And he answered them: "An evil and adulterous generation demands a sign! But no sign shall be given to it except the sign of Jonah the Prophet.** [...] On the day of judgment, the Queen of the South will rise up against this generation and condemn it, because she came from the ends of the earth to hear the wisdom of Solomon" (*Mt* 12:38-39.42).

To the request of the Jews to give a sign justifying his way of acting with authority in the temple, Jesus will again respond with the sign of his death and resurrection: his body is the temple of the new covenant which will be built in three days (cf. *Jn* 2:18-22). It is by virtue of this supreme sign that He has the capacity to move hearts and to draw all things to Himself (cf. *Mt* 27:54; *Jn* 12:32).

We must therefore now look more closely at *what kind of sign it is*.

##### The anthropological and theological contents of the "sign" of the Paschal Mystery of Jesus of Nazareth
The passion and death of Jesus of Nazareth are the sign of *someone whose coherence of life deserves respect and attention*. He who is willing to suffer death to bear witness to the truth of his doctrine deserves the attention of anyone who appreciates the consistency and sincerity of life. He who recommended to love his enemies, preached to bless those who cursed and invited to consider everyone as brothers and sisters, especially the last, the dispossessed and the oppressed, because they were loved by God, now with his own life bears witness to his sincere will not to hate, not to curse and not to renounce to become last, disinherited and oppressed, because he is supported by the faith that God loves him and asks him to do so. Such a person deserves that his words be reread, that his doctrine be examined, that the object of his preaching be taken into consideration. It is reasonable to do so. The sign of his coherence of life qualifies and founds such reasonableness. He is, in this sense, *a credible person*.

It also belongs to the logic of the Paschal Mystery as a resolutive sign of the credibility of Jesus of Nazareth *the way he faces his passion and death*. His humility and endurance at the time of the insults, judgment and scourging, the sobriety and depth of his comments towards his accusers, his full mastery of himself and his own emotionality throughout the hours of his capture and passion, his singular acceptance of the cross, accepted and not avoided, the forgiveness of his persecutors, his concern for those close to him: all these are elements that give the personality of the crucified Nazarene a singularity out of the ordinary that fully justifies attention to him and to the message he proclaimed. Listening to this man is reasonable, because there is something in him that does not repent in others, even in those who have suffered a similar history of tribulation and abandonment. There are reasons for credibility mysteriously hidden even in the way Jesus dies. A death would seem premature (cf. *Mk* 15:44; *Jn* 19:33), which comes almost imperishable, because it is assumed at the end of supreme invocations and dialogues. Mark's testimony seems very frank and significant in this regard: "Then the centurion who stood in front of him, seeing him die like that, said: "Truly this man was the Son of God!". (*Mk* 15:39; cf. also *Mt* 27:54); more solemn and meditated on is John's eyepiece: "He who has seen bears witness and his witness is true; he knows that he speaks the truth, that you too may believe" (*Jn* 19:35).

As will later be expressed in a more articulate way in the Gospels, apostolic preaching qualifies Jesus' Easter as *a vicarious sacrifice consumed for the love of mankind*, as a source of salvation for those who did not possess it or who had lost it. Even those who, in the first instance, did not understand the reason for this sacrifice and the meaning of this death, could not fail to feel called upon because, as has already been observed, anyone who bears witness to the truth of his message with the non-violent offering of his life certainly deserves respect and attention. But beyond this, it is the words of Jesus uttered at the Easter supper, celebrated as a sacramental anticipation of his death, that justify greater attention to the story of the crucified Nazarene. In affirming the purpose of what his gestures meant, a given body and blood shed *for you* (cf. *Lk* 22:19-20; *1 Cor* 12:24-25), for *many*, for the *forgiveness of sins* (cf. *Mt* 26:28; *Mk* 14:24), **Jesus establishes an unequivocal link between his death on the Cross and absolution from guilt**. **Whoever has the perception of the existence in himself of a moral conscience, of actions that are approved and others that are condemned, of a painful fracture between what one morally wishes to be and what one really is, cannot but read in the words of the Last Supper a content that touches him closely. No one has ever said to obtain God's forgiveness for everyone like this. Whoever, in his or her own existence, has had the experience of guilt and has wished for the corresponding forgiveness of a God on whom one recognizes one's life depends and to whom one recognizes oneself responsible, sees Jesus of Nazareth as someone whose life and death involve him or her deeply**.

If the credibility of mercy and forgiveness are better understood by a conscience that knows how to recognize itself as guilty before God, **the credibility of love, that of one *who declares to give his life for love and* then comes to really do what he says, is a significant appeal to all, because of the anthropological structure of every human being.** The capacity to give and receive love is in fact the fundamental existential experience of every person: the resolutive criterion of the credibility given by God to man, marked by the economy of love, reaches everyone in the deepest sanctuary of their being, that of their heart. It is presented as a promise fulfilled, the promise to give one's life for one's friends, then fulfilled in the sacrifice of the Cross, towards which Jesus goes with the full freedom of love. Evidently, the answer to the question "why am I loved in this way?", or even "what will this gesture of sacrifice tell me?", can only be better understood by those who already move within a moral life horizon, for whom the expression "died for our sins" is significant. Yet, even he who still does not feel fully involved in the death of Jesus as a *vicarious sacrifice*, cannot fail to feel involved in it at least as a sacrifice of *declared love*. Even if a witness, previously recognized as credible, were to err in offering himself unnecessarily as a designated victim, his gesture cannot fail to attract, deserve to listen to, to invite reflection. He who is dying, whoever he is, claims to die *for love of me*. The cross of Jesus Christ is not a sign in front of which one can pass indifferently: it is a human sign that measures the human.

An integral part of the "sign par excellence" is the announcement of the Risen One, a sign that becomes such also because of the radicality of the absolute novelty proposed to the recipient of the announcement. The resurrection of a crucifix humiliated and tormented, witnessed in a historical context made up of places, names and circumstances, which distance it from the typical narration of epic or mythological genre, is capable of making a significant appeal to all men. The value and density of meaning it carries, precisely because it is rooted in an invariable anthropology, that of life and death - not only in its existential aspects, but also in its physical and biological ones - are such that they remain unchanged throughout history, even when judged in the light of the progress of scientific thought or evaluated against the background of religious thought in general. It is the preaching of a disconcerting event, which can have as its author only God himself, which shows how Jesus of Nazareth is capable of realizing what he himself foretold, because he possesses, as something of his own, that life which in a fountain-like way belongs to God alone (cf. *Jn* 10:17-18). Jesus' words, which unite all men and women to his destiny of life and glory, are understood as the unexpected proclamation of something that has always been awaited (cf. *Jn* 14:1-3; 14:18-20; 17:24; 11:25-26).

Once it is recognized that the meaning of the cross is that of a vicarious sacrifice offered, out of love, in ransom for sins, the announcement of the Risen One assures that this forgiveness has indeed been granted, and that what happened on Calvary is an infinite source of merit and mercy. The Paschal Mystery of Jesus is thus understood as a revelation of merciful love and forgiveness, a proclamation that eternal life with the beloved is obtained through the cross, conversion and repentance.

**All this pushes the human being to approach the mystery/event of Jesus' resurrection with an unprecedented seriousness. To do so with a different attitude would mean renouncing the honesty of intelligence, not having fully grasped the truth of the biological, philosophical and religious dimensions that characterize the human race, nor the dignity of the ultimate questions that place him in the being of the cosmos and question him about the meaning of his own existence.**

##### Hermeneutics and dogmatic structure of the proclamation of the Paschal Mystery of Jesus
Before delving into the truth of the resurrection between faith and history in the next section, let us summarize the essential content of the Christian proclamation, whose credibility rests, ultimately, on the person of Jesus of Nazareth, in the light of the hermeneutics offered by the apostles of his earthly event and the mystery it contains.

The announcement to the world that Jesus died as our Redeemer, in order to obtain from God the forgiveness of man's sins, and rose from the dead, benefits from two precise hermeneutical collocations. One, towards the people who possess the Scriptures and the Covenant, unequivocally presents Jesus of Nazareth as the Christ, the Messiah of the Promise made to the Fathers, the One who fulfills the Scriptures, because all the Scriptures spoke of him; the other, towards the people who did not possess the Promise, makes it clear that the God of Jesus Christ is the God of heaven, the Father Almighty, the provident creator of heaven and earth. This last hermeneutics, which the Gospels do not develop but know (cf. *Mt* 6:9; 6:26-32; 11:25), soon appears in the apostolic and patristic preaching to the Gentiles and Greek-Roman culture.

The two solid links to the one true God who is revealed in the Scriptures and in the history of Israel, but also as the Creator of nature, do not constitute a preaching strategy: instead, they are two hermeneutics that make the Christian proclamation comprehensible, offering the necessary conceptual and contextual framework so that it can be correctly interpreted.

**The apostolic kerygma**, we repeat it once again, is then structured as follows:

- Jesus is the Son of God (because he is the author of miracles and, above all, because of his resurrection from the dead),
- Jesus is the Christ: in him the Scriptures have been fulfilled (in their aim at the Messiah, in predicting the Kingdom preached by him and the redemption of the people from their sins),
- Jesus Christ died for our sins and rose from the dead, a guarantee of the truth of our salvation and our forgiveness.

The Eucharistic celebration, the place where Jesus' Passover is proclaimed and the word that convokes the Church, also reproduces, from the beginning, a kerygmatic structure: Jesus is Christ (liturgy of the word: readings from the prophets, from the First Testament in general); he is the Son of God (liturgy of the word: readings from the Gospels); he died for our sins and rose from the dead (Eucharistic liturgy).

Already in the early baptismal liturgy the fundamental elements of the proclamation can be structured in apostolic *dógma*, presenting two precious theological clarifications:

- the cosmological collocation of salvation brought by Jesus of Nazareth, made explicit by his filial bond with God, Almighty Father, Creator of heaven and earth;
- the Trinitarian framework of the Christian event, linking it to the Father of Jesus Christ (*His* Son) and to the work of the Holy Spirit (*His* and the *Father's* Spirit).

With regard to the originality and historical diffusion of this announcement, the following important characteristics should be noted:

1. The apostolic preaching that Jesus Christ is the Redeemer of the world, died for our sins and rose from the dead, *breaks out with a surprising chronological rapidity and with a novelty of content that seems to derive neither from the Jewish nor the Hellenistic mentality*. In fact, the possibility that God had assumed human nature and died on the cross to save us remained a scandal for everyone. In its substance, this message presented itself as a novelty that upset Jewish messianic expectations but also the canons of Greek thought. Twenty years after Jesus' resurrection, Paul bears surprisingly explicit witness to it (cf. *1 Cor* 1:22-25; *Acts* 16:32-33).

1. If salvation could have been an expectation of those to whom the kerygma was announced, *the way in which it is stated that such salvation has now been given is totally unprecedented*. "The Gospel message which animates the Apostles of Christ appears as the fulfilment of what the people, in a fragmentary and obscure way, already possess. That this fullness of light emerges as a historical event - Christ's death and resurrection - is precisely the difficulty and torment of preaching".67 The Apostles feel the unprecedented weight of a word and a proclamation entrusted to them.

1. *The universality of redemption is not the result of a progressive categorical association, but is proclaimed as a fact*, the "fact" of the passion, death and resurrection of Jesus, who is Christ. The original apostolic preaching is presented from the beginning as a unity of the universal salvific dimension of Christ and of the historical story of Jesus. We are not faced with an undue overlapping of two different concepts: the object of faith is neither a Christ without Jesus, nor a Jesus without Christ; but precisely Jesus-the-Christ.

#### 5.6 The truth of the Resurrection of Jesus Christ, between faith and history
In its historical origin, the apostolic announcement of the Resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth was addressed to both Jews and pagans. For both its addressees it represented an absolute novelty; to both its acceptance aroused significant resistance, albeit for different reasons.

##### 5.6.1 The announcement of the Risen One enters history
The Jews were puzzled - as were the apostles themselves - by the fact that the resurrection of the body of a dead man could be given here and now, in the course of history, because they professed (at the time of Jesus certainly the Pharisees) a faith that projected such an event only in the *eschaton*, on the "day of Yahweh". The pagans, especially if of Greek culture, although easy to lend faith to miraculous events, were not prepared to give newness and immortality to the human body, seen rather as an obstacle to the life of the spirit.

Unlike what happened in the pagan environment, for the Jews the intelligibility of the Risen One could be based on an understanding of "eternal life with God" which from the time of the Maccabees also implied a reference to a true corporeity; always among the Jews, moreover, their religious literature spoke of the ascensions of Elijah and Enoch, thus making possible an interpretation of the Scriptures that attributed to the suffering Servant of Yahweh an end of life and salvation, and to the Messiah an exaltation/glorification by God. The pagans, for their part, possessed a philosophy of nature that could facilitate the recognition, in the proclamation of the Risen One, of the language of life and the irruption of the divine, but also the sense of a project of the Logos on a renewed cosmos. Nevertheless, for both of them, the proclamation that the crucified Nazarene man had been resurrected and had been familiar with his disciples did not cease to be seen by most of the recipients as a rambling (Jews) or as a fabulous tale (Greeks).

The proclamation of the Resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth also played, from the beginning, the role of sustaining the credibility of the whole Christian message in order to arouse adherence to the faith. It was (and it is) a motive certainly inseparable from the person of Jesus Christ, because it was a sign offered by him in order to bear witness to his credibility; yet it was a sign which from Jesus is directed towards history, because it is intersected in a sensitive way by the life of the Risen One and its consequences, with the concreteness witnessed by those who "ate and drank with him, after his resurrection from the dead" (cf. *Acts* 10:41).68 This implies that the horizon of understanding the Resurrection - whose hermeneutics are certainly due to the consideration of the whole Revelation accepted in faith - nevertheless embraces and involves nature and history as they are known to reason.

The link with history is well present: to the recipients of the proclamation, in fact, knowledge of Christ's resurrection is not proposed as an intimate and subjective mystery, the final result towards which the mystical experience of those who accept the word converge. Even if we cannot exclude that the experience of the Risen One has involved some form of mystical knowledge - as certainly happened in Paul and probably in some of the apparitions reported by the evangelists - the resurrection of Jesus is announced by the disciples as an "event" capable of justifying why interest in the history of Jesus of Nazareth and his message of salvation can and must be given. The resurrection of Jesus therefore involves faith but also history, it is a gift of grace but also concerns nature.

In the economy of the credibility of Revelation, the resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth also has a double meaning, subjective and objective. According to a personal-subjective dimension, it represents the answer to the anxieties of immortality and perennial life present in the human being, saving him from the discomfort he experiences in considering the corruptibility of his own body. The idea of a perennial life, however, is not *ipso facto a* guarantee of lasting happiness: this intimate aspiration, in fact, could remain a simple reflection of the biological instinct of survival, if the promise of a resurrection of life were not associated with the revelation of a perennial life-with the beloved. In this sense, the call of Jesus' resurrection cannot be separated from the promise of a personal love through which God, in Christ and through the Spirit, guarantees the recovery, full revelation and eternal possession of all that constitutes the historical experience of love, goodness, beauty and truth. The hope that sustains man in the most intimate fibres of his humanity is not, in fact, hope of eternal life *sic et simpliciter,* but hope of eternal love: what makes a person happy is only personal love, and this, paradoxically, more than life itself, as the historical experience of each one can show.

Considered instead in its objective-external dimension, the Resurrection is called to give credibility to the whole message of Jesus Christ, because it sheds light on the identity of his Subject. The Resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth proposes itself as a disconcerting event, which can have as its author only God himself; indeed, such an event shows that its protagonist possesses, as something of his own, that life which in a fountain-like way belongs only to God, something indicated by the fact that Jesus announces his Resurrection and then realizes it (cf. *Jn* 10:17-18).

##### 5.6.2 The living God, cause of life and giver of life
The resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth is a resurrection of life and for life. In it we are definitively revealed that the God of Jesus Christ is the source of life and gives hope of life. A brief biblical study of the relationship between God and life is therefore useful.

The First Testament presents the God of Abraham as the "living God" (cf. *Dt* 5:26; *Jesus* 3:10; *Ps* 84:3; *Is* 37:4; *Ez* 33:11; etc.) and "lover of life" (cf. *Wisdom* 11:24-26), a God who distinguishes himself from all idols precisely because he is alive, unlike the gods of other peoples who are only false simulacra (cf. *Jer* 10:10; *1 Sam* 17:26.36; *Dn* 14:4-5.23-27). To God life belongs as something of one's own, because he is the *cause of* the life of the living: thanks to a direct intervention of God "man became a living being" (*Gen* 2:7), and it is by the will of God that every breathing being (e.g. *nephew*, spirit, breath of life) shares in the gift of life (cf. *Gb* 34:14-15; *Ps* 104:29-30; etc.). The image of God *is on the side of life* and He defends it as His own:69 the respect due to human life is a sign of this belonging (cf. *Gen* 9:5-6). Life is opposed to sin and death, used almost as synonyms by Scripture (cf. e.g. *Ez c.* 18), to the point that God's extraneousness to sin is a manifestation of the relationship between God and life. The term "life" goes so far as to indicate "existence according to the will of God": just as sin is opposed to God, so is death itself opposed to him, something that God did not desire for man (cf. *Ez* 18:23,32).

The NT also knows an explicit confession of faith in the "living God" and reproposes it in very significant contexts (cf. *Mk* 12:26-27; *Mt* 16:16). In particular, it is precisely by begging Jesus "in the name of the living God" (κατὰ Θεοῦ τοῦ ζῶντος) that the Sanhedrin will ask him to reveal his true identity once and for all (cf. *Mt* 26:63). With the life of every human being God establishes a *personal* causal relationship, calling each one to existence himself and knowing him therefore in the most intimate reason for his living and existence (cf. *Ps* 139:13-14; *Jer* 1:5). The NT makes this primordial divine knowledge understood as predestination, an election in the Son preceding creation itself (cf. *Eph* 1:4; *Rom* 8:28-30).

Revelation about the divine origin of life finds one of its most significant expressions in the Gospel of John, which associates life with the very person of Jesus Christ, as in the well-known passage in the prologue: "in Him was life" (*Jn* 1:4). Jesus affirms of himself in the fourth gospel: "I am life (Εγώ εἰμι ἡ)", "I am the resurrection and the life" (*Jn* 14:6; 11:25); turning to the Father, he says that he has received from Him the power to give eternal life to those who have been given him (cf. *Jn* 17:2). Even more explicitly, he came into the world so that men "may have life and have it abundantly" (*Jn* 10:10). The person of Jesus has within himself the capacity, which *springs forth from the Father,* to associate men and women with life: "Just as the Father has life in himself, so he has granted the Son to have life in himself" (*Jn* 5:26); "just as the Father, who has life, sent me and I live for the Father, so also he who eats of me will live for me" (Jn 5:26). (*Jn* 6:57); "as the Father raises the dead and gives life, so too the Son gives life to whom He will" (*Jn* 6:57). (*John* 5:21). In this way, faith in Christ and in his saving mission becomes the source of life for mankind: he who believes in the Son of God has eternal life; he who directs himself to him has life; the words he speaks are words of eternal life; the bread he gives is the bread of life, because it is his own body; the water he gives is water that gushes forth to eternal life; in order to have life and bear fruit one must be united to him like branches to the vine (cf. *Jn* 3:15.16.35; 4:15; 5:40; 6:35.54; 6:68; 15:4-6). All the Johannine realism of the association between "the Word of life" and a visible, tangible life will touch one of its apexes in the prologue of his *First Letter* (cf. *1 Jn* 1:1-3). The miraculous healings operated by Jesus, of which the synoptics speak at length, especially the resurrections (cf. *Mk* 5:35-43; *Lk* 7:11-17; *Jn* 11:38-44), are all signs that indicate a redundancy, a relapse also on the physical-biological level, of that "life" that Jesus is in the mystery of his divine Person.

Life and death are two realities that lie entirely in God's hands, and in his alone (cf. *Jb* 12:10; *1 Sam* 2:6; *Dt* 32:39). To have in our hands the destiny of life and to call every living person to existence means that in God, as we have seen, life can be preached as something "proper and original". To be on the side of life, in this case, means the ability to bind the righteous to Himself with a relationship "stronger than death itself", that is, that it is not interrupted by biological death: God does not abandon life in the tomb nor does He allow His saint to see corruption (cf. *Ps* 16:23; cf. *Ps* 49:16; 73:23-24). But the God of Israel does not only give back moral life, hope, as the context of Ezekiel's vision in the bony plain might suggest (cf. *Ez* 37:1-14). He is also capable of returning to life after death. This is borne out by Israel's faith in the survival of those who die (the *refaim* who inhabit the *sheol*), although the immortality of the soul will not be conceptualized specifically, since the unity of the person is known above all. Faith in a resurrection of the righteous in terms of "eternal life", which already in earlier times was associated with the idea of judgment on the "day of Yahweh", will then gradually become established at the time of Babylonian exile (cf. *Dn* 12:2-3) and above all from Hellenic domination (cf. *2Mac* c. 7; *Wisdom* c. 2).

In accordance with his mission to come into the world so that men "may have life and have it abundantly" (*Jn* 10:10), Jesus manifests by his resurrection that the "gift of life" brought to men is not exhausted on the ethical-moral level, but involves a true association with the mystery of the resurrected-always-living; it is therefore the gift of a life that continues transfigured after death, that is linked to Israel's faith in the resurrection of the dead "on the last day" (cf. *Jn* 11:24) and clarifies it definitively: "This is the will of the one who sent me: that I lose nothing of what he gave me, but that I may rise again on the last day. For this is the will of my Father: that whoever sees the Son and believes in him may have eternal life; and I will raise him up on the last day" (*Jn* 6:39-40). The promise of eternal life (αἰώνιος ζωή) - an expression that indicates "a life that shares in the eternity of God" - is clear and unequivocal in Jesus' words and in general throughout the NT. In John's Gospel the expression will appear 17 times. The promise of life, which the early religious experience of Israel saw more easily associated with the promise of the earth, now becomes the promise of an everlasting life in the "Kingdom of God", in the "kingdom of Christ", "together with Christ", "with God". In the Gospels this revelation finds its summit in the eschatological discourses of the synoptics, while in the apostolic letters it will be expressed in the description of our solidarity with the Risen Christ. In the book of *Revelation* the situation of "perennial life with God" is described in a language which recalls the youthful elements associated with Christ the Lamb as the source of life, transfiguring them into a context of definitive cosmic renewal (cf. *Rev* 21:1-7; 22:1-5). God's "dwelling place with men" is definitive, complete. And the consolation of men in the eternal love of divine life is also accomplished: "Behold, the tent of God with men! He will dwell with them and they will be His people and He will be God with them. And he will wipe away every tear from their eyes; and there will be no more death, nor mourning, nor lament, nor toil, for the former things have passed away" (*Rev* 21:3-4).

Now, the supreme sign that God has the power to give life after death is precisely Christ's resurrection. The Christian faith is therefore faith in "God rising from the dead". It is also the supreme sign that Christ himself can give life, because "he is the risen one". And it is also a sign of the power of the Holy Spirit and of his revelation as "he who gives life" (cf. *Wisdom* 12:1). Confessed by the Church as "Dominum et vivificantem", his role in the gift of life, known from the first page of the *Book of Genesis* (cf. *Gen* 1:2), finds its most accomplished historical manifestation in the resurrection of Jesus (cf. *Rom* 8:9-12).

The resurrection of Christ must be understood as the Trinitarian action of the living God. The formula "God resurrected him" (*Acts* 2:24) is among the oldest, because the resurrection is initially understood as the action of God-the-Father. Gradually, however, the awareness of the power proper to the Son will also grow, because faith in the divinity of Jesus Christ, Son of God and true God: "That is why the Father loves me: because I give my life, and then take it up again. No one takes it away from me: I give it from myself. I have the power to give it and the power to take it again. This is the command that I have received from my Father" (*Jn* 10:17-18). The Son rises by his own virtue, but as one who receives everything from the Father.

##### 5.6.3 The Resurrection of Jesus Christ as Mystery and Event
At the height of Revelation, the Easter of Jesus Christ presents itself to us in its dimension of *event,* but also of *mystery*. It intersects history but also transcends it.

##### The announcement of the Risen One and its historical foundation
There are many aspects that concern *its* ***dimension as a historical event***. **The rapid and progressive coming together of the nascent Church, whose beginning and raison d'être find a satisfactory explanation only in the propagation of the proclamation of the Risen One, belongs to history**. It is a message that spreads in an immediate way, extending in a few years throughout the Mediterranean basin. His first written testimony, traceable in the *First Letter to the Thessalonians* (cf. *1 Thess* 1:10; 4:14), most likely dates back to the year 50-51, while several sources, including pagan sources, testify to the presence of this message in Rome, on the occasion of the persecution promoted by Nero, and in large parts of the Roman Empire, already around the year 60 and thus about 30 years after the events in Jerusalem.

It is certainly the object of history the custom, immediately practiced by all Christian communities, to celebrate their main cultic meeting on the day called "day of the sun" and renamed "day of the Lord" (*dies Domini* ; *dominicus*), the day *after* the Jewish Sabbath. These were festive meetings, assemblies of thanksgiving (gr. εὐχαριστία, gratitude, gratitude), which possessed, however, the sacramental sign of a sacrifice of death, and were therefore understandable only if the death commemorated, that of Jesus of Nazareth on the cross on the day *before the* Sabbath, had not been the last word of what was remembered, which, in fact, will then be explicitly confirmed by the structuring of those liturgical formulas which will present the Eucharist as a public witness of faith in the resurrection.

Finally, **the tradition of the "empty tomb**", that is the absence of historical data that treat the tomb of Jesus in the same way as other tombs of important people, which have become a place of memory as sites that guarded, or had permanently guarded in the past, the corpse, must be considered as a datum accessible to history, even with all the limitations associated with the historical method. Despite the extent and scope of the religious movement that arose around Jesus of Nazareth, his corpse or the place of his burial have never constituted, for the primitive Church, any point of reference for the worship of its followers, unlike what has commonly happened in other religious movements.

##### The resurrection of Jesus is also the object of faith
The Resurrection of Jesus Christ, Son of God, appears in all the symbols of the early Church as a confession of a binding and common faith. The resurrection's belonging to the object of faith does not depend only on the temporal distance that separates the newly baptized from the events transmitted, now beyond their direct experience: in fact, believing the risen Jesus is believing something that seems humanly impossible, and that seemed so even to the contemporaries of Jesus Christ.

The historical testimony of the apparitions of the Risen One, as transmitted by oral tradition and by the documents drawn up by the believing community, does not reduce the resurrection to an event that can be circumscribed within the canons of history alone: **the apostles see Jesus alive, but they *believe him risen****, that* is, they believe in him as the source of life, as one who is able to resume his life after death and to communicate it to anyone who believes in him.70 That the resurrection of Jesus implied a certain explicit faith on the part of the disciples is also testified by the NT, when feelings of disbelief are reported, at least by some of his followers (cf. *Mt* 28:17; *Mk* 16:11.13-14; *Lk* 24:11; 24:21-25; *Jn* 20:25). The Resurrection is therefore *both announced and believed*: as the ancient precept *Exultet* of the Easter Vigil states, no one knows the moment in which it took place.

It is aspects of the resurrection of Jesus Christ that transcend history that are presented to us by the letters of the Apostles, by Paul in particular: it is the beginning of a new creation, the first fruits of the eschatological future, the eternal present of a sacramental economy capable of re-presenting Christ and his merits beyond the chronological passage of time. The Church confesses her faith in this transcendence of the Resurrection over history through her liturgical actions, presenting the sacraments as the actions of Christ, especially the Eucharistic celebration, whose meta-historical meaning is expressed by its nature as a memorial, that is, as a salvific re-presentation, of the death-resurrection of Jesus.

##### Articulation between faith and history
Assessing the articulation of the relationship between faith and history, the physical death of Jesus of Nazareth and the discovery of the empty tomb are events that belong, in principle, to the sphere of the conclusions of history, and belong to it with a degree of certainty proportional to their accessibility and knowability, direct or indirect, attainable by the historical method. They are events which, in themselves, do not transcend human reason and which are transmitted in time by means of a proclamation that postulates a simple *human faith*, based on the reliability of witnesses.

In the case of the apparitions of the Risen One, the articulation between faith and history is more complex. They, too, presented themselves to their protagonists as events that happened in history, albeit very singular events; their transmission to those who did not experience them required, in principle, a human faith. And yet it had to be (and it is for us) a well-examined and critically founded human faith. The proposed facts, that is, the apparition of a dead man coming back to life, went beyond normal human experience: it was therefore necessary to carefully examine the reliability of their witnesses. It is not surprising that, initially, some interpreted them as being faced with a ghost (cf. *Lk* 24:37-43). *Strictu sensu*, therefore, the apparitions of Jesus were not an object of theological faith, neither for the apostles nor for us who rely on their testimony.

To be the object of theological faith, and therefore the object of a knowledge that only grace can put into action, is instead the Resurrection considered in its most intimate and profound meaning, as a divine action through which God, "who gives life to the dead and calls to existence things that do not yet exist" (*Rom* 4:12), can bring back to life, fully constituted of body and soul, those who have already died. It is in the God capable of this action that the Apostle Thomas will confess to believe (cf. *Jn* 20:28), and this is the object of faith of all the apostles at the moment when they recognize the true and definitive identity of the Master, the Son of the eternal Father. This recognition cannot depend totally on flesh and blood, because it requires the help of divine grace (cf. *Mt* 16:17).

It must therefore be concluded that the Resurrection is indeed the object of history, but also the object of faith. It reproduces the logic, repeatedly manifested by Christian Revelation, of presenting itself as a mystery and as an event: an event which intersects history but transcends it. In the aspects that pertain to the dimension of event, the facts that led to faith in the Risen One - his real death, the empty tomb, the apparitions of the Risen One who offers himself to the sensory experience of his disciples, the consideration in a new light of the First Testament prophecies, the spreading of a witness proclamation, etc. - are all aspects that are relevant to the dimension of event. - They are the object of history, as far as their reconstruction can reasonably go. In the aspects that concern the mystery - the divinity of the Risen One, how is it possible to experience continuity between the corpse of the crucified one and the transfigured body of the Risen One, the eschatological meaning that the Resurrection encompasses and the fruits of salvation that it anticipates as the firstfruits - the believer exercises his act of faith in the light of the coherence of the whole Revelation and the value of apostolic witness. In the Resurrection of Jesus Christ, the historical dimension and the dimension of faith are inseparable, as are the dimensions of event and mystery in Revelation and the Incarnation of the Word. Faith implies the historical truth of a series of events, from which it adheres to new, higher truths which transcend both the historical fact and the forces of natural reason alone. History, by itself, cannot inspire faith, but faith, without the indication of historical events which reason deems appropriate, does not come to formulate its option.

##### 5.6.4 The testimony of the narratives of the resurrection of Jesus Christ transmitted by the New Testament
The resurrection of Jesus Christ is presented by the NT as the origin of the apostles' preaching and the foundation of faith (cf. *Acts* 2:22-41 and 10:36-43; *1 Cor* 15:3-4 and 15:12-20). From a historical point of view it is placed, together with Pentecost, at the origin of the spread of the historical movement which arose around Jesus of Nazareth.71

##### Narrated appearances and announced message
In contrast to **the *announcement* of the resurrection, which despite the different editorial forms used by the NT - "God raised him up", "Jesus rose from the dead", "Jesus appeared alive", "we saw the Lord", etc.. - It manifests a certain basic coherence about the substance of the message transmitted**, the ***apparitions*** of the Risen One reported by the evangelical accounts present instead a certain fragmentation and diversification of contents. They **do not allow a chronological reconstruction or a precise comparative picture of the events**, not only because of the **diversity of the traditions and witnesses involved, but also because of the experiential, emotional and spiritual density that they communicated.** As Karl Adam rightly observed some time ago, we are not faced with different narratives of the same moment, but rather **with different modalities of the same message**.72 The narratives of the apparitions seem to be *a context in* which a *text,* or rather *the text par excellence, is to be* transmitted and made intelligible. **Each of the editors narrates and emphasizes in his own style those aspects that he considers most important**: Luke develops as usual what Mark tells in an essential way; Matthew emphasizes the most significant themes with regard to Jewish listeners; John favors certain themes that allow him to develop in a theological key.

At the same time, the fragmentariness, the diversity, and at times even the literal contradictions of some stories, as well as the presence of omissions that are difficult to explain - such as the total silence about the meetings in the cenacle in the editorial office attributed to one of the Twelve (Matthew), or the absence of episodes referring to Peter, precisely in the gospel that is believed to gather his oral preaching (Mark) - have aroused perplexity and questions. Already **the first Christian authors expressed the conviction that the differences found in the Gospel accounts of the apparitions did not alter the substance of the fact transmitted, but obliged us to explore a plurality of biblical senses**. Medieval and modern apologetics had instead emphasized the idea of *concordia discors*, recalling that the truthfulness of a story would be reinforced by the existence of non-identical and partially discordant versions from different witness sources. Contemporary exegesis, on the other hand, patiently seeks to examine and understand the literary freedom of the various editors, without relying too much on *concordia discors* which, when elevated to a supreme criterion, would sooner or later contradict the exegetical criterion of multiple attestations, usually used in the verification of the historicity of the narrated facts.

In order to address the problem correctly, it should never be forgotten that the resurrection of Jesus Christ was not *primarily* transmitted by Scripture, but by Tradition. The announcement is not based on what is written, but on the contrary, what has been written is based on what was announced by an oral tradition. Yesterday as today, it was and is the life of Christians that manifests this event to the world, and it is this life that cannot be explained *if Jesus had not truly risen*, much more than the diversity of the apparitions narrated.

In spite of the plurality of oral traditions subsequently placed in writing, the substance of what was transmitted is well sketched: **the tomb was found unexpectedly open and empty; a group of people, apostles and disciples, saw Jesus alive; Jesus himself met the disciples several times, in episodes of singular realism, before finally escaping from their physical experience.** Yet, all these events are in reality **a *single fact***. **They are understandable only in their unity, as reflections of a precise message that breaks out and affirms itself: Jesus' resurrection is the unexpected way in which *the Promise was fulfilled*.** Regardless of the different narrative modalities that the announcement was taking on, first orally and then in writing, the historical-unitary character of the *fact announced* is well illustrated, as noted above, by the early manner in which the Eucharistic supper was celebrated: both by the choice to celebrate it on Sunday, because it witnessed *decisive events* for the community that had arisen around Jesus of Nazareth, and by its structuring as a re-presentation of an Easter mystery of death and resurrection.

##### The Resurrection of Jesus in the New Testament: an overall view
In addition to the numerous New Testament passages where explicit references to faith in the risen Christ appear - present in the Pauline letters and in the episodes of the *Acts* concerning the preaching of Paul and the other apostles - the NT reports at least six important editorial places, relatively extensive, which have as their object the Resurrection:

1. four main accounts, transmitted by the four canonical gospels, mentioning the death and burial of Jesus, his resurrection from death and his subsequent apparitions to various figures in different contexts (*Mt* 27:57-28:20; *Mk* 15:42-16:18; *Lk* 23:50-24:49; *Jn* 19:38-21:14);

1. the Pauline tradition about the death, burial and resurrection of Jesus, with a list of apparitions that followed it, collected by the *First Corinthians* (*1 Cor* 15:1-11), followed by a theological development in an eschatological key (vv. 12-58);

1. the Lucanian narration which precedes the Ascension and which speaks of an iterated frequentation of the Risen One (*Acts* 1:3-9).

To these must be added at least six "missionary discourses" transmitted to us by *Acts*, in which the apostles offer a kerygmatic exposition that flows into a specific testimony of the resurrection of Jesus (cf. *Acts* 2:14-40; 3:12-26; 4:8-12; 5:29-32; 10:34-43; 13:14-42). Among these, in terms of liveliness and internal articulation, stand out the solemn speech given by Peter on the day of Pentecost (*Acts* 2:14-36), the catechesis he directed to the family of Cornelius, centurion of the Italic court (*Acts* 10:37-43), and the extensive speech of Paul given in the synagogue of Antioch in Pisidia (*Acts* 13:17-41).

At the basis of all four Gospel narratives there is a fundamental and original experience: **that of the tomb found open and empty on the morning of the day after Saturday**. All the editors are unanimous in stating that **the first protagonists of this experience were women**. The four canonical gospels agree on the name of Mary of Magdala (cf. *Mt* 28:1; *Mk* 16:1; *Lk* 24:10; *Jn* 20:1 and 20:11); three converge on that of Mary, mother of James and Joseph (cf. *Mt* 27:56 with 27:61 and 28:1; *Mk* 16:1; *Lk* 24:10). The identity of other women is specified, or simply indicated, in various forms: Salome (*Mk* 16:1), Joan (*Lk* 24:10; cf. also *Lk* 8:3), "the other [women] who were with them" (*Lk* 24:10).

Speaking of the women who witnessed the death and burial of Jesus, the Gospels present us under the cross again Mary of Magdala (cf. *Mt* 27:56,61; *Mk* 15:40; *Jn* 19:25), Mary mother of James and Joseph (cf. *Mt* 27,56; *Mk 15,*40), Mary mother of the sons of Zebedee (cf. *Mt 27,56)*, Mary of Cleopas (cf. *Jn 19,*25) as well as Mary, mother of Jesus, together with the disciple John (cf. *Jn* 19,25). Among the witnesses of the Crucifixion and the events of Friday there is also talk of "many other [women] who had gone up with him to Jerusalem" (*Mk* 15:40), that is "the women who had come with Jesus from Galilee" (*Lk* 23:55).

Two Gospel testimonies agree in reporting that, after the women, the first of the apostles to take note of the empty tomb was Peter (cf. *Lk* 24:12; *Jn* 20:4-9); in particular, the fourth Gospel shows him accompanied by John, who was also an eyewitness to the same fact (cf. *Jn* 20:8). Among the eyewitnesses of the empty tomb, no Gospel source mentions Mary mother of Jesus, whom only one source, John, had mentioned under the cross.

Beginning with the discovery of the open and empty tomb, the New Testament sources list, as is well known, a series of apparitions of the Risen One, in contexts and with editorial styles that are also very different from one another: sometimes sober and of a historiographical-reassumptive nature, as in Mark's second closing and in the Pauline tradition (cf. *Mk* 16,9-14; *1 Cor* 15,5-7); other times of a paradigmatic nature more similar to literary constructions aimed at transmitting historical data in a narrative or parenetic form, as perhaps in the scene of Emmaus and in the second closing of John (cf. *Lk* 24,14-35; *Jn* 21,1-25); other times, finally, collecting speeches and reporting actions of the Risen One in a very detailed way, as in the apparitions to the apostles in the Upper Room (cf. *Lk* 24,36-49; *Jn* 20,19-29).

An orderly analysis of the narratives shows how apparitions are the subjects of the apparitions: Mary of Magdala (*Mt* 28,9; *Mk* 16,9; *Jn* 20,14-18) and the other Mary, mother of James and Joseph (*Mt* 28,9); Simon-Pietro (*Lk* 24,34; *1Cor* 15,5); James (*1Cor* 15,7); two disciples on the way (*Mk* 16:12; *Lk* 24:14-35); the apostles gathered in the house (cenacle) in Jerusalem (*Lk 24:*36-49; *Jn* 20:19-23 and *Jn* 20:26-29; *the Eleven*: *Mk* 16:14; *to the Twelve: 1 Cor* 15:5 and then *to all the apostles*: *1 Cor* 15:7), especially Thomas (*Jn* 20:27-28). Then to the "eleven disciples" on a mountain in Galilee (*Mt* 28:16-20); to more than five hundred brothers at once (*1 Cor 15:*6); to the Eleven near the lake of Tiberias (*Jn* 21:1-13), especially to Peter (*Jn* 21:15-19).

The Lucanian fountain willingly lingers on the presence of the Risen One with the disciples for an indefinite time; more precisely, "He showed himself alive to them after his passion, with many trials, for forty days, appearing to them and speaking to them of things concerning the kingdom of God" (cf. *Acts* 1:3). The disciples would have kept a precise memory of this encounter until a final apparition on the road to Bethany, which Luke always places before an Ascension to Heaven (cf. *Lk* 24:50-52; *Acts* 1:9-10). John's first closing, "Jesus, in the presence of his disciples, made many other signs that were not written" (*Jn* 20:30), could also be compatible with the testimony of a relatively extensive attendance after the Resurrection.

**A common element of the accounts of the apparitions of the Risen One is an attitude of "bewilderment and incredulity" on the part of their recipients**: the apostles, at the announcement of Magdalene's "hearing that he was alive and had been seen by her, they did not believe" (*Mk* 16:11); they do not seem to want to believe even when they receive the announcement from the two disciples who witnessed the apparition along the road (*Mk* 16:13); on the occasion of the apparitions of the Risen One in the Upper Room, the disciples, "shocked and full of fear, thought they saw a ghost" (*Lk* 24:37); to overcome their perplexity, Jesus takes food with them (cf. *Lk* 24:41-43; *Jn* 21:9-15; cf. also *Acts* 1:4 and 10:40-41); the Eleven are reproached for their disbelief (cf. *Mk* 16:14); the two disciples to whom he appears on the road to Emmaus are described as "foolish and slow of heart to believe in everything the prophets have said" (*Lk* 24:25); some of the Eleven, in the context of the apparition on a mountain in Galilee "but they doubted" (*Mt* 28:17).

Like the other Apostles, Paul proclaims the Risen Christ (cf. *Acts* 13:16-42; 17:29-31; 23:6; 24:21). The transmission of the various events surrounding the resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth reported in the *First Letter to the Corinthians* is the oldest that has reached us, dating from the year 53 or 54 of the Christian era. He introduces the account with particular solemnity, reproposing a formula of rabbinical tradition: "To you, in fact, I have transmitted, first of all, what I also received...". (*1Cor* 15,3); closing his kerygma with an equally solemn and traditional formula: "So both they and I preach and so you believed" (v. 11). At the end of all the apparitions transmitted to him by Tradition he will also place, as is well known, his personal encounter with the Risen One, emphasizing his peculiarity: "The last of all appeared to me as a final child. For I am the least of the apostles, and I am not worthy to be called an apostle because I persecuted the Church of God" (*1 Cor* 15:8-9). Paul of Tarsus is convinced that he saw Jesus in person: he heard the voice of the Lord on the road to Damascus (cf. *Acts* 9:4-6; 22:7-10; 26:14-18; *2 Cor* 12:4), but he declares that he was also the protagonist of one of his visions/appearances: "Have I not seen Jesus our Lord? (cf. *1 Cor* 9:1). The Gospel that he preaches attests to having known him "by the revelation of Jesus Christ" (*Gal* 1:12 and 1:16). Finally, Luke transmits to us two singular episodes in which the Lord makes himself present in vision again to Paul, to encourage him in his work of preaching and witness (cf. *Acts* 18:9; 23:11).

##### The core of the message of the Resurrection: historicity of the apparitions of the Risen One and summary observations
Beyond the narrative differences between the various sources, the diversity of contexts and secondary events that accompany the stories of the resurrection and its announcement (presence of guards outside the tomb and earthquake in Matthew; silence and fear of women in Mark; absence of mentions to Mary mother of Jesus at the moment of death and burial in all sources except John; apparitions in Galilee reported only by Matthew and the second closing of John; disuniformity in the instructions received by the Master regarding the movements to be made, etc.).) a comparative analysis of the New Testament data shows some important congruencies. They are:

1. The presence of women as the first witnesses of the empty tomb, in the context of the events that took place in the morning of the day after Saturday. They are the recipients of the first apparitions of the Risen One. The mention of Mary of Magdala is unanimous in all sources and belongs to a tradition of certain historical antiquity. From the women the announcement of the empty tomb began, addressed in the first place to the closest disciples of the Master; among these is once again Peter who plays a special role. The announcement of the empty tomb, however, is greeted with strong disbelief, perplexity and perhaps even fear for the unknown developments that the event might have brought with it.
