1. In all narratives, the apparitions have the character of something unexpected. Like the announcement of the empty tomb, the spread of news of the first apparitions meets with considerable resistance. To this resistance, which would never seem to be completely overcome, at least in some, the transmission of the announcement of the Resurrection bears witness to the fact that the Risen One himself wanted to offer himself as an object of sensitive experience, as shown by the insistence of the accounts of the apparitions on the "corporeity" of the Risen One, of which the empty tomb represents the necessary condition.

1. There is an inability of the disciples, immediately after the resurrection, to interpret this event as a *fulfilment of the Scriptures* (cf. *Lk* 24:25-27; *Jn* 20:9); this inability is underlined by the fact that it must be the words of the Risen One themselves that highlight this link (cf. *Lk* 24:44-47). The reading of the Resurrection of Jesus Christ in the light of the Scriptures, however, soon finds a precise space in the primitive kerygma (cf. *1 Cor* 15:4; *Acts* 10:43), to be then matured in a solid and convincing way in the Symbols of faith, where the Resurrection and reference to the fulfilment of the Scriptures seem already indissolubly united.

1. There is a strong convergence that the group of twelve Apostles chosen by Jesus during his public life (among whom, as of Thursday evening, Judas Iscariot was no longer present) is certainly the recipient of specific apparitions. These are more than one, they do not have the character of brief transitory experiences, and, beyond the chosen literary and narrative forms, they convey the idea of a certain, subsequent frequentation of the disciples with the Risen One, at least sufficient to receive from him again teachings and instructions about a mission that was now entrusted to them. It should be noted that the Pauline tradition is also aware of at least a double apparition "to the Twelve" and "to all the apostles", in Jerusalem (cf. *1 Cor* 15:5 and 15:7), which could reasonably be put in relation to the double apparition of John Pauline at the first closing of the fourth Gospel.

The narrations of the apparitions reported by the various New Testament sources, while showing a certain dependence on each other, at least in some cases they should be clearly considered independent of each other, as are the Pauline tradition, the Johannine tradition and the synoptic tradition. There are therefore elements to attribute to the *fact of the* apparitions of the living body of the Risen One to the disciples a judgment of historicity based on a multiple criterion of attestation.

In favour of the objective historicity and not total subjectivity of the apparitions there is also an application of the criterion of continuity/discontinuity. Discontinuity can be recognized in at least two cases. In the first place, the resurrection of the body of Jesus did not correspond to the mentality of the Jews, whose eschatological expectations referred to a resurrection "on the last day" (cf. *Jn* 11:24); in the second place, the hermeneutical significance of the resurrection as a fulfilment of the Scriptures and an event capable of revealing the consistency of the Old Testament faith in a spiritual and not political sense, is not grasped by the protagonists themselves, except at a distance of time, because it is extraneous to their immediate religious context. The events narrated have a significant background, however, also a factor of continuity, namely the attitude shown in general by the disciples, in line with what one would expect from anyone: bewilderment, disbelief and skepticism.

#### 5.7 Objections to the resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth
Beyond the exegetical debate about the various ways of interpreting the narratives of the Resurrection of Jesus Christ, it is appropriate to reconsider some of the objections raised over time to the Resurrection. Initially moved, according to different perspectives, by Jewish thought and then by Greek-Roman thought, these objections will be destined to remain present for a long time in the debate between Christianity and modern thought, almost always, however, in the forms subsequently elaborated by Enlightenment and rationalism. Catholic Apologetics has elaborated some answers that deserve to be revisited and updated in the light of the greater knowledge of the biblical datum and the historical context.

##### 5.7.1 The preaching of the Risen One as a mythological fable
The criticism that rationalism and enlightenment directed to Christianity in the 18th and 19th centuries supported the idea that the whole doctrine of the resurrection, *as a whole*, was the result of the transposition of myths of death and rebirth, widely present in the religious language of antiquity, often in connection with the cycles of vegetation and the changing seasons, whose influence on human life and its stages is well witnessed by the literature of practically every people and culture of the Mediterranean basin and the Middle East. Such an objection is sometimes made, even today, by shallow but influential writings, with the aim of spreading a folkloristic and popular atheism.

The data in favour of the historicity of Jesus of Nazareth and the temporal references fixed both by the events of which he was the protagonist in Israel and by the dating of the first written testimonies concerning the announcement of his resurrection from the dead, impose some important limits to a mythical reading of the New Testament narratives. The construction of a new myth requires a maturation, in the popular conscience and in its celebrations, certainly longer than two or three decades, or rather than the time elapsed from the "events of Jerusalem" and from the written writing of the announcement of the Risen One. Too strong are the links of Jesus' passion and death with the history of Rome and Israel, and too many references to witnesses, protagonists and circumstances, to allow full freedom for the "construction" or even just the mythological "transposition" of a story of the resurrection of the crucified King of the Jews. Places, names, characters, events, are too detailed and susceptible of verification by the recipients of the announcement to justify their easy mixing with elements of mythological scope.

There is no evidence of "creative" encounters between early Christians and pagan religious culture: rather, we know that the Christian message asked of its recipients the specific change of categories and the conversion of hearts. Both the early New Testament sources, think of *Acts* (cf. *Acts* 8:9-12; 13:6-8; 19:18-19; cf. *Gal* 4:8-11; *1Tm 4:*7; *2Tm 4:*3-4; *2Pt* 1:16), both the works of the Fathers of the Church, but also pagan historiography, all are unanimous in testifying that Christians never made compromises with pagan religion and mythology, but strove to purify them from those very elements that criticism of the historicity of the resurrection of Jesus would like to operate in the drafting of the New Testament narratives and confessions.

It is significant to note that in Origen's confutation of Celsus' criticism of the resurrection narratives of Jesus and some widespread pagan conceptions, the arguments in favour of Christianity underline the relationship between the true death of Jesus and a story before the eyes of all.

*[Embedded Image]*
 Origen, *Contra Celsum*, II, 56

If the New Testament editors had wanted to devise suitable narratives to represent what might have happened to Jesus of Nazareth, with nuances that could have ranged from a simple explanation of why the empty tomb to the celebration of a glorification of the expected Messiah, what literary models would it have been more logical for them to turn to? For literary constructions devised for such purposes, the Jewish milieu *already possessed* its own models: these were firstly the apocalyptic genre and secondly the celebratory literature used in some areas of prophecy. In reality, we find no traces of such genres in the tales of the resurrection in canonical writings. The sober genre of the canonical evangelical tales and the essential and kerygmatic genre of the confessions, both evangelical and the letters of the apostles, represent in this regard a *unicum* of difficult explanation.

As already observed, on a time scale of a few decades from the events in Jerusalem, to take elements from Greek-Roman mythological literature or from archaic Egyptian or Assyrian-Babylonian myths, which also spoke of resurrections and ascensions, would not have been possible because of the cultural distance that separated these latter contexts from the community in which the announcement of the Risen One had arisen and was now developing. Mythical contaminations of this announcement would have been possible, but on longer scales of time, once places, witnesses and circumstances could now belong to a past no longer accessible.

Apocalyptic literary genres were used by apocryphal literature, later than the canonical gospels, to celebrate the deeds of the exalted Messiah. Emblematic, in this regard, is the account of the resurrection in the *Gospel of Peter*:

*[Embedded Image]*
 A description of the resurrection in the apocryphal gospel said of Peter

On closer inspection, the objection that the resurrection of Jesus, or the fact of his physical resurrection, was "a religious myth of Greek-Roman derivation", or in any case similar to the mysterious cults of the ancient East, and therefore a story "fruit of fantasy, without any link with reality", was implicitly put forward by the liberal exegetical perspective of the "First Research" on Jesus, of which we have spoken previously, from the *Life of Jesus* (1835) by D.F. Strauss up to *The Essence of Christianity* (1900) by A. von Harnack. The reasons that led us to consider this position no longer sustainable can also be used today to respond to the objection that the announcement of the Risen One would like a message fruit of mythological syncretism. If classical apologetics perhaps did so in a heuristic and polemical way, contemporary Fundamental Theology can do so in a more mature way, leaving open what is still unresolved, but clearing the field of unfounded hypotheses.

##### 5.7.2 The presence of some inconsistencies in NT narratives and the possibility of their composition
As discussed above, the supposed internal incongruities of the narrations can be answered by underlining the kerygmatic and not chronological character of the events reported, recalling the different contextualizations and the different recipients of the stories, as well as the presence of literary elaborations aimed at conveying the underlying historical data through paradigmatic narrative reconstructions. The fact, then, that there is a real underlying historical fact - identifiable with the experience of the empty tomb and the spreading of the news of apparitions to several witnesses - is traditionally based on the realism and sobriety of the evangelical language and on the reactions of the disciples, who distance the events narrated from similar episodes traceable in other religious traditions, when not simply mythological, as well as from the apocryphal writings themselves. There are, however, some dissonances which are imposed by their evidence and which therefore require greater attention on the part of theology, aimed at offering some traces of their composition. We mention two of them here:

1. the apparent lack of importance given by the evangelists to the events related to the resurrection of Jesus and the teachings that followed, when compared to the events of the public life of the Master, especially taking into account the testimonial purposes of these writings (the imbalance is evident in the Gospel of Mark, where, stopping at the first closing, only 8 verses in all are dedicated to the resurrection);

1. the apparent ambiguity about the places where Jesus' earthly event ended (whether in Galilee or in Judea, in the city of Jerusalem).

The gospels were conceived as a written fixation of an oral preaching in which the exposition of Jesus' teachings (i.e. the "way of the Lord", cf. *Acts* 18:25-26) represented the main content. The great prominence given to the facts of passion and death, as the culmination towards which almost all of Jesus' preaching converges, reveals the apologetic-demonstrative intention to base the credibility of Jesus and his teachings on these facts: if the Resurrection is the definitive sign with which Jesus Christ manifests his credibility, public life, miracles, teachings, and above all his passion and death, are the sign with which the evangelists present the credibility of Jesus. The event of the Resurrection closes the written evangelical narratives, but it does not replace them, nor can it, on its own, summarize them. From a certain point of view, the reality of Christ's resurrection should be considered the *presupposition of* the writing of the gospels and their narratives, not the final end of a demonstration itinerary.73 The oral exposition of the kerygma, especially in the examples offered by the *Acts of the Apostles,* shows that the proclamation of the resurrection of Jesus - certainly present at the heart of the Gospel message - seems almost to prepare the way for the subsequent listening to the preaching of Jesus' teachings, which will instead find greater space in the written text.

In the case of the Gospel of Mark, which if limited to the first closing solves the post-septic events in only 8 verses, it is possible that the final part of its original editing has been lost, thus obliging successive editors to summarize with discretion and sobriety (another 12 verses) the facts reported by other Gospel sources. The different style of the second closure (*Mk* 16:9-20) and the same authoritative decision to make it a commentary on the proclamation of this gospel show that this imbalance was already recognized in the apostolic or immediately post-apostolic period, and was understood as the effect of the loss of material considered reasonably present in an earlier period.

Uncertainty is known about the places where Jesus' earthly event ended, whether in Galilee or in the city of Jerusalem in Judea. Also well known is the possible inconsistency between the command by which Jesus orders the disciples to follow him in Galilee, where the Risen One would have preceded them (cf. *Mt* 26:32; 28:7; *Mk* 16:7), and the command to remain in Jerusalem until they would be clothed with power from above, that is, until the day of the descent of the Holy Spirit (cf. *Lk* 24:49; *Acts* 1:4-5). There corresponds a similar parallelism, which is not easily reconciled, between the apparitions to the group of apostles in Galilee (cf. *Mt* 28:16-17; *Jn* 21:1-14) and those in Jerusalem (cf. *Mk* 16:14; *Lk* 24:36-37; *Jn* 20:19 and 20:26). The fact that two sources, Mark and John, cover both the geographical circumstances of Judea and Galilee, if on the one hand it would make a certain congruence with the data in question, on the other hand it does not *ipso facto* solve the problems of chronology of the movements and the respective presences of the disciples in the two places. It does not go unnoticed that, with regard to the geography of the apparitions, the mention of the "missing geographical place" (Judea for Mark and Galilee for John) appears in both cases in the *second closings* of the two gospels, respectively with a final reference by Mark to the table of the Eleven in Jerusalem and a final reference by John to the episode of the miraculous fishing in Tiberias.

There are certainly indications that to resolve such a discrepancy we must once again turn to the different contextualizations within which the various evangelists worked. This is confirmed, for example, by the fact that the omission of the Gospel of Luke to the events of Galilee is intentionally dictated by well-known literary reasons linked both to the recipients of the writing and to the narrative plot chosen by him, which revolves around Jerusalem and the Temple. In the writing of the *Acts*, however, Luke indicates in a period of "forty days" (*Acts* 1,3) the time spent by Jesus with his disciples after his resurrection, making it clear that the rapid closure of his gospel about the facts of the resurrection and ascension to Jerusalem does not meet chronological criteria.

Despite these clarifications, a basic question remains: what really happened between the "events of the day after Saturday" and Pentecost? A question that opens up other minor but equally significant questions: what was the true content of the command formulated by the Risen One: to remain in Jerusalem *until Pentecost* or to follow him in Galilee *before Ascension*? And if we wanted to compose the two commands, would it be plausible to hypothesize a presence of the apostles in Galilee between the Resurrection and Pentecost? And did the apparitions on the Lake of Tiberias reported by John's second closing occur before or after Pentecost?

Starting from the editorial material we have at our disposal we are not able to answer the first question: it is not possible to trace with certainty the content of the original indication of the Risen One, since we cannot exclude that in the editorial forms we have received it was formulated according to the narrative-literary needs of the recipients of the writings.

The second question, the one about the modularity of the apparitions in both places, could be answered *theoretically in the* affirmative, since a period of forty days is sufficient to justify one or even more movements from Judea to Galilee and vice versa. In theory, taking literally as the original message the mandate to go to Galilee, it would also be possible to hypothesize that the apparitions in the cenacle immediately arrived to heal the disbelief of the apostles. One wonders, however, whether, *in practice*, leaving Jerusalem and then returning to it might be reasonable: what sense would it have made to the apostles to leave the place of the extraordinary events they were witnessing and return to their work as fishermen in Galilee? If this could be understood in the light of a precise mandate received from the Risen One, either through the women or directly from him, as reported by Matthew and Mark, it would nevertheless have to be concluded that a new mandate, of which we have no trace, led them to return to Jerusalem again before Pentecost. The hypothesis that only a part of them (Peter, Thomas, James, John and Bartholomew, according to *Jn* 21:2) was in Tiberias, because they were aware of the command to go to Galilee, and that they then returned to Jerusalem once they received news of the command pronounced by Jesus in the Upper Room, does not agree with the fact that both traditions involve the "Eleven". In fact, the "Eleven disciples" are explicitly mentioned in the context of the apparitions on a mountain in Galilee (cf. *Mt* 28:16), and it is still "the Eleven and the others who were with them" (*Lk* 24:33) who received the indication, during the apparition in the Upper Room, to remain in the city of Jerusalem (cf. *Lk* 24:49). On the other hand, they would argue in favour of a certain reasonableness of the hypothesis of a stay (at least of some disciples) in Galilee between the Resurrection and Pentecost, the climate of suspicion and probable danger that surrounded Jesus' disciples in Jerusalem immediately after the first apparitions (to the point of suggesting their departure from the city?), as well as the plausibility of returning there on the feast of Pentecost, also because of the great possibility of contacts that this would have entailed. The data available are therefore, also in this case, insufficient and we are not able to answer with certainty the proposed question.

Also with regard to the third question, that of the chronology of the apparitions of John in Tiberias, we do not have unequivocal answers. John chooses his editorial material in an original way and proposes a theological symbolism that usually transfigures events and signs that really happened. In the episodes narrated around the Lake of Tiberias, *it seems to read again* the similar fishing with which Luke inaugurated the first apostolic mission during the public life of the Master (cf. *Lk* 5:4-11), now proposed in a context that highlights the role played by Peter, but also by John, in the transmission of the faith in the Risen One, the primacy progressively recognized to Peter by the early Church, the apostolic mandate to preach the Gospel with a universal afflatus.74 In the light of the New Testament datum and the literary forms with which it was transmitted to us, we must therefore conclude that we are unable to *reconstruct chronologically* and *geographically* what happened between the Resurrection and Pentecost. We can only affirm with certainty the presence of the Apostles in Jerusalem for much of this period, or perhaps for its entire extension, and that the promulgation of the kerygma "Jesus died for our sins and rose from the dead, fulfilling the Scriptures" originated in Jerusalem from the events around the feast of Pentecost, after which it began to spread rapidly.

According to some exegetes, considering that resurrection and elevation/ascension were understood by the editors of the NT in a single theological framework and that the former is often read in the light of the latter (cf. *Rom* 8:34; *FIl* 2:9-11; *Lk* 24:26; *Jn* 20:17; *1Pt* 1:11; *Heb* 12:2) could generate an advantageous hermeneutics for the solution of the previous questions. The idea that some of the apparitions transmitted, the narrative pictures that represent them, or even some of the locutions having the Risen One as their object, have as their protagonist the "glorified Christ at the right hand of the Father" becomes then plausible, thus favouring the composition of some apparent contradictions: the key to justify the meaning of the teachings of the Risen One would not be that of forcing the various apparitions within a strictly chronological scheme, but that of a theological rereading of what was received from the Risen-Glorified Christ, and above all of what was understood in the light of Pentecost. Emblematic in this regard is the missionary mandate of *Mt* 28:16-20 and the whole context that frames it, whose subject is indeed the Risen One, but as now the repository of "all power in heaven and on earth", whose words give rise to the spread of the Church, sealed, as at Pentecost, by the sacrament of Baptism.75

In essence, the uncertainties and inadequacies in our explanations do not translate into a judgment of inconsistency of the Easter message, compromising our access to a historical dimension of events. On closer inspection, the orderly chronological or geographical reconstruction of the movements of Jesus' disciples is not necessary, neither to preserve the historicity of the event which gave rise to the salvific announcement of Easter Sunday, nor to establish the truthfulness with which the presence of the Risen One and the outpouring of the Spirit accompanied the preaching and the works of the apostles, now witnesses of the Resurrection.

##### 5.7.3 Psychology of witnesses and denial of the realism of apparitions
A classic objection is to hypothesize that the apparitions described by the Gospels were actually visions or hallucinations of which those who transmitted news were the protagonists. Such phenomena, of a subjective nature, would have been realized under the emotional impulse deriving from the discovery of the empty tomb, also because of the strong impression that the personality of the Master had caused in his disciples. Following what is reported in the New Testament narratives, the apostles believe that the first apparitions of the Risen Lord to women belong to this kind of phenomena: to them the announcement of the women "seemed a rambling" (*Lk* 24:11), therefore they did not believe it (cf. *Lk* 24:11); with regard to Magdalene "hearing that he was alive and had been seen by her, they did not believe" (*Mk* 16:11). The Gospels point out that this attitude, partially mitigated by the discovery of the empty tomb by Peter and John, is only changed after the narration of the collective apparitions in which the apostles themselves will be protagonists. The possibility of examining whether the disciples were victims of those same deceptive moods that they themselves attributed to the women can only be conducted from the New Testament data, the only ones in our possession, but also the only ones employed as a starting point by those who deny the realism of the apparitions.

In the NT the Twelve, especially through the interventions of Peter, but also in the dialogues of the other apostles, manifest themselves as simple men, with a healthy life, whose psychology reveals traits of extreme realism and concreteness. Most of them were fishermen, accustomed to making frank and instinctive judgments, little inclined, perhaps with rare exceptions, to experiences of a mystical or intensely spiritual nature. Numerous episodes of which the Gospels are studded, show their frequent delay in understanding the words and teachings of the Master, which they often intend in a concrete-material and not spiritual sense. During the public life of Jesus, the apostles manifest an underlying attitude, of a realistic nature, which frequently leads them to be convinced whether what is happening around them is merely a subjective vision, deceptive phenomena, or instead reality. In the episode in which Jesus walks towards them on the waters of the lake, they ask themselves whether what they see is a "ghost" (cf. *Mt* 14:26; *Mk* 6:49-50); according to the Lucanian tradition, the same judgment - "they thought they saw a ghost" (cf. *Lk* 24:37) - they initially reserved it also on the occasion of the first apparition of the Risen One in the Upper Room, so much so as to cause in the Risen Jesus a significant comment: "Touch me and look; a ghost has no flesh and bone as you see that I have" (*Lk* 24:39).

The idea that the apparitions of the Risen One correspond to subjective visions or hallucinations therefore finds little correspondence in their human psychology. The unanimity of the *reactions of incredulity* is a very significant element in the Gospel narratives that have come down to us: not only do the traditions of the accounts of the apparitions have nothing triumphalistic, but if we exclude the movement of joy, still uncertain in Luke (cf. *Lk* 24:41) and now lying only in John (cf. John (cf. *Jn* 20:20), what these stories seem to want to transmit is a sense of bewilderment which then translates into an exhortation to successive generations not to want to repeat their mistake, that of having offered resistance to come out of the disappointment and defeat of the cross, which for the great majority of the disciples was still the last public experience of the Master.

As far as spiritual experiences are concerned, whether of a mystical or ecstatic order, here too it does not seem an easy attribution of the twelve. In one of the moments of the deepest existential intensity for Jesus, like that of his prayer in the Garden of Olives, the apostles are unable to share a prayer experience with their Master. The *Acts of the Apostles* speak to us of visions and mystical experiences. Peter qualifies as "vision" (gr. ὅραμα) the apparition of the sheet with the animals to be eaten (cf. *Acts* 10,9-19); he wonders whether the miraculous liberation by the angel during his detention in prison is the result of a vision (cf. *Acts* 12,9); Luke tells us that Paul receives a phrase from the Lord during the night, by vision (cf. *Acts* 18,9). In his letters, Paul speaks to the Corinthians about revelations and visions received from God (cf. *2* Cor 12:1). Although the vocabulary used to indicate these kinds of visions is largely analogous to that used for the apparitions of the Risen One, the contexts are different and present significant differences. The apparitions of the Risen One are described as prolonged experiences, not instantaneous or uncertain, nor linked to particular states of consciousness (dreams, ecstasy, prayers, etc.). They enjoy a specific realism, expressed in a clearly intentional way by the actions of sharing a common meal (cf. *Lk* 24,35.43; *Jn* 21,12-13; *Acts* 10,41) and of experiencing, through the external sense of touch, the reality of the body of the Risen One (cf. *Lk* 24,39; *Jn* 20,27). This is certainly not a casual insistence on the Gospel narratives, which can be reasonably related to the presence of a certain distrust in some members of the community of disciples, or in any case aimed at strengthening the faith in the Risen One of the recipients of the proclamation of the Resurrection, expressed paradigmatically by the words of Jesus to the Apostle Thomas (cf. *Jn* 20:29). It should be said, in essence, that the objection of visions or hallucinations, rather than having arisen in modern times, seems to have accompanied the initial spread of the message of the Resurrection and that the editors of the NT themselves take it into account, taking care to provide elements of judgment aimed at countering it.

Finally, a criterion of judgement external to the New Testament source, would lead to the conclusion that the theory of hallucinations, visions or ecstasies, would not be able to give reason for the sudden spread of the news of the Resurrection and to support the subsequent rapid expansion of the Christian message. Experiences of a subjective kind, lived by different individuals, would easily contradict each other, slowing down the determination with which the rest of the disciples would welcome faith in the Risen One. Only the common account of some objective facts, albeit in limited and fragmentary numbers, lived simultaneously and in a comparable way by several protagonists, could be able to channel, quickly and with certainty, the faith of the disciples towards the preaching of the Risen Jesus.

##### 5.7.4 The oldest of the objections: the stealthy removal of Jesus' corpse and disappointed eschatological expectations
Among the objections to the resurrection is the hypothesis of the removal of the corpse from the tomb, which would then be followed, depending on the motive of the robbery, by the intentional announcement of fictitious apparitions or the dissemination of subjective experiences of visions of the risen Jesus as a result of excitement or fanaticism of the community of disciples, or as a simple projection of their hope. It seems logical that the hypothesis of the removal of the corpse by the disciples can only respond to two purposes:

1. politico-revolutionary: to trigger a popular protest against Roman domination in the wake of faith in the Messiah son of David, announced resurrected and working with his own;

1. spiritual-religious: the disappointment of the defeat by the Jewish religious authorities suggested a revenge, now making the memory of the Master eternal, proclaiming him resurrected.

Although the two motivations were, in the Jewish mentality, in a certain relationship between them, it is useful to distinguish them for argumentative purposes. A third purpose, that is

1. overcome the disappointment of waiting for an eschatological return of the Master, which never came true,

would not necessarily imply the removal of the corpse, but only the diffusion of a late announcement of the Risen One, always, however, starting from the tradition of an empty tomb.

The hypothesis of a stealthy abduction of the corpse of Jesus of Nazareth soon spread among the adversaries of the Christian community, as testified by the Gospel of Matthew, offering the account of a Roman garrison asked of Pilate by the Pharisees and the high priests, as a guarantee of the custody of the tomb, with the events that followed (cf. *Mt* 27,62-66 and 28,4.11-15). Beyond the problem of whether the facts narrated allow a reasonable correspondence with reality,76 The episode reported shows that this judgement was already well established at the time of the drafting of this gospel and that, through this gospel narrative, an attempt is made to offer a suitable response to support the preaching of the resurrection. The objection of the stealing of the corpse, collected by the Jewish Talmud, mentioned around the year 150 by Justin in his *Dialogue with Triphon, a* Jew,77 and taken up in modern times by the Enlightenment with Voltaire and rationalism with Reimarus, it continues to play a certain role even today, because it has the advantage of starting from the only one that both sides in the field did not question, namely the presence of the open and empty tomb. However, in order to be reasonably developed, this objection has to face the problem of providing plausible solutions both for the agent and for the motive of the robbery.

If, following the hypothesis (a), it was his disciples who stole the corpse of Jesus, in order to restore politically the kingdom of Israel (cf. *Acts* 1,6), foreseeing to exploit the wave of consensus that a large part of the population still paid tribute to the movement created around their Master, logic immediately presents us with two counter-objectives.

The first is that, in preaching the risen Christ, the apostles associate with it a content that they themselves, only a few years earlier, would never have imagined: the preaching of the remission of sins and spiritual salvation addressed to all peoples. The second is that the action of the stealing of the corpse, not having had the desired political effect, could not sustain for long the public commitment of the apostles, until the outpouring of their blood as witnesses of the Risen One. The consistency of the apostles in preaching the Risen Christ to the point of martyrdom is not only testified by biblical sources, but also by pagan ones.78

If the intentional subtraction of the corpse instead pursued exclusively spiritual purposes, according to hypothesis (b), the idea of "simulating" by deception a prodigy of divine character clashes with the context of the narratives developed around the announcement of the Resurrection, which remains a non-triumphalistic context, but rather, as we have already seen, of bewilderment and gradual, painful awareness. These same counter-objections, i.e. the sober style of the Gospel narratives and the consistency of the disciples' lives, can also reasonably exclude the hypothesis (c) if it involved a stealthy subtraction of the corpse.

Only the hypothesis remains at this point that the starting point remains only and exclusively the open and empty tomb, and that the disciples try in some way to interpret it, ignoring the cause of the disappearance of Jesus' corpse.79 The agent of the abduction of the corpse, or the cause of its disappearance, would remain unknown. The announcement of the Risen One would then be their "spiritual" reaction, immediate or late, to an event whose reasons they ignored. The question to be asked in this case becomes then: would the discovery of the empty tomb be sufficient, by itself, to graft a "faith in the resurrection" and the consequent subjective visions of the Risen One? In other words: would the disappearance of the corpse of Jesus of Nazareth, for whatever reason unknown to us as well as to his contemporaries, a disappearance not supported by real apparitions of the living Risen One, but only by visions, hallucinations or simple hopes, have been enough to give rise to a religious, cultural and theological phenomenon such as Christianity?

The question has an important value, crucial for the defence in the face of the reason for the whole Christian event, and cannot be resolved in haste. In order to answer it one must not now look for some precise counter-evidence, but rather examine the Christian phenomenon as a whole. The Resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth, as the culmination and fulfilment of his Paschal Mystery, is in fact not just any old event, however unusual it may be. It has the capacity to enlighten the *whole faith of Israel*, the long times and profound religious experiences that have sustained it throughout history. The idea that the preaching of Jesus alive and risen is the result of a contingent and fortuitous event - the disappearance of his corpse by unknown hands and for unknown reasons - and that this event is almost unexpectedly used as a trigger for a coherent and comprehensive re-reading of the whole faith of Israel, is a hypothesis that leaves reason deeply perplexed. An event with unknown causes would in fact be capable of revealing, and thus of making understood, the whole cosmic and anthropological depth of a mystery of death and resurrection as the first fruits of a new creation and fulfillment of the Promises, something that the disciples themselves had not yet understood. It would be like finding by chance a reading key capable of decoding the entire religious history of a people, simply because such a key, which was not finalized to this, is occasionally made available and its applicability proved, proving prodigiously, but unintentionally suitable, to provide an unexpected and unseen opening.

Thus one grasps the importance that the entire First Testament, and the profound unitary coherence that innervates it, possess to understand the Christian message, centered on the mystery of the Incarnation, passion, death and resurrection of Jesus Christ. The First Testament represents in a certain way "the condition of possibility" of the Resurrection. If, in the face of the apostles' claim to preach Jesus Christ the fulfillment of the Scriptures, who died for our sins and rose from the dead, human reason intends to react by formally denying and rejecting the credibility of such an event, such a rejection, if it really wants to maintain rational coherence, should lead to denying and rejecting the entire religious experience of Israel, with all the events and testimonies that accompanied it. Here we need to understand even better the need for that specification that the early Christian faith intended from the beginning to affix to his confession of the Risen One: "he is risen according to the Scriptures". If reason were to refrain from making judgements about the truth of the Resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth and limit itself to recording the *mere historicum of* the empty tomb, it would inevitably have to conclude that faith in the Resurrection is or falls not only with the credibility of the witnesses of the Risen One, but also with the credibility of the whole Revelation of which, ultimately, the Resurrection is the key to its reading and fulfilment.

##### 5.7.5 The absence of public appearances and the logic of communicating that Jesus Christ is risen
Finally, reason raises a final, severe question: why does the historical dimension of Jesus' resurrection, on which the fate of all humanity depends, end in the experience of a small group of disciples? Why did Jesus not return to claim his divinity before his adversaries, at least before some of them? Why among those who bear witness to the divinity and the historicity of the event are only his followers? If Saul of Tarsus had seen the Risen One at a meeting in Jerusalem and not only heard his voice on the road to Damascus, would his testimony not have been more credible? What extraordinary responsibility is placed on the shoulders of these women and these few men, what immense weight is placed on them, to the point of being the only people on whom the orientation of the whole of history will depend? In fact, there is an intimate contrast between the private dimension of the Risen One's experiences and the public, universal dimension entrusted to the preaching of his disciples.80 Already the rationalist Samuel Reimarus, now in modern times, considered the absence of public apparitions sufficient to refute Christianity, because it was incompatible with the mission that Jesus had come to bring into the world.

##### The value of the experience of selected witnesses
The reason of the believer, who has already formulated his faith in the Risen Christ on the basis of the credibility of witnesses and other reasons he has judged reasonable, is generally not disturbed by this apparent contrast but strives to understand, in the light of the unfolding of the facts, what the teaching contained therein is. In essence, the believer approaches the dynamics of the Christian proclamation of the Resurrection with the attitude of those who wish to understand its intimate dynamics. The latter is almost immediately recognized in the logic of witness and in the personalist dimension of the content announced, as we see it paradigmatically expressed in Peter's discourse in the house of the pagan Cornelius (cf. *Acts* 10:37-42). One wonders, however, if there are arguments, valid even *for those who do not yet believe*, capable of showing the reasonableness of such a strategy.

It should first be noted that the announcement of the Risen One, although originating within a limited circle of selected witnesses, does not correspond to a private "revelation", following the model of the Gnostic revelations that a small group of followers receives and transmits by carefully selecting their recipients. The announcement of the resurrection has a profoundly different logic. In the preaching of the Apostles, in fact, a parallel seems to be established between the public scope of the death that preceded it and the ray, in a certain way equally public, to which the message of the Resurrection is now addressed. Unlike Gnosis, the message to be proclaimed is not directed to the elect or the perfect, but to everyone, because "God does not prefer people", because "he who fears him and practises justice, whatever people he belongs to, is accepted by him": he, who is "the Lord of all", delivered a saving message to "proclaim to the people" (cf. *Acts* 10:34-42).

A second element to consider is that the announcement of the Risen One is an "event of revelation" and therefore operates within a logic that is proper to it, in a certain way similar to that of miracles. As manifested in the public life of Jesus, the recognition of the latter requires openness to the gift of faith, dispositions of humility and listening. Quite unlike the spectacular scene in the apocryphal *Gospel of Peter*, which spoke of crowds rushing from Jerusalem to see Jesus coming out of the tomb in a scene meticulously described by the editor, the canonical gospels show the unwillingness of the Risen One to satisfy general and unconditional apparitions. This does not surprise the believing conscience so much, because it knows that the Risen One is the exalted Jesus at the right hand of the Father, somehow removed from a "public" vision: as Origen already pointed out to Celso, to recognize him one needs humility and openness to the gift of faith.81 Jesus Christ does not seek spectacle, but the conversion of hearts.

A third consideration, finally, points out that the small number of privileged witnesses inverses the realism of the apparitions themselves, which, if shared in a general way and proclaimed by all, would, after all, have shown that anyone, reflecting on what happened (empty tomb and diffusion of the first announcement), could in short "see" Jesus as the fruit of a subjective spiritual experience or an easily shared reflection on the fulfillment of prophecies. The difficulty that the apparitions were reserved for a chosen few, with the consequent suspicion of infallibility, could thus even be overturned, becoming an argument in favour of a limited but objective nucleus of the Easter experience, against its subjective origin and, for this reason, of a wider scope.

##### The content and logic of the transmission of the proclamation of the Risen Jesus-Christ
But what is the content of this saving message and what are the needs connected to it? A careful consideration of this content, centred on the recognition of a personal love, that of a life offered in sacrifice for love, and on a life praxis that must go as far as the gift of self, easily shows that such a message could propagate only through a personal and free adhesion, that is, according to the typical dynamic of an interpersonal relationship that is born of love and moves to love. Since Revelation is founded as a whole on the categories of witness and freedom, the freedom of God who self-communicates his mystery according to the vital flow of a reciprocal witness which binds the Son to the Father and the Spirit to the Son, it seems logical that the transmission of Revelation should also take place on the prolongation of the same flow of witness and should be welcomed in freedom.

Now, only the inter-personal message, sustained by the testimony of a life practice that can only be perceived in the reciprocal frequentation - *cor ad cor loquitur*, as the motto chosen by Card. J.H. Newman - is able to realize such a transmission. An announcement that would simultaneously reach a large number of people, but would not be able to *present the witness who proclaims it,* making them grasp its personal credibility, nor would it be able to *gather the commitment of personal freedom,* because received anonymously, would not be adequate to convey the Christian proposal.82 This is why, during his public life, Jesus of Nazareth did not want to limit himself to making speeches to the crowds, but dedicated considerable energy to the formation of a group of disciples and then of apostles who would attend him closely, who would understand the logic of the Kingdom through his daily behavior and who would listen to all the demands of his following. And this is the reason why these very people, "those who had eaten and drank with him", those to whom he had given his body and blood in food and drink, those to whom he wanted to wash his feet and announce the redeeming sense of his *kénosis,* were also the witnesses of the apparitions of the Risen One and of the experiences that followed them. Among these witnesses are also the women who, although not called to be part of the group of the Twelve, were able to attend Jesus, accompanying him more closely and listening to his voice in a more familiar way, experiencing the credibility of his person and the sincerity of his dedication.

*[Embedded Image]*
 J.H. Newman, because the Risen One appeared to only a few chosen disciples...

From the logic just outlined not only does the reasonableness of the absence of multitudinous and public apparitions of the Risen One descend, but also important consequences for the Christian apostolate and for evangelization. As much as we must reasonably rely - as is now the secular tradition of the Church - on forms of public diffusion capable of reaching and involving an increasing number of men and women of every place and culture, the ways in which Christianity spreads must always allow us to experience an *authentic* relationship with the witness, to recognize his credibility, to listen to his proclamation according to a style and logic capable of establishing a *personal relationship*. Even on the part of the recipient of the proclamation there must always be the conditions for understanding the *personal commitment* linked to the Christian proposal, a proposal that cannot be listened to anonymously, as one of many, but must be allowed to fall, and thus embodied, in one's own experience.

If the Christian message is a message that demands life and promises life, then it can only be proclaimed by appealing to the depths of personal being and can only be made credible by those who demonstrate, in a sincere and authentic personal relationship, that they have already given their lives for Christ. One understands then why one of the principal challenges of contemporary evangelization lies in achieving a true and proper *inculturation of faith* in the *culture of the mass media*.83 In other words, it is necessary to study and implement forms and ways of reproducing and conveying, in an ever wider dissemination, the dimension and logic proper to personal relationships and personal witness, which remain indispensable both for the proclamation of the Risen Christ and for the proposal of his following in all its radicality and coherence.

There is a strong need today to speak of the risen Christ. It is necessary to confront the interlocutor with the fact that, in history, this is a unique and singular event, indeed it is the only true *novelty*, the "good *news*". And it is also necessary to confront the interlocutor with the fact that the very existence of the Church demands an explanation, which can only be traced back to the Resurrection of Jesus. However, to move to faith the action of grace is necessary, because faith in the risen Christ is not the conclusion of an argument. There is therefore a need for humility: it must be recognized that we do not possess the reasons for our life and death. To those who know how to recognize themselves in this way, in need of salvation in body and spirit, God does not deny the gift of faith.

#### 5.8 The Church, witness to the charity of Jesus Christ and his Resurrection
As we saw earlier at the beginning of the discussion on the credibility of Revelation, Christ and the Church are indicated by the Second Vatican Council as signs of salvation placed at the basis of every understanding of the credibility of Christianity, to be then brought back to a single sign, that of Christ-present-in-the-Church. In this section we will try to examine more closely how and according to which categories, the Church manifests the credibility of Revelation and how this has been understood in the historical passage. Contemporary Fundamental Theology seeks to develop, in this regard, an important basic strategy: to show in a convincing and well-founded way why the interlocutor, placing himself before the Church, is actually placing himself before Jesus of Nazareth. By taking a stand before the word that the Church preaches, contemporary man is taking a stand before the divine claim of Jesus Christ and the trials that he gives to the world to manifest himself as sent by the Father.84

##### 5.8.1 Signs made by the apostles and signs in the Church according to the New Testament
The NT speaks of "signs" that accompany the preaching of the apostles. The second closing of Mark's Gospel contains a concise and symbolic list: "These are the signs that will accompany those who believe: in my name they will drive out demons, they will speak new tongues, they will take snakes in their hands, and if they drink any poison, it will not harm them; they will lay their hands on the sick and they will heal them. (Mc16.17-18). We find similar signs associated with the evangelizing work of the disciples before Jesus' resurrection (cf. *Lk* 10:19). From the reading of the *Acts it* emerges that alongside the "fulfillment of prophecies", the credibility of the message announced also seems to be entrusted to "signs and wonders" (gr. τε τέρατα καὶ σημεῖα) which accompany the preaching. It is affirmed, in fact, that "a sense of fear was in all and wonders and signs came about through the work of the apostles" (*Acts* 2:43). It is said of Paul and Barnabas that the Lord "bore witness to the preaching of his grace and granted that signs and wonders were done by their hands" (*Acts* 14:3) and that the Twelve listened with interest to the wonders done by them among the Gentiles (cf. *Acts* 15:12). Simon the Wizard was attracted by the wonders performed by Philip when he proclaimed the Gospel (cf. *Acts* 8:13). In symbolic continuity with the promise made in Mark, the people of Malta are struck by Paul's invulnerability to the venom of a serpent and thus change their opinion of him (cf. *Acts* 28:6). Among these wonders there are certainly miracles (cf. *Acts* 3:1-10; 9:36-41; 20:7-12), but it would be reductive to think that the signs of the Apostles are exhausted only to them.

The preaching of the Apostles is accompanied by a deep feeling of respect and trust on the part of those who listen to them and see them working. Jews and Greeks, believers and pagans, appreciate their solidarity, frankness and coherence of life, to the point that the first disciples lived "enjoying the favour of all the people" (*Acts* 2:47; cf. also *Acts* 4:13-14; 4:33; 16:28-30; 26:28-32). Christian practice, that is, the virtuous behaviour of believers, is presented in the letters of the Apostles as a sign capable of moving others towards the faith, thus becoming a condition that will soon become one of the main reasons for the credibility of the proclamation: the witness of life. According to the programme of Christian life presented by the Petrine catechesis to catechumens, those who see good works practiced by Christians will thus come to recognize and praise God (cf. *1 Pet* 2:12); pagan husbands will be won over to the faith through the conduct of Christian wives (cf. *1 Pet* 3:1-2), public authorities by those who act as good subjects (cf. *1 Pet* 2:13-17). The numerous exhortations to the practice of the virtues contained in the parenetic sections of practically all the Pauline letters, although they appear to be addressed *primarily* to the brothers in order to keep the community united and vibrant, undoubtedly manifest an implicit evangelizing projection, as shown by the recommendation to overcome evil with good (cf. *Rom* 12:21; cf. also *1Pt* 3:9), which could not be reductively interpreted as a mere invitation to overcome internal disputes, even if not absent.

In essence, even if we do not explicitly find in the NT the idea of sign or reason for credibility associated with the Church, there is no doubt that all the premises exist. Every single Christian is himself a sign of the faith he professes, that is, a sign of Christ. The life of Christ is reproduced in him and, therefore, he shows it through his words and works. One can rightly say of the Christian that he presents himself on the scene of the world as *alter Christus, ipse Christus*.85

##### 5.8.2 The Church as a reason for the credibility of the Christian message in the Fathers of the Church
In addition to the well-known arguments based on the fulfilment of the Scriptures, the miracles of Jesus of Nazareth and the excellence of Christian doctrine, there are also precise references in the works of the Fathers to the value that witness, holiness of life and martyrdom possess for the spreading of the Gospel in the pagan world. Even if some "proofs" insist more on the contents of the doctrine believed (prophecies, miracles, the greatness of evangelical morality), and others concern Christians more directly as credible subjects, in speaking of the Church the two perspectives seem to merge: in continuity with what we read in the *Acts of the Apostles,* also for the Fathers the Church is the Word that spreads, it is the Gospel itself, it is the whole of Christians who bear witness to it with their lives. Its rapid expansion, capable of reaching and spiritually conquering distant peoples and different cultures, is presented by the Fathers as proof of its divine origin and of the presence in it of a Truth to which nations gradually open themselves. The Church is a sign that refers to God, to the Spirit who enlivens her, and it is a reason for credibility - albeit with different purposes - both for those who are already within her and for those who observe her life and virtues in amazement.

The considerations proposed by the Fathers of the Church gradually bring into focus the notes of *catholicity*, *apostolicity* and *unity*. The proof of the geographical extension of the Church - which Augustine will indicate as *miraculum magnum*86 - is directed above all to those who still do not believe, while the characteristics of catholicity and apostolicity, as notes identifying the "true" Church of Jesus Christ, are used above all to unmask internal divisions and bring heretics back to orthodoxy. For Ottato di Milevi, the adjective "Catholic" indicates both harmony with reason and truth, as well as its universal spread (*ecclesia catholica, quod sit rationabilis et ubique diffusa*).87 On the Church as a motive for faith in Jesus Christ, consider a text from Arnobius of Sicca:

*[Embedded Image]*
 Arnobius of Sicca, *Adversus nationes*, II, 5

Further proof of the divinity of the Church and the credibility that is due to it is, for the Fathers, the excellence of the virtues it teaches, when compared to the vices and corruption of the pagans. In his reply to Celsus, Origen uses as proof of the truth professed by the Church the moral life of Christian communities and those who govern them, emphasizing their difference from the assemblies of political and civil life, thus bringing out the superior dignity of the Christian message.88 With an image reminiscent of the passage of *Is* 11:12 used by the two Vatican Councils concerning the "sign" of the Church, Origen maintains that the prophetic contents which the Catholic Church proclaims outweigh all human doctrines in wisdom and make it a dwelling place for all visible, elevated to the heights.89

The sign in question concerns not only the objective goodness of the virtues practiced by Christians, but the way in which the apostolic preaching has been able to change the moral customs of dissolute and superstitious peoples, inspiring their conversion to truth and goodness. Irenaeus of Lyons points out that the messianic era of universal pacification is already present in the Church of Christ, observing how barbarian and cruel peoples, after the knowledge of the Saviour, now live justice and peace.90

Once lovers of lust, now we are eager only for chastity; once devoted to the magical arts, now we are consecrated to the good and begotten God; yearning more than any other for the means to achieve riches and possessions, now, bringing into community what we possess, we share it with those in need. We hated and killed one another, and did not have common meals with those who were not of the same race because of our customs; now, after the manifestation of Christ, we all have the same kind of life, and we pray for our enemies.91

Present in the background of the whole Augustinian treatment of the *City of God*, the idea that the improvement of customs and the virtuous renewal of humanity are a sign that speaks in favour of the divine origin of the Church and her message of salvation is also expounded by Eusebius of Caesarea in *The Gospel Preparation*. The Church "does not derive from a human initiative, but, in fact, from the foreknowledge of God"; the new way of life introduced by Jesus, Eusebius maintains, "I believe it is a great proof of the divine and mysterious power of our Saviour".92

*[Embedded Image]*
 The customs of the barbarians have changed because of Christianity.

Some of the Fathers' considerations contain an undoubted rhetorical dimension, but their position is less naive than we think. Christianity was in fact forging, together with Greek culture and Roman law, the social progress of the countries of Europe and the Mediterranean basin, in order to share its fruits later with the peoples who would receive its influence. The Christian way of life probably did not involve large masses of men and women, but nevertheless it was objectively experienced in the living communities that embodied the Gospel with fidelity and radicalism, becoming ferment for the whole Church. These are the communities which, in a natural and unspectacular way, continue today to show the world, more than twenty centuries after the resurrection of Jesus, what the Apologists of the first three centuries illustrated to emperors and rulers. As insuperably described by the author of the well-known *Letter to Diognetus* - which still today continues to move us - these testimonies were not only aimed at defending the Christian community from unjust outrages and illegal persecution, but were also intended to attract non-believers to faith in Jesus Christ, through the appeal that this way of life exercised, and still exercises, over all.

For the Fathers, the sign that the Church is showing the world to accept the preached Word does not consist of miracles of power over nature or physical health: it mainly concerns the miracle of Christian life. Although that kind of miracles will not be lacking either, the patristic tradition called for recognition in the Church of the spiritual miracles of conversion and the promotion of the human being, rereading in this sense the missionary mandates given by Jesus to the apostles (cf. *Mk* 16:17). In line with the invitation of Jesus taken up by John, ""Come and see": they went and saw where he dwelt" (*Jn* 1:39), and with the invitation addressed by Philip to Nathanael: "Come and see" (*Jn* 1:48), proof of the credibility of the word announced was and is offered by the life of the Church, together with the very content of the doctrine preached. In this way the Christians of the early centuries defended themselves against the atrocious rumours circulating about them, contrasting with the example, loyalty and forgiveness, the accusations of those who, ignoring their lives, slandered them.

##### 5.8.3 The classical treatment of *viae historica*, *notarum* and *empirica*: the evolution of their understanding in contemporary times
As is well known, classical Apologetics had developed three "ways" to show the divine authority of the Catholic Church and, therefore, the credibility of its preaching. **The *via historica* sought to establish continuity between the Catholic Church and the apostolic community founded by Jesus Christ, thus bringing the mission and authority of the Church back to the mandate and word of its Founder**. It originated with the intention of combating the first heresies, with the commitment to show **which, among the different traditions that demanded saving authority**, would restore its historical origin to the primitive Christian community governed by the Apostles. Therefore, **from its first systematic expressions, the *via historica* was associated with the mark of the Church's *apostolicity****,* as in the *Adversus haereses* of Irenaeus of Lyons. **Beginning with the Council of Trent, the *via historica* was used primarily to underline the foundational will of Jesus Christ with regard to the establishment of the Petrine primacy**.

The *Via Notarum* intended to deepen the study of "characteristic marks" of the true Church to **use them as criteria for its recognition**. The systematization of this *path was to* take place especially in the 16th century, after the separation made by the followers of the Reformation, also because of the theological-intellectual climate present at the time, and which was instead still partly absent at the time of the Orthodox schism. The progressive proposal of multiple marks then gradually **converged towards the indication of the four marks present in the Niceno-Constantinopolitan Symbol, which professes faith in the one, holy, catholic and apostolic Church**. The method of using the marks also underwent a certain evolution: first a biblical-patristic foundation was sought in the 16th-17th centuries, then a largely empirical demonstration in the 18th and 19th centuries; the notes acquired romantic tones at the end of the 19th century, especially when commenting on the universal expansion of the Church, its unity and its spiritual fruitfulness.93

The *via empirica* will be systematized by Vatican Council I, especially thanks to the elaboration of Card. Victor-Auguste-Isidore Deschamps (1810-1883), Bishop of Malines, promoter of the perspective according to which the Church**, as a "banner raised in the midst of the nations", would show by itself, almost empirically, the credibility and the divine origin of Christian revelation from which it originates**. Its apologetic use will come to **present the Church as a "moral miracle" and will be affirmed in modern times especially with the manual work of Réginald Garrigou-Lagrange** (*De Revelatione*, 1918) and Hermann Dieckmann (*De Ecclesia,* 1925; De *Revelatione christiana,* 1930). Of the empirical path, as we have seen, there are important precedents in patristic works, especially in **Irenaeus, Tertullian, Origen and Augustine. In these authors it converges with the exposition of the marks of the universality and holiness of the Church, being almost indistinguishable from them**. After all, even in the treatise Apologetica classica tre *viae* could not be exposed in an analytical and independent way, but **they were linked together: the *via notarum* was in fact based on a certain historical analysis, and then inevitably referred to an empirical verification.**

Since the Second Vatican Council, fundamental theology has begun to consider both *notae* and *viae as a whole,* without ever separating them from the understanding of the Church as a sign of Christ and sacrament of salvation. The methodology needed to highlight them cannot, therefore, be only historical-positive, but must be properly theological, and therefore biblical-dogmatic. For example, when fundamental theology reflects on the *historical path* and studies the origin of the Church, it approaches this origin not only as if it were a temporal event, but seeking its theological roots, that is, understanding the Church as a salvific-sacramental mystery.

The understanding of *notae* by contemporary theology cannot fail to point out two clarifications, which show some limitations of the classical approach:

1. **the *notae* will find their full development, and therefore their full visibility, only in an eschatological perspective, because in the present they remain veiled, as in chiaroscuro**;

1. **not a few of the contents associated with the four marks of the Symbol, such as holiness and also some aspects of apostolicity, are also shared by non-Catholic Christian denominations.**

**With regard to this last clarification, it is significantly stated in *Lumen Gentium* that "the only Church of Christ, which in the symbol we profess to be one, holy, Catholic and apostolic [...] constituted in this world and organized as a society, subsists (*subsistit*) in the Catholic Church, governed by the successor of Peter and the bishops in communion with him, even though outside its visible organism there are many elements of sanctification and truth, which, as gifts proper to the Church of Christ, push towards Catholic unity"** (n. 8). **Therefore, the *notae* that identify the one, true Church of Christ exist in the Catholic Church, but they are not its exclusive prerogative, since outside its visible boundaries such notes can still be found in part. As for unity, it also seems to be an objective, and no longer (only) an attribute of the Catholic Church**.

The uncertainty with which post-conciliar theology found itself having to deal with the *via notarum* is well illustrated by the position of René Latourelle himself who, if on the one hand, does not hesitate to repropose the idea of the Church as a "moral miracle",94 on the other hand, he cannot fail to recognize that "it is certainly impossible to speak to contemporary man of admirable expansion, of eminent holiness, of inexhaustible fruitfulness, of Catholic unity, of the invited stability of the Church without immediately provoking an uncontrollable allergy".95

Also because of this, the *empirical way* cannot be presented today, in a simplistic way, as the ostentation of an evident sign in itself. If in the Second Vatican Council the Church continues to possess the value of a "sign" this happens, as already observed, because she is a "sign of Christ". In this sense, the empirical approach *is oriented towards the experience of the person of Jesus Christ* and the sublimity of his doctrine, an experience certainly mediated by time but which evangelical proclamation and witness are called to reproduce in the life of those who hear the word. In essence, the credibility of faith is still the credibility of the person of Jesus Christ. **When, on the other hand, the empirical approach is directed towards the Church, it reflects today on the divine-human nature of her mission, evaluated against the chiaroscuro background of her real history, and thus also in its dimensions of "paradox", directing the interlocutor to recognize the value of witness, which is expressed especially in service to every man and to the whole man, in charity.** The contemporary understanding of what in the past was called the "empirical way", presents in essence a sensitive evolution, pointing towards the experience of "witness", Christian and ecclesial, a category which today occupies a fundamental role in the economy of credibility.

In relation to the empirical way, S. Pié-Ninot offers a systematization of the different theological-fundamental proposals by organizing them in four main areas:

1. **the "path of the sociology of the institution", which captures in the reality of the institution (understood in its sacramental as well as historical-visible aspects), the identifying sign of the Church;**

1. **the "self-explanatory way", which takes up the suggestions of the traditional empirical way, but reinterpreted in the light of the mystery-paradossus relationship;**

1. **the "hagiophanic way", that is, the note of the holiness of the Church as she offers herself to experience and questions the interlocutor;**

1. **and finally the "significant way", which privileges, within the sacramental perspective, those signs that acquire a more easily accessible meaning, such as signs of charity, service, dialogue, ecumenism, love, forgiveness.****96**

##### 5.8.4 Continuity of the Christian event and the transmission of its saving message in the Catholic Church
**Strictly speaking, neither the *historicity of the Gospel* message nor the *continuity* between the proclamation of the Gospel by the Church and the original content of Christ's preaching represent a reason for the credibility of the word proclaimed.** The original and founding reason for this credibility is still Jesus Christ, of whom the Church is a sign. However, a demonstration of this historicity and continuity **retain all their importance because they offer the conditions for the interlocutor to be confronted with the living experience of Christ and to be called with personal responsibility to consider the credibility of the person of Christ himself**. What is announced to man today, and what he is called to decide, is not an abstract idea, but a living Word. Contemporary theology proposes to demonstrate, according to a renewed *historical path*, that this Word has remained unchanged and alive in the Church, coming to us as the Word of Christ-present in the Church.

##### Continuity between the community around Jesus of Nazareth and the Catholic Church
The religious movement that originated around Jesus of Nazareth, who confessed his divinity and proclaimed his resurrection from the dead, is based on a series of events that took place in Jerusalem at the time of the Roman Emperor Tiberius. If we exclude the New Testament sources, the historical documents that speak of these origins, as noted above, are relatively few, but significant. Nevertheless, the large number of qualified witnesses who, starting from the decades immediately following those events, have left historical-documental traces of their direct connection with the person and doctrine of Jesus (Apostolic Fathers, authors and Christian martyrs of the first and second centuries), as well as the indirect effects that the life of believers in Christ in these early decades has given to history (catacombs and burials, epigraphs, places of worship, etc.), are sufficient to establish with certainty the rootedness of the Christian religion in precise founding events of historical value.97

The task of Fundamental Theology is not so much to investigate these roots further as to provide elements of reason in favour of the fact that the Gospel message proclaimed by the early Christian community is really at the origin of the Church's mission as we know it, showing itself in continuity with it. The theme in question is not that, typical of classical apologetics, of showing which of today's Christian confessions is linked to the true Church founded by Jesus Christ, but, more simply, of illustrating that, at least with regard to the Catholic Church, such a link exists with certainty and is available to the judgement of reason. The elements of continuity, from the apostolic epoch up to the present day, which are considered to be conclusive in this regard are:

- continuity in reading the same Scripture and preaching the same Gospel;
- continuity in the celebration of the Eucharistic sacrifice, whose centrality and essence remain unchanged;
- continuity in the virtues required for the *sequela Christi*, in particular through the testimony of martyrdom;
- continuity based on the laying on of hands in the sacrament of orders.

With regard to the theme of continuity between the primitive Christian community, repository of the teachings and mission received from Jesus Christ, and the Catholic Church as it has historically configured and developed over two millennia, the reflections proposed by John Henry Newman in his work The *Development of Christian Doctrine* (1845) are of particular interest. The Oxford theologian affirms that the different historical expressions (institutions, principles, specific teachings, etc.) manifested by a movement of ideas or a doctrine in the course of its own temporal development, when this process is guided by the indications and corrections coming from those who share and embody its purpose, are the result of the progressive clarification of what is germinally contained in the initial idea.98 One of the proofs that what is progressively made explicit does not corrupt the doctrine but represents its homogeneous development, would then lie in the permanence of the identity of the subject of this development: he continues to be himself and does not change his identity, because developments and enrichment do not weaken him, but rather strengthen him. Newman thinks primarily of the Church and applies his reflections to what the Church, in the course of time, has had to accomplish, establish, decide, specify, giving rise to subsequent determinations which, not having made it disappear, but on the contrary having made it stable and robust in its mission and identity, could not but be implicitly present in its original charism and mandate, as delivered by its Founder.99

##### The question of the origin and foundation of the Church
**The idea that it was possible to explain the reality of the Church by going back to a "foundation" by Jesus Christ was born in modern times**. Patristic thought reflected more willingly on its mystical-sacramental "origin", using images of the open side of Jesus on the cross (with its Old Testament parallel of the origin of Eve, mother of the living, from the rib of Adam) and, above all, the action of the Spirit on the day of Pentecost. The medieval era developed the understanding of the foundation of the Church from a juridical perspective, trying to justify/found the jurisdictional authority of the Church based on its divine origin. The Protestant Reformation and the different ecclesiological conception of which it became the spokesman, led to the devaluation of the reality of the Church as an *institution,* making the question of its explicit foundation lose interest. For its part, **Tridentine and post-Tridentine apologetics moved along the opposite side, reinforcing the value of the Church's visible-institutional dimension and beginning to link it to a specific reading of some essential biblical passages**.

Rationalist criticism had already advanced at a very early stage, with H.S. Reimarus (1694-1768), the thesis that the institution of the Church did not respond to the will of Jesus, whose aim would be the restoration of the Kingdom of David, but would be achieved by his followers once the Master's failure was recognized. In the contingency of the modernist crisis and the spread of the exegesis of liberal theology, and in reaction to the accusation that the Church was "a birth of collective consciousness" (*Pascendi*, DH 3492), the Catholic *Magisterium* considered it necessary to state explicitly, in the anti-modernist oath *Sacrorum antistitum* (1910), that "the Church, guardian and teacher of the revealed word, was instituted directly and immediately (*proxime ac directo institutam*) by the same true and historical Christ, while He lived among us". (DH 3540; see also 3452). Before that time, Vatican I had already affirmed that Jesus Christ "decided to build (*build decrevit*) the holy Church" (DH 3050) and Leo XIII had linked the note of unity of the Church to the free will of Jesus Christ who, in founding it, "speaks only of a Church, which He calls 'His'". (*Satis cognitum*, see DH 3302-3303).

With regard to the relationship between Jesus Christ and the Church, biblical exegesis has gone through several phases since the end of the 19th century. The modernist theses and liberal exegesis relegated the Church to a post-Easter creation without any special connection with the preaching of the historical Jesus (A. von Harnack, A. Sabatier, G. Tyrrell, A. Loisy, and in part up to the first Bultmann); a subsequent reading, definable as "eschatological interpretation", recognized the link existing between the preaching of the Church-Kingdom of God and the person of Jesus, but indicated in the Church a reality projected to the end of time, thus devaluing the institutional aspects that pretended to root it in historical time (A. Nygren, F.J. Leenhardt, up to J. Jeremias); the attempt to distinguish a charismatic-spiritual Church, originated in Jesus and preached by Paul, from an institutional Church, originated at the time of the Synoptics, limited only to the former its link with the doctrine of the Kingdom proclaimed by Jesus (E. Käsemann); the Catholic exegesis at the turn of the Second Vatican Council, while recognizing the relevant weight of the post-Easter events in the genesis of the Church, tried to show its continuity with the preaching and gestures of Jesus (R. Schnackenburg, A. Vögtle), initiating the perspective that would later be known as "implicit ecclesiology" (W. Trilling).

A rather influential thesis was that sustained by G. Lohfink, according to which the preaching of Jesus had as its sole purpose that of "reuniting Israel", that is, reawakening its faith in a climate of conversion and spiritual worship oriented towards the realization of its universal mission.100 At first glance there would be no lack of elements to support such a perspective. The Apostles, in fact, initially attended the temple in Jerusalem teaching the doctrine of Jesus, and Paul, in practically all his missionary journeys, began his mission by preaching in the synagogues, or in any case by going to the Jewish communities where they were present. Starting from the worsening rejection of the Jews, apostolic preaching would instead take on the character of the convocation of a "new" Israel.
