A further exegetical observation would point out that the term *ekklesía* does not appear in Luke's Gospel (it will appear only three times in the two verses of *Mt* 16,18 and *Mt* 18,17), but only in the *Acts,* and that in the NT the expression *Church of Christ* rarely appears, while it seems affirmed, in continuity with the First Testament, that of the *Church of God*. This would demonstrate the only late maturation of the idea of an *Ekklesía* different from the "assembly of the people of God" (eb.: *qehal Jahvè*), which was its usual Old Testament meaning.

However, there are equally significant biblical data that seem to reduce the scope of the previous arguments. The primitive preaching of the Apostles to the Gentiles, for example, would demonstrate the universal and radically new breath of the mission received from Jesus, while the fact that in the NT it is affirmed in many places that the Church is *the Body of Christ, would be* enough to affirm that she *is as* much *Christ's as* her own Body (cf. *Col* 1:24: *Eph* 1:22-23). To this should be added the consideration that the mission of Jesus *within Israel and for Israel should* not be seen in opposition to the universal mission of the Church as she appears in the post-Easter period, but that she finds in that her reason for being. The logic of salvation history has been precisely that of realizing, through the formation of a people and the covenant with it, a blessing and a salvation directed to all peoples, a logic which is reflected in the formation, within Israel, of the College of the Twelve and the first discipleship of the Lord. It is in this ecclesial community that Israel renews its universal consciousness and destination, even though the preaching of the Gospel of Christ, not meeting with the Chosen People the hoped-for response, ended up by maturing in the community of believers in Christ the awareness that it was itself the new Israel and the new People of God, thus fulfilling the events and developments that the Word of God had already delivered to the Prophets and the foretold Jesus himself.

Summarizing the path of the exegetical positions seen so far, one can affirm the convenience, in general, of not seeking specific or explicit foundational events, framing instead the theme within a greater historical contextualization. For its part, fundamental and dogmatic theology cannot but focus its attention on words and events in the life of Jesus which, even if they do not speak of foundation, manifest the birth of a new and more radical mission conferred by Christ on the apostles and his disciples, a manifestation, in turn, of the awareness of the radical newness of his messianic mission as Son sent by the Father. These could be summarized as follows:

- the many parallels between the universal mission of the people of Israel and the mission entrusted by Jesus to his disciples, especially the Twelve;
- the role played by the Twelve during public preaching and the specific teachings of Jesus, in particular the role played by Simon-Pietro as proof of the birth of a new community united by faith in Jesus, Son of God;
- the messianic consciousness and the divine sonship of Jesus, capable of inaugurating a discontinuity so radical, even in the continuity of Israel's mission, that it required a subject to whom to entrust a *new* economy, as new was the message delivered to the world;
- the institution of the Eucharist, as the culmination and summary of a new saving economy that now makes the People of God the Body of Christ, building a new community *which is the Church.*101

Even within the Old Testament categories (Easter dinner, prayer in the temple, call to the conversion of Israel), Jesus calls and forms a group of disciples to be witnesses of how a *new covenant, a* universal openness to all peoples, of which he is now himself and no longer Israel, the main author and mediator, is being realized in him, with his redeeming sacrifice.

**In the light of the previous debate, contemporary theology has however matured the idea that the notion of "foundation", coming from a substantially juridical-institutional lexicon, is probably not the most adequate to understand the origin of the Church, that is, ultimately, its relationship with Christ.** Moreover, the study of the origin of the Church from Jesus Christ must not separate what was accomplished or arranged by Him during His public life from what was accomplished at His Easter and in the events that followed it, nor can it lose the necessary Trinitarian breath: at the heart of the origin of the Church is the whole of the Most Holy Trinity, because the mission of the Son necessarily refers back to the Father, and what the Son entrusted to humanity and history could not be accomplished except through the mission of the Spirit. Finally, without prejudice to the peculiarity of the institution of the Eucharist and its fundamental role for the constitution of the Church-Body of Christ, it seems more theologically correct to speak not of a specific formal act, but rather of a set of Christ's acts aimed at the foundation/formation of the Church, through which we can reasonably trace Jesus' intentions regarding the Church and her future. Even more, "the whole work and life of Jesus in a certain way constitutes the root and foundation of the Church, which is like the fruit of his whole existence".102

What was the position of Vatican Council II in this regard? The Council uses the verb "fondare", referring to the Church and its Founder, in various places, without, however, attributing a particular declaratory emphasis to it, leaving preferably that the term "foundation" be referred first to Christ and then to the College of Twelve. Thus *Ad Gentes* affirms that "the Catholic Church was founded as necessary, by God through Jesus Christ" (n. 7; cf. also LG, 14) and *Gaudium et Spes* will say that "the Church, proceeding from the love of the eternal Father, founded in time by Christ the Redeemer, gathered together in the Holy Spirit, has a salvific and eschatological purpose, which cannot be fully attained except in the future world". (n. 40). *Lumen Gentium*, in recalling that "the mystery of the holy Church is manifested in her foundation" (n. 5), proceeds by exposing the fruits of the preaching of Jesus as the next cause of aggregation of discipleship and, therefore, of the Church herself. This causal action is prolonged in apostolic preaching, since "the apostles, preaching the Gospel everywhere, welcomed by the hearers under the action of the Holy Spirit, gather together the universal Church, which the Lord founded on the apostles and built upon Blessed Peter, their head, while Jesus Christ himself is its cornerstone" (n. 19). A different theological breath the Council will show when it speaks of the *origin of* the Church, rather than its *foundation*. The recurring doctrine is that the Church draws its origin from the eternal plan of the Father and from the missions in the time of the Son and the Holy Spirit, according to a dogmatic development which, practically with the same scheme and with few variations, is reproposed in paragraphs 2 to 5 of both the Constitution *Lumen Gentium and* the decree *Ad Gentes*.

Twenty years after the Council declarations, the International Theological Commission has dealt with the theme of the origin of the Church in two documents: *Some Questions of Ecclesiology* (1985) and *The Awareness Jesus Had of Himself* (1986). The first of these devotes a broad discussion to the theme of "The Founding of the Church by Jesus Christ", suggesting the idea that the establishment of the Church by its Founder was a historical process marked by certain preparatory elements, stages and developments.103

The second document sets out his theses on Jesus' self-awareness in four formal propositions; of these, the third is dedicated to the relationship between Jesus and the Church. "In order to carry out his saving mission," it is said, "Jesus wished to gather people together for the Kingdom and call them together around him. To this end Jesus carried out concrete acts whose only possible interpretation, if taken as a whole, is the preparation of the Church which will be definitively constituted at the time of the events of Easter and Pentecost. It is therefore necessary to affirm that Jesus wanted to found the Church".104

##### 5.8.5 The sacramentality of the Church as a sign of transcendence that goes beyond itself
Among the greatest ecclesiological contributions of the Second Vatican Council is that of having presented the Church as a "**universal sacrament of salvation**" (LG, 48; cf. also LG, 1; GS 45), **in close connection with her being a "sign of Christ".** Among the advantages of this formulation is that it **can refer simultaneously to both her dimensions, visible and invisible, human and divine, in analogy to the mystery of the Incarnate Word.**105 In order that the presentation of the Church as a sacrament, and thus as a sign referring to Christ, may be part of a discussion of credibility, it is necessary to specify *what this sign consists of*. This question can be answered by saying that **the Church makes Revelation credible through her holiness and her witness,** but also, as we shall briefly explain in this section, **through her capacity, like every sacrament, to refer beyond herself.** Its sacramental nature can be used as a trace of a transcendence capable of directing a judgment of credibility towards the recognition of Christ and the Spirit present in the Church.

**It is an indicative but not demonstrative sign, attractive but not apodictic**, as is every sacrament in signifying the grace it actually confers. An incomplete but not extrinsic sign, because the Son and the Spirit are really present in her and in her means of salvation. **The Church is capable of signalling the mystery**, directing those who approach her and revealing it to those who hear her message. In this sense, the Church **refers to something and to Someone who transcends her, Someone whom she can continue to point out despite the limitations and the faults of the men who compose her.** The Church can therefore present herself to the world as "**paradox and mystery**".106

The tension between paradox and mystery can also become the right light in which to read a re-proposal of the *notarum way* today. Of the notes that characterize her, the Church possesses the germs and the firstfruits, that is, their presence *in mystery*; she possesses them in a sufficient way to show them to the world, but still insufficient to display them as already accomplished, because of their eschatological tension, which makes them still perceived, in the present time, as a *paradox*.

Some authors - mainly from the perspective of "liberation theology", but also with J. Moltmann and C. Duquoc - have proposed a *dialectical* reading of this tension, interpreting the notes as an expression of a congenital conflict, the resolution of which, however, must be committed in the present; others, like R. Latourelle, have instead underlined the *revelatory* value of the contrast between the human-visible, and therefore limited, dimension of the Church, and a dimension that certainly transcends it, which makes possible the fruits of good and salvation that are realized through it.

The Second Vatican Council also made itself an interpreter of the tension between what the Church is heading towards and inevitably postpones, and what she expresses in the present time: the Church, it is said, "includes sinners in her bosom, holy at the same time and always in need of purification, and unceasingly applies herself to penance and renewal" (LG, 8). **The human condition, and therefore fallible and limited, as well as the very errors of those who make up the Church, are not necessarily an obstacle to the recognition of her divine nature - although in some circumstances they may come to be so - because God's saving action in her is grasped through the presence of signs and fruits which cannot depend totally on her**. To make the Church a credible sign of Revelation, a sign that sends back beyond herself, there are various aspects that contribute to the experience of the interlocutor to whom her appeal is addressed: the Gospel mandate, corroborated by tangible fruits, to spread throughout the earth overcoming all divisions of race and language, reaching every man created in the image and likeness of God; her real concern for every man and for the whole man; the strength with which she manages to overcome every obstacle in order to bear witness to her Founder's love for all men and women. Although she is composed of sinners, the Church proposes herself to the world with the signs of charity, fruitfulness, forgiveness, and unity, postponing the justification of her energy, her permanence, and the holiness of her fruits, to her divine origin.

The Church shows its value as a sign that also refers beyond itself in its constant prophetic ministry. She inevitably also presents herself as a "sign of contradiction".

A reading that emphasized the *sacramental nature of* the Church's sign was suggested even before Vatican II by Henri de Lubac, especially in his ecclesiological essays, *Catholicisme. Les aspects sociaux du dogme* (1938), *Meditation sur l'Eglise (*1953) and *Paradoxe et mystère de l'Eglise* (1967).107 In the wake of the Council's teachings, Y.-M. also contributed. Congar, with the work *Un peuple messianique. L'Eglise, sacrement du salut* (1975).108 **If the Church is a "sacrament," then we cannot stop at it as such, but we must transcend it: it too, like the sign, is annulled in the face of what it means, but nevertheless remains indispensable because it is through it that it draws upon the *res of* which it is *signum*.** The world's judgment is wrong when, admiring the Church for her moral authority, for her care in preserving art and culture, for the promotion of the justice which she spreads, respecting it for its balance and spirituality, she also remains incapable of accessing her true heart, not understanding the profound reason for what she observes. When this happens," observes de Lubac, "the sacramental sign is shattered, becoming proof of the fact that those manifestations previously praised and admired may easily no longer be understood, ending up being denigrated shortly afterwards. In the eyes of those who observe the Church without believing in her divine mission, she remains an inexplicable paradox and all her actions misrepresented. If this is a risk present in non-believers who limit themselves to observing her as they do from outside, it is also a risk for those who, present within her, forget *what* she is a sign of and *who* she is a sign of, or when they speak of her in a self-referential and absolute way, thus dulling her sacramental nature.

*[Embedded Image]*
 H. de Lubac, Without Christ, the Church ceases to be a sign

##### 5.8.6 The credibility of saints and the sign of holiness in the Church
Talking about holiness is, for the Church, a difficult subject, probably the most difficult of all. Yet, she cannot do without it, because Saint is the One for whom and by whom the Church exists, and saints are called to be those who become her children. He who speaks of holiness can immediately see his every word or gesture judged by others, and rightly so. Aware of this tragic seriousness, but also of its fascinating appeal, the Church cannot but speak of holiness. It is the ontological holiness of the Body of Christ of which it is a living mystery and which it makes present on its altars, and it is the holiness to which those who communicate to that Body, of which they are living members, must aspire. For these reasons, beyond the pertinence of its classification as a *note ecclesiae*, the sign of the "holiness of the Church" calls for its specific deepening.109

The first Christians, the NT informs us, were aware that holiness expressed their *separation* from sin but not from the world, their *election* but not a privilege, their *familiarity* with God in Jesus Christ but not the dissolution of a transcendence, the gift of a *divine sonship*, not the fruit of a presumptuous appropriation. Called saints in the Pauline Epistolary (cf. *Rom* 1:7; *1 Cor 1:*2; *2 Cor 1:*1), Christians incorporated into the Church through baptism form a royal priesthood and a holy nation (cf. *1 Pet* 2:9). The will of God for each of them is their personal sanctification (cf. *1Ts* 4,3). Holiness is a sign of belonging, but also the fruit of conduct and virtuous life (cf. *1 Pet* 1:15), it is for every Christian, both past and present, a *gift* and a *task*; yet it is not just any task, but an arduous task, to which grace must meet the sincere commitment of all forces (cf. *Mk* 12:30): the Christian witness is not a hero, but a martyr.

In countries of ancient Christian tradition, especially in the Western world, when the sanctity of the Church is spoken of, it is frequent to hear the objection of those who point out the infidelities, scandals and counter- witnesses of which the baptized are protagonists, even those constituted in responsibility and authority. Faced with this difficulty one usually reacts in two ways, both unsatisfactory: to renounce speaking about holiness, almost forcing one to a sort of collective ecclesiastical humility, or to continue to speak about it, relegating it, however, to a sphere that is only ideal, eschatological.

It is the latter "solution" that presents the most delicate aspects. It has a fund of truth in that holiness certainly admits its fullness and fulfillment only in eschatological terms, in the Kingdom and not on earth, at home and not in the street. However, in the face of the accusations of a public opinion that spreads by defaming (but sometimes also slandering) the errors, abuses and worldly logic of men of the Church, wanting to *take away the credibility of* the Church as a preaching and teaching subject, one often reacts *by detaching holiness from the world*. To those who point out the faults of those who call themselves Christians, they respond by showing the irremediable, but also understandable, weakness of human beings. In doing so one ends up believing that holiness cannot be expressed in social and civil life: one admires the exercise of it in a few idealized examples, perhaps protagonists of a hermit's life, but one does not consider it realistically suitable for a daily life lived in the midst of the world.110

To sustain her credibility in Christ, the Church cannot silence the sign of holiness, nor idealize it to the point of making it disembodied. Instead, she must speak of it again, *showing it realized and eloquent in the life and witness of the saints*.

##### Recognizing the sign of holiness
At the time of indicating how the sign of holiness can be recognized today, contemporary Fundamental Theology suggests some lines of discernment. Salvador Pié-Ninot distinguishes a specific "hagiophanous way" that offers itself to experience and questions the interlocutor, flanking immediately afterwards a "significant" way that privileges, within the sacramental perspective, those signs that acquire a more easily accessible meaning, such as the signs of charity, service, dialogue, ecumenism, love, forgiveness. He reviews the perspectives that have been advanced on this theme by various authors, from Congar to de Lubac, from Rahner to Küng, from Semmelroth to Ratzinger, from von Balthasar to Thils, from Metz to Moltmann.111

A detailed declension is that offered by César Izquierdo, according to which the holiness of the Church is made visible in four aspects:

1. through the mysterious-sacramental dimension of his liturgy, in which both the Church as a whole and the individual faithful manifest to the world the mystery of Christ;

1. through the universal call to holiness, which the Church carries out together with her constant call to conversion, on the basis of the word of her divine Founder;

1. in the lives of the saints, now *at home*, who with the heroism of their virtues gave an example capable of attracting and moving hearts towards faith;

1. thanks to the testimony of believers still *in progress*, a testimony that should be read not only as a coherence of life, but also as a reference to transcendence and the Other.112

The ability to recognize the signs associated with holiness depends on the social, intellectual and experiential context within which one moves. In line with what was pointed out at the beginning of *Gaudium et Spes* - "the joys and hopes, the sorrows and anxieties of today's people, especially the poor and all those who suffer, are also the joys and hopes, the sorrows and anxieties of Christ's disciples" - sharing and loving is, for those who proclaim the Word, the condition of possibility for the sign of holiness to be perceived. Beyond differences in culture, environment and lived experiences, holiness must first of all speak the language of Jesus: compassion, healing, sincere interest, service, altruism, mercy, condescension. Selfless affection and understanding remain the most evident signs for man of all time, especially for contemporary man, wounded by loneliness and fragmentation, yet always eager to give and receive affection.

The doctrine contained in *Lumen Gentium*, reproduced to a great extent in the other documents of the Second Vatican Council, repeatedly mentions the notion of "holiness", that which the Church possesses as a reflection and effective action of her Founder and that to which all her individual members are called, not losing sight of this in realism when commenting on the human condition, which is lacking and limited to believers.113

A sign of holiness is also the "joy", of particular value today for evangelization.114 Following Saint Augustine, who rests in this on Plato, fundamental theology can emphasize its connection with the *classical* subject of the human search for happiness. Every man tends towards happiness and recognizes that it dwells with God: joy is the sign that this happiness has been found and, with it, the place of God.

##### Children of God in the Church of Saints and Martyrs: the role of the saints for the faith and perseverance of God's people
The idea that the encounter with the life of the saints moves hearts to conversion and arouses effective desires of service to the Church has long belonged to the Christian tradition. The life of the Egyptian monk Anthony initiated Augustine's friends to conversion and laid the foundations for his own; the reading of the life of Saint Teresa of Avila was enough for Edith Stein to change the course of her existence; countless men and women discovered faith through the reading of the biography of Saint Theresa of the Child Jesus, and this in lands far removed from the humble convent of Lisieux. In the past as in the present, the encounter with holiness gives credibility to the Gospel, urges more than one to leave unbelief and offers new and deeper reasons for perseverance to those who have already embraced the faith.

It is in particular the encounter with the testimony of martyrdom that produces these fruits. Tertullian's expression "blood of the martyrs, seed of new Christians" is not an abstract aphorism, but conveys a truth capable of crossing the Church of all times, from Jerusalem to Rome, from Carthage to Madrid, from Seoul to Beijing. The history of the Christian martyrs seems to wind through the different eras like countless and diverse streams, whose primordial source lies in the imposing figure of the martyrs/witnesses transmitted to us by the Johannine Apocalypse. The fascination exercised by the saints and martyrs of the first centuries of Christianity is not questioned by anyone; it is not in fact denied by any philosophical argument against the Christian faith, nor attacked by any adversary of the Church.

It is an intangible and ever-living legacy, to which all Christian denominations intend to reconnect, capable of sustaining, on their own, the task of convincing the world of the sincerity of the faith of believers, purifying the Church from any scandal or counter-examination that may have wounded her throughout history. The Church's liturgy affirms and prays with them: "In the witness of faith of your saints you always make your Church fruitful with the creative power of your Spirit, and you give us, your children, *a* sure *sign of your* love.115

The sign of holiness is a sign of *unity in diversity*, a sign of the inexhaustible creative richness of the Spirit which becomes concrete and manifests itself in the plurality of forms and charisms. **The variety of Christian saints and their unconditional adherence to the one Jesus Christ, because different ways of expressing a single *sequela Christi*, help to understand the sacramental nature of the Church, providing light to compose constructively the natural tensions and pluralism present within her.** The faith of believers is confirmed because each one has the guarantee that the talents and qualities received from God, but also the sensitivity and personality that characterize each one, are not disruptive forces but can be composed, with humility and intelligence, in the one Church of Christ. What elsewhere would inevitably lead to originality, autonomy and fragmentation, in the Catholic Church miraculously becomes an expression of beauty and unity. Splendid jewels of which everyone can be glorified, with which one can adorn oneself and see oneself as one's own, because they belong in a certain way to every *Christian*.

The sign of holiness is also *the sign of an attractive humanity*. The saint, precisely because he is fully human, attracts and sends back beyond himself, reproducing in himself the same sacramental dynamic which characterizes the Church as a whole. The true saint, if he is *Christ's*, is not admired as a humanly unattainable model, but as a human being who judges and chooses, loves and fights, loses and wins.116 The exemplariness to which Jesus exhorted his own is not a way of life that separates, but salt that gives flavour, light that illuminates. Paradoxically, the saint belongs as much to Christ as to the world, and if he belongs to the world it is because he has decided to belong to Christ. To turn to them for the first time, or to return to them after a long time, is called *conversion*: in fact, it is precisely the example of the saints and the recognition of their *true humanity* that, with the help of grace, reveals to man the face of Jesus Christ and makes him decide to want to be his own. However real the presence of Jesus Christ is today in the midst of his own and in the Church, this presence is always shrouded in mystery and is offered to our experience only through mediations, albeit sacramental ones. The saint, on the contrary, can present himself to his interlocutor without mediation: we touch him, we speak with him, we experience his smile and understanding, he leans over us with his charity and lifts us up with his strength. The humanity of the witnesses of Jesus Christ is the gateway to the recognition of holiness, and thus to the recognition of God.

**The saints support the credibility of Revelation because they allow those who receive their testimony, believers or non-believers, *to reason and believe with their hearts and intelligence***. The believer sees in them members of his own body, the Body of Christ, whom he trusts; but even the non-believer, when not hindered by **prejudices** or self-sufficiency, can see in the saints men and women who have asked the same questions, shared the same doubts, sought the same answers, and did so in many cases with skills that the subject recognizes, at least for some specific fields, superior to his own. **They are witnesses who can be humanly trusted.** The questions to the Christian faith which may arise from the fields of history, philosophy or science, and which could keep the non-believer from turning to the option of faith, the saints who were historians, philosophers, men of science and men of letters also addressed them and found there an answer, at least sufficient to legitimize the human choice of their Christian faith. Everyone, believers and non-believers alike, can rely on their knowledge, their intellectual honesty, their psychological maturity: the former to find confirmation of their faith when the progress of knowledge and the vicissitudes of life, including intellectual life, will ask them new and more difficult questions; the latter to reduce their objections or even to clear the field of the prejudice that the assent of faith is the result of naivety or is a cause of incompatibility on a rational, philosophical or scientific level.

What does it mean then, in the final analysis, for the dynamic of the faith, to recognize oneself as a child of a Church of saints and martyrs? It means being able to rely on a motherhood on which one depends and which sustains us;117 It means knowing how to benefit from the human and spiritual experience of the saints; it means knowing how to live in communion with them, without ever losing awareness of that ecclesial dimension which must enlighten the life of every baptized person and which represents the true dimension with which the Church, a sign of Christ, must be presented to those who do not yet believe. In responding to God who reveals himself, we are not isolated, but we respond in the Church to a Word which the Church transmits to us. The lack of ecclesial meaning is not a harmless deficiency, which the "adult" Christian can make up for with presumed maturity. Wanting to prescind, through ignorance or conscious choice, from those bonds of communion that a better knowledge of the Church and her witnesses creates, is never without consequences. The Christian, in order to believe in the Risen One and remain faithful, must maintain full awareness of this communion, losing it to Jesus Christ.

##### 5.8.7 Testimony, a constitutive category of Revelation and its credibility
The Christocentric perspective of credibility adopted by contemporary Fundamental Theology highlights the **importance of the witness of life of Christians: the credibility of the Word announced is, in fact, the credibility of Christians and of the Church as a whole.** Their condition as living members of the Mystical Body of Christ leads believers in him to show the world the fruit of their virtues, that is, the sign that the mystery of Christ has left its mark on their lives. To make Christ credible, Christians must present him to the world by reproducing in them the very logic of his resolutive sign, that of love which knows how to forgive and "give its life for its friends" (*Jn* 15:13).

The use of witness as a fundamental category for the study of the credibility of Revelation is not part of a "strategy of proclamation", but is rather dictated by the logic with which the word of God has been addressed to man. Sacred Scripture describes the revelatory activity of the Trinity in the form of reciprocal testimonies: the testimony that the Son gives to the Father, that which the Father gives to the Son, that which the Spirit will give to the Son in all obedience to the Father. As exemplarily stated in the Fourth Gospel, the Apostles and the Church are placed in the flow of this witness to the point of making it one with the proclamation of the Paschal Mystery. They are witnesses of all that Christ did and taught, but they are witnesses above all of what they lived together with him, that is, of the truth of his person. This implies that the Church too, in guarding and transmitting this word, cannot prescind from witness: what she believes, in order to be transmitted, must also be lived. Witness is not a juxtaposed quality, a tactical expedient which can merely condition the outcome of the proclamation; rather, it is an integral part of the proclamation itself: what is proclaimed to the world is a life and not an abstract message; more precisely, the life of Christ present in the Christian, the life of Christ visible and operative in the Church.

Within contemporary Fundamental Theology, **the "way of witness" today collects both the legacy of the *via notarum* and that of the *via empirica***.118 It concerns both external aspects, which are reflected in the public behaviour of the Church and therefore of Christians, and internal aspects, which concern the coherence and unity of life of each believer in Christ.

##### The Church, witness of the risen Christ
If we wanted to ask ourselves what is the main identity that qualifies the Christian as a witness, we would simply have to answer that the Christian and the Church are **witnesses of *the risen Christ***. The credibility that flows from Christian witness is in continuity with the credibility of the resurrection of Jesus Christ. It is because of their faith in the risen Christ that Christians direct their entire behaviour in charity and commit themselves to upholding the truth; it is faith in the risen Christ that they are called to proclaim their *hope* - a fruitful and industrious *expectation* - before the world. If, with regard to its content, witness is substantially centred on charity, its intelligence and hermeneutics can be understood only as an *extension of* the witness of the Resurrection. Apostle, evangelizer, martyr and witness of the risen Christ are terms permeated with one another.

Although the NT makes it clear that the bearers of the proclamation of Jesus' resurrection are primarily the apostles (cf. *Acts* 2:32; 3:15; 4:33), it is equally clear that he who becomes a disciple actually becomes a "witness to the resurrection": even if he did not see Jesus alive, he nevertheless becomes a witness because he learned it from the testimony of others. The flow of witness from Easter Sunday actually reaches the present. The radicalism of Christian conduct, in fact, can be understood *only in the light of faith in the Risen One*: for this reason, anyone who lives the Christian life and virtues, even if no longer in historical contact with the humanity of the Risen One, can be qualified as his witness by right.

The liturgical cycle through which the Church reproposes to her faithful the central mysteries of the faith presents itself as a great proclamation of the Resurrection, prepared by times of waiting and conversion, catechesis, prayer and purification. Everything seems to converge forcefully towards the great proclamation, still shrouded in trepidation and almost incredulous expectation in the Easter Preaching, and then now explicit in the Sequence of Easter Sunday which precedes the proclamation of the Gospel: *Surrexit Dominus vere!*

The announcement is directed to the world, not only to believers, as witnessed by the ancient practice of the primitive Easter liturgy. The presence of the catechumens, from whom those who receive baptism will proceed, are an expression, and in a certain way also a symbol, of all those who are called to become part of the Body of Christ. Although the liturgy of the light of the great Easter Vigil has as its first meaning that of presenting the light of the Paschal candle as a symbol of the Risen One which nourishes the faith of the baptized, each of whom will renew his or her promises with a burning flame, it should also be noted that the passage of light and flame from candle to candle is very well suited to the image of a faith which spreads in a short time, because it is communicated by each baptized person to those around him or her, until it illuminates the entire space of the temple.

The Sunday Eucharistic celebration thus becomes a historical witness to the resurrection of Jesus and a commitment to the profession of faith of the baptized person. But it is the whole of the Church's liturgy, the dignity of her worship and the sincere veneration of the mystery which is realized in it, which manifests the *presence of* God, thus becoming an invitation to listen to the Word and a sign of Revelation. Today, through the work of the *mass media*, Christian liturgical meetings come to the eyes of the world, and this takes on a scope that goes far beyond the confines of a "portico of catechumens".

##### The witness of the baptized and the apostolic mission of the Church in the world
The noun "witness" (gr. μάρτυς) is used in the NT especially by Luke in the *Acts*, while John preferably uses the term "testimony" (gr. μαρτυρία) and the verbal forms of the verb "to testify" (gr. μαρτυρέω), recurrent almost 50 times in the Fourth Gospel. This terminology has its own specificity, close to the meaning of a "commitment of the one who has made memory" (in possible relation to μεριμνάω: I give thought, I work), that is fidelity not only to the facts seen but also to their meaning, thus distinguishing it from the concept of "eyewitness" (gr. αὐτόπτης; cf. e.g. *Lk* 1,2), which seems to have a more technical and less existential meaning. Not so, in fact, the ocular presence of the "beloved disciple" under the cross, where the testimony of what he sees is still a μαρτυρία, as if to indicate a special solemnity (cf. *Jn* 19,35). After his resurrection, Jesus announces to the apostles that they will be his witnesses (μάρτυρες) to the ends of the earth (cf. *Lk* 24:48; *Acts* 1:8), especially witnesses of his resurrection, with a testimony that implies something more than the mere presence of the eye.

In his life and in his preaching, Jesus presents himself to the world as a witness and revelator of the Father, as one who bears witness to the truth. This testimony is expressed through works, the works that the Father has given him to do. It is, therefore, a testimony, oriented to a praxis, which certainly contains in itself the concept of coherence, but in a certain way surpasses it, because it is even more founding. Nevertheless, the coherence of life between works and words is a necessary condition of witness and so Jesus taught it. He did not fail to confirm the authority of his doctrine with the credibility of his works and he exhorted his disciples to do likewise: the praise that men and women will be able to direct to the heavenly Father is based on the recognition of the good works of those who believe in that Father and entrust themselves to him (cf. *Mt* 5:16). By teaching with his works, Jesus intended to set himself explicitly as an example for the behaviour of his own (cf. *Mt* 11:29; *Mt* 10:38; *Jn* 13:13-15; 13:34-35; cf. also *1Pt* 2:21).

Much of the preaching of the *First Letter of Peter*, directed to those about to be reborn from baptism, develops a praise of good example: "your conduct among the Gentiles is blameless, that while they slander you as evildoers, when they see your good works they may come to glorify God" (*1 Pet* 2:12; cf. also 2:13-17; 3:1-2; 3:15-16); good conduct which, again in the same *Letter*, is anchored in the solid Christological reference (cf. *1 Pet 2:*21-25).

Thus Paul, in the relatives addressed to the Christian communities he founded, exhorts them by admonishing them: "Be my imitators as I am of Christ" (*1 Cor* 11:1; cf. 4:16; *Phil* 3:17; *Eph* 5:1); and again: "you have become imitators of us and of the Lord" (1 *Th 1:*6), indeed "you have become imitators of the Churches of God in Jesus Christ" (*1 Th* 2:14). The preaching and evangelization of those whom he left behind as bishops must also develop in the flow of example and the witness of life: "be an example to the faithful in word, in conduct, in charity, in faith, in chastity" (*1 Tim* 4:12; cf. *Tit* 2:7). In Paul's words, in short, the Christian offers the world "the witness of his conscience", governed not by the wisdom of the flesh but by the grace of God (cf. *2 Cor* 1:12). The echo of this Gospel teaching and of this apostolic practice, as is well known, will long be present in the *Apologies* which the authors of the first three centuries will address to the pagan emperors, in which the description of the daily life of Christians and of the civil and social duties exemplary to them will play a very important role.

When it emphasizes the divine-human sacramental dynamic of the Church, the Second Vatican Council affirms that the role of the universal Church, as a sign of Revelation, must find its concrete counterpart in the living witness of the faithful belonging to each particular church. In fact, the particular church is "also sent to those who do not believe in Christ [...] to constitute, through the witness of life of the individual faithful and of the whole community, the *sign* that points to Christ" (AG, 20). Every particular church therefore also calls upon non-believers, showing itself to them as a sign of a credibility that directs them towards Christ, and it does so in both its personal and collective dimensions, that is, through "the witness of life of the individual faithful and of the whole community" (*ibid.*).

It is customary in Council texts to refer to "witness" by speaking of the different roles of the various members of the one people of God. Thus bishops and pastors must present to the world the witness of Christ through the Church,119 like the parish priests in their task of preaching and evangelizing, like each of the faithful, "that they, rooted in faith, hope and charity, may grow in Christ and that the Christian community may bear that witness of charity which the Lord has recommended" (CD, 30). Priests, for their part, "must be for all a living witness of God" (LG, 41), showing both those who already believe and those who do not yet believe, Catholics and non-Catholics, the image of a priestly ministry that gives everyone a witness of truth and life.120

There are many applications to the life of the lay faithful: the obligation to bear witness through their Christian apostolate is rooted in having received the sacrament of baptism;121 They are consecrated members of a common priesthood to bear witness of Christ to the world; as Christian parents they are to bear witness of faith to their children, and the whole family to society; they are responsible like every other member of the People of God to propagate the Kingdom of God through the witness of their apostolate;122 It is, above all, a testimony of life and word which has the purpose of proclaiming the Gospel in every environment of society, transforming it into charity and orienting it to Christ.123

One understands then why the lack of witness does not pass unnoticed, but becomes a cause of non-belief, which the Council is not afraid to include, as is well known, among the factors that contributed to the genesis of atheism (cf. GS, 19). Faced with the presence of the scandal of life in the witnesses of Christ, it is necessary to know how to show that the assistance of the Holy Spirit has never failed in what Jesus promised it for, that is, to lead to the truth: there have been no errors in the teaching of the Gospel, but errors in the personal lives of individual Christians. Recognition of the errors or limitations of her children's past conduct also becomes, for the Church, a reason for the credibility of her divine origin. The CTI document on forgiveness requested by the Church for the errors of her children in the Jubilee context should also be considered in this regard: *Memory and Reconciliation: The Church and the Faults of the Past* (2000).

##### The Church, witness of a universal call to holiness
We believe that a complete reading of the value of witness as a sign of Revelation in the light of Vatican II cannot be conducted without considering the important Council doctrine on the universal call to holiness.124 It is in it that, after all, witness converges, and does so according to a logic which, in our view, is formally distinguishable (though certainly connected) with the *note of the* Church's holiness. Here holiness is not yet seen in its ostensible-ontological dimension, or within the logic of paradox, but as a sign that the Gospel *cannot ask less of* those who want to follow Christ. The Church becomes a credible sign of Revelation because she is not afraid to demand from her children the "miracle of holiness", demonstrating that the Gospel is understood and propagated only through a radicalism that does not come to terms with spiritual mediocrity, thus showing the sincerity, consistency and truth of those who embrace it, assuming also the consequences on a personal level. Only here can we understand why testimony becomes μαρτυρία. The awareness of having to strive, always and in any case, for holiness, despite personal limitations and always trusting in the grace of God and the gifts of the Spirit, makes the witness a martyr, that is, an authentic, true witness.

And the very notion of martyrdom takes on a pregnant meaning here: it no longer indicates only the witness of a faithful person who offers his or her life because of faith at a supreme moment of generosity and grace, but also the daily, ordinary witness of one who knows how to be faithful to the Gospel day by day, embodying its consequences in professional, family, social and civil tasks.125 The value of this witness also makes it possible to show the credibility of the witness to those who consider the capacity to give their lives for an ideal to be incontestable, when realized in the emotional and solemn context of a single, unrepeatable act. The living tradition of the Church could easily show that, in the Christian, the bloody martyrdom of life is inseparable from this other type of martyrdom, which bears witness to the heroism of perseverance in goodness, thus helping the interlocutor to discriminate *what types of martyrs one is* faced with.

In accordance with what is expressed in Sacred Scripture and in continuity with the life of the Church, centered on the celebration of the lived mystery, from the Second Vatican Council to the present day the Magisterium of the Church has repeatedly exhorted the faithful to be aware of the value and necessity of witness, with an intensity dictated by the progressive demands of evangelization. These are not mere exhortations of circumstance, but teachings which aim to increase the conscience of the People of God about the centrality and irrenounceability of this category, both for the understanding of the true Christian identity and for the practice of life which must accompany it.

After the Second Vatican Council, a reflection on the value of "witness" has been re-proposed in recent decades on various important occasions. Ten years after the conclusion of the Council, Paul VI was already holding back extensively on the value of Christian witness in his encyclical *Evangelii Nuntiandi* (1975), with which he intended to relaunch the missionary afflatus of the Church that some reductive, and in part misleading, interpretations of interreligious dialogue suggested to resize.126 It states lucidly that "the witness of an authentically Christian life, abandoned in God in a communion which nothing must interrupt, but equally given to one's neighbour with limitless zeal, is the first means of evangelization", immediately following a phrase which will later be used by many authors and on many occasions: "contemporary man listens more willingly to witnesses than to teachers, or if he listens to teachers he does so because they are witnesses.127

*
*[Embedded Image]*
 Evangelii nuntiandi, n. 21*

In the Extraordinary Synod of Bishops in 1985, convened with the purpose of discussing the extent to which the Vatican Council was applied in the Church, the witness appears in close connection with the mandate to bring about a "new evangelization", directed also (and above all) to those countries which in the past boasted a profound Christian tradition and which today, having lost their Christian memory and heritage, are the object of an extensive secularization, almost a "silent apostasy".128 The Magisterium of John Paul II interpreted this request first through specific documents such as *Christifidelis laici* (1987) and *Redemptoris missio* (1990), and then decisively directed the preparation and celebration of the Great Jubilee of the Year 2000, especially through the two main letters at the turn of the new millennium, *Tertio millennio adveniente (*1994) and *Novo millennio ineunte* (2001). They call for a growth in the awareness of the whole People of God of the task required by this new evangelization, exhorting the integral rediscovery of Christian identity and its spiritual, historical and cultural roots. In more recent times, a new extended appeal to Christian witness in the common ecclesial commitment to a new *evangelization* has been offered by Pope Francis, *Evangelii gaudium* (2013), a document drafted at the end of the Synod of Bishops dedicated to the New Evangelization and the Transmission of the Christian Faith (2012).

#### 5.9 Systematic summary of the significance and credibility of Christian Revelation
Examining the reasons that make us first recognize and then follow the signs of a saving revelation, clarifying and differentiating them, can also give rise to a "systematics" of the significance of Revelation and its credibility. The first will have as its object what makes the message "significant", that is, something of which man understands the meaning, the content, the value, even if he does not adhere to it, although he may adhere to it later. The second indicates the properties that the Revelator has to propose, and the Revelation to be confessed, as credible.129

**The ultimate motives for *credibility* must be sought in the credibility of the revealed Subject, because the credibility of Revelation and the credibility of faith are, in fact, the credibility of a person, the person of Jesus Christ.** But the elements that contribute to a judgment of credibility formulated, with the "eyes of faith", within a believing experience, will have to be at some level *significant* even for those who do not yet believe, when presented to the recipient of the announcement together with the contents announced.

##### 5.9.1 Preparatory elements: education in the metaphysical sense, restoration of reason and desires
The proclamation of the God of Jesus Christ, Creator of heaven and earth, needs a propaedeutics. If it is not possessed, it must be acquired by turning to religiosity and the wisdom of life as the coordinates that have accompanied the appearance of the human species on planet Earth and continue today to qualify what is human, distinguishing it from what is not. The great tradition of classical thought is an expression of this, witnessing to the dignity of the search for truth and good ethical life, as for example in Greekism and Stoicism; and so are the best contributions of religious thought, respectful of the signs of the Absolute in nature and in the conscience of men, when educated in recollection and contemplation, as in Hinduism, Confucianism or Buddhism. The elements that characterize such a propaedeutics identify an educational path that can still be shared today and could well qualify, in our opinion, a pedagogical program of Christian inspiration, in school as in the family.

##### Re-education to the metaphysical sense
A discourse on what transcends the empirical order and the possibility of recognizing the manifestations of the Absolute are possible only in a subject who possesses *a healthy metaphysical sense*. Acquired spontaneously in childhood and early adolescence, this sense is now seriously compromised by numerous internal and external influences, such as superficiality, sensuality, idolatry or the ideological drift of reason. It is part of an education that helps to restore it:

- to encourage contact with created nature, making people reflect on their own contingency, on the gratuitousness of life and on the gift character of the physical universe and its laws;
- to foster relational life, bringing out of oneself and seeking sources of meaning not only in one's own interiority, but also in the face and life in others;
- educate to the sense of hearing, so that it does not remain crushed by touch and sight, as a sense capable of memory and historical relaxation, and therefore suitable to root the identity and freedom of the subject;
- to educate to the inner listening of one's own conscience, as already recommended by the perception of Augustine's inner Master, helping to internalize experiences and events, to know how to grasp the transcendence of one's own immaterial soul on nature and things;
- help to recognize the truth of one's freedom, the good or bad consequences of one's actions;
- to educate, more generally, to the humility of study and intellectual depth, never to conform to commonplaces, superficial opinions, hearsay.

##### Educating to a *recta ratio*: realism, humility, freedom, hope
Once the Being has been recovered as its own object (metaphysics), natural reason must be (re)educated to its righteous exercise. Also in this case, it is pride, ideological closure, hedonist materialism, or the numerous conditionings coming from relativist and disengaged thought that hinder it. We point out the signs of the good health of reason - and the corresponding virtues to be acquired - in the following four characteristics.

- *Humility*. The human being cannot present himself as a measure of the whole, because he is incapable of saying or explaining the whole of reality in an exhaustive way. To open oneself up to the possibility that the foundation of the world is Other-than-human, and can "narrate" to man the ultimate meaning of reality, is a philosophically and existentially reasonable position. To deny it sickens reason, to recognize it as healing.
- *Realism*. A healthy reason is a reason willing to learn from reality, to listen to things, not to take ideas - however coherent and attractive they may be - as the only starting point of knowledge. Reality does not "pose" or "objectify" itself, but "discovers" itself. Only if one is willing to receive reality as something "given" can one prepare to recognize it as something "given" and, finally, "created".
- *Freedom*. In order to orient itself towards the truth, human reason must remain free, that is, it must not be bound to any ideology, or rather, it must be bound only to the truth, rejecting the seductions and conditioning of economic and political power, profit and authoritarianism. Free reason also means affirmation of the "truth of freedom", and therefore the ability to discern the moral value of one's actions, the essential emergency of man over all the other living beings on the planet. To deny freedom is to deny man.
- *Hope*. An intellectual position that chooses skepticism and distrust as the rule of its own method and investigation, not only cannot open itself to the truth, but makes any real progress in knowledge impossible. Reasonable trust in the knowledge of others represents an anthropologically mature and psychologically balanced position. Extreme skepticism is a position of principle and its denials are prejudicial.

Ultimately, human reason is called to strip itself of its pride, not its strength, admitting its own ontological contingency, its finite horizon, its necessary gnoseological openness beyond any self-referentiality and self-sufficiency. In other words, it must remain open to *be revealed as created reason*. Thomas Aquinas often referred to all this with the expression *recta ratio,* thus taken up also by the Magisterium of the Church. Such is a reason *capax fidei*, a reason capable of recognizing the preambles of faith and of recognizing its significance for the understanding of the Word of God, spoken both in nature and in history.

##### Education of feelings and desires
The restoration of reason means, in reality, the restoration of the person; this also implies the re-education of feelings and the healing of disordered desires, restoring their orientation to truth, goodness, beauty. Here too, openness to Revelation needs a necessary propaedeutics:

- to know how to distinguish the different outcome of desires, because the fulfillment of some of them makes one happy and satisfies, while that of others deceives and leaves one unsatisfied;
- educating to the desire for relational goods and their primacy over consumer goods, learning to value their capacity to be shared and to enrich, recognizing their contribution to the growth of the subject, and thus reaffirming the substantial difference between being and having;
- Among the relational goods, educate first of all to the recognition of sincere love and selflessness, identifying charity and mercy as the place of the human being, and showing that the desire for happiness of the human being can only be satisfied by the relationship with another personal being, in giving and receiving affection;
- knowing how to evaluate the dynamics of one's own desires within a social and relational horizon, wondering whether the desires one intends to satisfy are meeting the desires of the other, or hindering or humiliating them;
- educate feelings, because they generate the passions that accompany and orient desires: the type of music to listen to, the type of literature in which to immerse oneself in, the value of the artistic works one gets used to appreciating, the quality of the shows one attends, the nature of the emotions to arouse and share contribute to the education of feelings;
- clarify the ambivalence of *curiositas*, capable of deepening, but also of dispersing; educate his rectum by addressing itself to know and understand in order to reach the truth, reporting with courage its drift when the subject surrenders to the superficial fruition of the world, to the flow of sensations and emotions, alienating the human spirit from itself, making it unable to possess itself in order to the right exercise of freedom;
- to educate, in short, to discern what is proper to the life of the human spirit: its openness to being, that which favours its orientation towards truth, goodness and beauty.

A large part of the "education" that has just been developed here finds its natural and essential place in the family, a fruitful encounter of paternity and maternity linked by a stable donation. Only those who know and feel loved by their parents, or have experienced a free and unconditional love that reflects the oblative logic of the family, can be educated (and educate others) to an affective life and a relational life open to the transcendent nature of the human being. Only on this foundation can the person stand firmly and look serenely and openly out of himself, to the world and to others, with a psychological maturity that represents the necessary condition for being the subject of authentic relationships.

##### 5.9.2 The Preambles of the Faith and their workings for understanding the apostolic kerygma
When the person has been able to develop a humble, realistic, free and trusting reason, capable of metaphysical meaning, and has been able to (re)educate desires and passions in the search for and enjoyment of goods appropriate to the relational and spiritual nature of the human being, he is then in a position to acquire experiences and knowledge that we have qualified as "**preambles of faith".** They do not concern today only the theoretical philosophical sphere, because in today's situation the reasons that guide action and existential choices are also rooted in other spheres of judgement. The two great contexts in the life of the person in which to identify such *praeambula* are "philosophy" and "religion", here understood in their broader semantic field**. If the philosophical sense and the religious sense do not reach sufficient maturity and expression, it will not be possible to recognize the message of Revelation as meaningful, nor the Revelator as a credible person.** In order that the various segments of the apostolic kerygma "Jesus is the Christ - Jesus Christ is the Son of God - he died for our sins - he rose from the dead" acquire meaning for the mind and heart of those who receive the announcement, it is necessary today that they possess **a grid of historical, philosophical, anthropological knowledge.**

##### Preambles of the anthropological-existential field
These are to be considered experiences and knowledge necessary for the subject, so that he may be able to listen with fruitfulness and correctly evaluate the Gospel proclamation:

- the presence of a demand for meaning, and therefore the recognition of the meaning of the term ***God***, as this is indicated in the richness and diversity of human contexts and experiences, that is, **access to the Absolute through the religious sense**;
- **the affirmation of human freedom**, a reflection of self-domination and self-awareness, a manifestation of the transcendence of culture over nature and the true figure of the human being;
- **the reasonableness of the human aspiration to goodness and justice, as a preamble which will make it possible to recognize why this demand is then satisfied by the contents of Christian preaching;**
- the perception of inter-personal love as a source of meaning and happiness, which will make it possible to recognize as meaningful the credibility of love and selfless personal giving;
- the existence of a natural moral law, recognizable both in the judgment of conscience that warns to do good and avoid evil, and in the perception of a certain number of moral obligations (in essence coinciding with the *Decalogue* transmitted by the Jewish-Christian tradition);
- the perception of guilt, as moral default towards Someone inwardly called to live responsibly;
- the anthropological significance of the testimony, as reasonable access to knowledge beyond the subject's empirical experience;
- the ability to accept the appeal aroused by the coherence of life, sincere love and self-giving, considered attitudes adequate to arouse attention and interest in the witness, recognizing then that this appeal is fully and unambiguously exercised by the witness of Jesus of Nazareth.

##### Preambles of the historical-positive field
There is knowledge that the interlocutor must possess in order to evaluate the importance of the Word and recognize its appeal as significant. It requires depth, sometimes even study, love for the truth and a desire to clarify existentially relevant issues. In particular, it is necessary:

- to know some elements of the scientific context that instruct us about the position of our planet, of life and of the human race, in the framework of the historical and evolutionary development of the cosmos; in a special way, those knowledge that reveal the singular position of the human person and his emergency on the rest of animal life, because it is the seat of a phenomenology not easily reducible to an absolute materialism;
- take note that the religious experience of the human being is a historically original and geographically shared experience, a fundamental structure of his conscience that qualifies him in the panorama of the living;
- to know how to place the religious history of the people of Israel within the religious and cultural journey of the peoples, recognizing it as the repository of a tradition built on promises received from God and the testimony of their historical fulfilment;
- the possibility of accessing the empirical data of the historical existence of Jesus of Nazareth and the religious movement that originated around him and his doctrine, in particular the historicity of his death sentence on the cross and the reading of this event provided by him and his disciples;
- to know how to distinguish the absolute novelty of the announcement of the bodily resurrection of Jesus of Nazareth from the dead, even when compared to what is transmitted by other religious traditions or mythological contexts, as well as the existence of historical data that testify how this announcement spread rapidly, structuring itself in an original way around the Sunday Eucharistic celebration;
- to recognize the reliability of historical-hermeneutical criteria that ensure the historicity and authenticity of at least a certain number of narratives in the New Testament, which, while drafted within the horizon of a believing community, make it possible to reconstruct objective elements of the personality, psychology, works and teachings of Jesus of Nazareth.

##### Philosophical and metaphysical preambles
Traditionally associated with a metaphysical structure developed by the Neo-Scholastic Apologetics, various preambles of a philosophical nature admitted (and in part still admit) a use as evidence of the divine origin of the Hebrew-Christian Revelation, thus indicating the ability to recognize that Revelation is an event that receives the testimony of the Absolute, as this is known by a reason of metaphysical instance. They belong to these preambles:

- the philosophical itineraries that lead to a natural knowledge of God's existence, or rather to a knowledge of those *philosophical images* of God as indicated by cosmological (causal chains) or even anthropological (freedom, moral law, aesthetics) paths;
- the reasonableness of the existence of a transcendent and necessary ontological foundation, capable of justifying the being of the world, its intelligibility and its contingency, excluding which human reason would fall into a radical and insoluble self-referentiality, both logical and ontological;
- to understand the historical nature of human existence and the irreversibility of history, not closed in on itself but interpreter of true progress, and thus to be able to recognize as significant the *promise of fulfilment*;
- Once we have conceptually identified *the God to whom Jesus Christ becomes equal and whose Son is said to be* with the Creator and Almighty God, then the miracles of Jesus of Nazareth can be recognized as *God's actions on nature, on* behalf of man;
- Once we have identified *the God to whom Jesus Christ becomes equal and whose Son is said to be* with the provident God and Lord of history, then the entire religious history of Israel and the Christian preaching of its fulfillment in Jesus Christ can be understood as *God's actions on history*;
- to possess a necessary grid of philosophical reference (*dóxa*) that allows, once approached to the contents of Revelation, to read in the latter the logic of paradox (*para-dóxa*) and scandal, that is to say to recognize what exceeds what the human being could expect or imagine, and what, by disorienting him, can really be revealed to him as Other-than-human.

##### Role of the preambles of faith in understanding the apostolic kerygma
In the light of what we have seen, we can easily grasp the need (and the operationality) of a certain amount of philosophical or historical knowledge, but also of necessary anthropological and religious experiences, so that the apostolic kerygma will be understood by those who receive its proclamation. Their purpose is to prepare to enter (*prae-ambulum*) into a path of listening, being preliminary to something else, indicating it as significant, endowed with meaning, worthy of attention.

- *Jesus of Nazareth, historical subject of the Christian announcement*. The understanding of the whole announcement depends on the understanding of its *subject*, Jesus of Nazareth. It is therefore necessary to ensure historical access to the person of Jesus. Even in countries of ancient Christian tradition, the situation of secularization and religious indifference is such that, when one names Jesus, the interlocutor may no longer know who one is really talking about. It is then necessary to know and make known what are the main extra-Biblical testimonies about Jesus of Nazareth, to understand and make known what is the historicity of the NT documents that have come down to us.
- *Jesus is the Christ*. Even if the fulfillment of individual prophecies, promises and announcements that have actually been fulfilled continue to have their value, for most of our contemporaries it is more effective to present the fulfillment of salvation history as a *whole*. In one case or another, for *Christ to* be understood as the fulfillment of the Scriptures of Israel, it is necessary to know the main stages and figures that the Scriptures go through, showing that salvific history is presented as a great unified project of love and mercy, which acquires its definitive light and coherence only in Jesus Christ and his Paschal Mystery. In explaining the Scriptures, the historical and meta-historical aspects should be harmonized, not insisting unnecessarily on the former, when they are not, and knowing how to place the latter in relation to the existential life and daily experience of each one.
- *Jesus Christ is the Son of God*. The proclamation that Jesus is the Son of God can only be understood by those who have an idea about the existence of God, that is, about the existence of a foundation of reality, of Someone who holds the laws of nature and the destiny of our lives and is the guarantor of our moral conscience. Since this knowledge is very little shared today, those who evangelize must return to teach it. When the notion of God returns to be known through religious sense and philosophical reflection, then it makes sense to *recognize where God acts*: this is the meaning of the apostolic preaching on *Jesus' miracles*, intentionally and originally associated with this very segment of the kerygma.
- *Jesus Christ died for our sins*. In order to understand this preaching and recognize it as meaningful, it is necessary to have a perception of one's moral life and to confess the existence, in the human heart, of a judgment that approves some actions and condemns others. It is necessary, in essence, to have a perception of what *guilt* is. Only those who understand, albeit weakly, the existence of a natural moral law that binds them to a just and rewarding Creator, can attribute meaning to the announcement of the forgiveness of sins made by Jesus' cross, because they recognize that they have something to be forgiven.
- *Jesus Christ has risen from the dead*. The value and density of meaning that this statement carries are rooted in an invariable anthropology, that of life and death - not only in its existential aspects, but also in its physical and biological ones. Only the shared possession of a *dóxa* of human expectations can make the resurrection understood as a truly disconcerting event, which can have as its author only God himself. Such an event shows that Jesus of Nazareth is capable of realizing what he himself foretold and possesses, as something of his own, that life which in a fountain-like way can belong only to God. Opening to the hope that the Resurrection of Jesus concerns the life and truth of every human being, the proclamation of the Risen One intercepts the anxieties of immortality and ever faithful love, stronger than death, present in everyone.

##### 5.9.3 Reasonableness of the act of faith and credibility of Revelation
*What makes the detector/witness person, an authentically credible person?* The evaluation of such credibility is only possible on the basis of the personal encounter with the revelator/witness and his attendance, first of all the encounter with Jesus of Nazareth and the attendance of his memory. Access to the person(s) of Jesus is certainly mediated by history and the limited documentary material available to us, but the *style of* his life and his teachings is prolonged in those who declare themselves his disciples. What makes the witness credible - both Jesus Christ and his disciples - is essentially his psychological maturity. The act by which one adheres to what the witness reveals or transmits is a reasonable act in so far as one is certain of the human and psychic equilibrium of the witness, is convinced of the sincerity of his life, the rectitude of his intentions, the coherence of his works. The careful evaluation of all this is not compatible with superficial information, nor does it admit prejudicial attitudes: the certainty of the reliability of the witness can only be achieved through knowledge and acquaintance, motivated by the value of what is at stake.

*What makes a revealed dogmatic teaching, of which we have no experience, a credible truth for us?* As a first approach, in order for a certain teaching to be "credible", it must be intelligible and meaningful in the light of the anthropological and existential context possessed by the subject, in the light of his experiences and experiences, without contradicting or denying knowledge that is considered certain and shared by all.

So that, then, it is reasonable to attribute to God certain revealed contents, it is required that their origin and the way they reach us be accompanied by the signs that a *recta ratio* would associate with the presence of an Absolute, that is, an Almighty Creator, as the authority involved in the facts that generated the delivered message. Once accepted in faith, the credibility of dogmatic teachings rests on the credibility of God the Revelator: the formal reason and the ultimate reason for faith's adherence to them is God Himself.

On an objective level, the credibility of the contents believed is shown by their consistency/analogy with the other truths proposed by Revelation, by the correspondence between the object of one's own belief and what the subject has constantly (also in faith) sought, now finding answers to its questions and puzzles, and finally by the fruits that these teachings produce in human life, *first and foremost* charity and holiness, and what they imply for the revelation of the human being and for its construction in history.

*How can the risk involved in the relationship between the credibility of the witness and the objective truth of the content announced be assumed in a sensible and responsible manner?* An act of faith could not be qualified as reasonable if the risk that the witness is mistaken or deceived in good faith were not also assessed. The "risk" can be taken if this reassures us of the reliability and sincerity of the witness, assessed as deeply as possible: the more sensible and psychologically mature a witness is, the less likely he or she is to deceive, make a mistake in good faith, deceive or be deceived. In this sense, the most reasonable request we can make is that he be a *martyr* and a *saint*, that is, a "true witness" to a heroic degree. This also applies to the examination of the life of Jesus of Nazareth. Even more radically, the only real guarantee that the message delivered by him is true, that he does not deceive or deceive himself in good faith, is that he is the Truth in person, that it is Almighty God who bears witness in his favour, whose work in history can be re-known, albeit with all the limitations of chance, by philosophical reason and religious experience.

##### 5.9.4 Signs of God and human judgments
René Latourelle assigned the theology of credibility the task of incorporating three aspects: historical, anthropological and semiological.130 In the first place, on the historical level, it should be pointed out that the events that call for God's intervention in history, especially the event of Jesus Christ, the historicity of his person and his teachings, *have substantially happened*. Secondly, on the anthropological level, it had to be shown that the message delivered through these events, the Revelation of the one God in the First Testament and the Christian proclamation in the New, decipher man, reveal him to himself and thus *offer an answer to his enigmas*. Finally, the signs involved in this self-revelation of God, that is, the *signs of the divinity of Jesus Christ and their extension in the Church* as a sign of Christ, a sign of his charity and mercy among men, had to be recognizable and intelligible.

The first two areas indicate at bottom that body of knowledge preparatory to the proclamation of the Gospel, which we call preambles of the faith in the broad sense, which operate at both historical and anthropological levels. Within these levels, the interlocutor's contact with the teachings of Jesus Christ and his history, which is rooted in the historical-religious tradition of the people of Israel, is still a documentary-testimonial encounter. However, unlike the encounter with opinions or simple beliefs, it is supported by historical events that the interlocutor can to a certain extent know and examine, how he can know and experience in his own conscience the existential density of the preaching of Jesus and how he is challenged by it.

The third area, the semiological one, is what we now intend to decline, following, however, the idea that alongside the *signs,* whose subject is God who reveals Himself, one can (and sometimes must) also speak of the *reasons that* support a judgment formulated by the recipient of the proclamation. Many of these signs admit a double perspective: that of those who, from their own experience of faith, observe the stained glass windows from inside a Gothic cathedral in all their chromatic definition, grasping the coherence, intelligibility and transcendence of the Word as the property of the Revelation believed and the faith confessed; and the perspective of those who, not yet believers, that is, from outside the cathedral, observe the same stained glass windows, comparing what they blurredly intuit with the historical, philosophical or religious knowledge they possess and with the existential experiences of which they are protagonists, judging the decision to enter reasonable. This "moving to enter" is at bottom the stretch of road that man must travel, mysteriously supported by grace, to go towards God who reveals himself. The apologetic dimension of Fundamental Theology is called to stir and support this decision.

This "twofold perspective" of the theology of credibility means that a good number of witness signs that God provides to his people as reasons to believe his Promise, as well as the signs that Jesus offers to his contemporaries in suffrage of his own messianic condition (Synoptics) and of his divine sonship and equality with the Father (John), also play the role of "elements of judgment" offered to a *recta ratio* that moves towards divine revelation. Along the first perspective they are fully recognizable only with the eyes of faith, while along the second perspective they can be known and used, to a limited but well-founded extent, also in historical and documentary terms thanks to the experience and life of the Christians who have come down to us today.

Among the elements of God's self-testimony in the First Testament provided as evidence to believe the Word of Yahweh, we have met in this chapter:

- prodigious signs and saving interventions made in favor of Israel, transmitted from generation to generation by its historical memory and religious experience, signs that accompany the fulfilment of a word announced through which Jahweh confirms his omnipotence and fidelity;
- saving interventions with which God hears and answers the prayers of the righteous and helps those who come to him, as proof of his presence among men and confirmation of his identity as the one true God;
- the proclamation and fulfilment of signs by which, through the ministry and life of the prophets who speak in His name, God attests to the credibility of His word;
- the call to his own condition as Creator of heaven and earth, as the foundation of the truth of his Word and exhortation to trust his promises.
