Although with different strategies depending on the recipients of the NT narratives, the memory of events related to the story of Jesus of Nazareth and the reflection of the believing community on what happened, intentionally provides elements of judgment to motivate the faith that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God. These are:

- of the *signs* made by Jesus, that is prodigious works, miracles, healings, actions on nature;
- the credibility of the person of Jesus justified by his moral authority, mercy and empathy, psychological maturity, generosity and altruism, coherence between words and deeds;
- the fascination and attraction that the teachings of Jesus Christ, inseparable from his person, exercised (and exert) on listeners, revealing the intentions of the heart and proposing a law of charity in freedom;
- the credibility of the person of Jesus, manifested especially by the human balance with which he faces the humiliations and sufferings of his passion and death, faithful to the end to a choice of non-violence and forgiveness;
- the announcement of His resurrection from the dead, based on the manifestations of the Risen One to His disciples, as confirmation of the credibility of the divine sonship of Jesus and the truth of His teachings;
- the fulfillment of the prophecies, that is, in a more complete sense, the fulfillment of God's promises to his people gathered from the Scriptures, fulfilled in the events of Jesus' history, particularly in the Paschal Mystery of his Passion, Death and Resurrection;
- the subjective dimension with which, in every age, the lived Gospel is perceived by the believing conscience as a source of intimate joy and existential fulfillment;
- the objective dimension with which the Christian message is recognized as capable of summarizing and giving full meaning to the expectation of myth, to the best instances of religious thought and of a perennial humanism, also showing its credibility in this.

Finally, there are signs of Jesus Christ *prolonged in his Church,* caught by the believer with the eyes of faith and proposed, to those who do not yet believe, as elements to judge that God is present, in Christ and in the Spirit, in human history. We summarize them:

- the logic of a sacramental sign with which the Church manifests herself to the world, sending her life and actions beyond herself, revealing, through the divine-human dimension of her preaching and liturgy, God's presence in the world;
- the fruits that the preaching of the Gospel has brought forth throughout history, contributing decisively to the development of civil society; the social and cultural works that the disciples of Jesus realized as a consequence of their faith, the reason for their credibility and the respect due to the message they delivered (hospitals, welcoming the weakest, works of solidarity, schools, universities, cultivation of science, art);
- the witness of life of the disciples of Jesus Christ in the Church, who attract others to the faith through their virtuous behaviour, in a special way through the witness of dialogue, charity and solidarity with all and with each one, *revealing God present in love*.

*11 We agree* *in this regard with Luciano Baccari's analysis of the value of natural revelation present in the nucleus of human experience that grasps the gratuitousness of existence - in which scientific experience in fact participates - when it tends to impose itself as experience/appreciation of the whole. "Very often, with regard to the world, instead of speaking of a gift, one speaks of a given, taking its causal semantic value away from the latter; so that it, instead of being valid as a given from, would simply be valid as a given. However, only apparently or surreptitiously does the discourse appear semantically concluded, because in reality the semantic force of the term always pushes, willy-nilly or not, to its explication, so that the discourse always ends with an appeal to someone who is at the origin of the world [...]. Only a religious experience or experience of revelation is able to grasp the entirety of human experience; that is, only if the experience is religious is also total. This experience, precisely because of its revelatory character, goes beyond the logical-scientific limit, according to which the part cannot grasp the whole, because now, to grasp the whole, it is no longer a part of it, but the gift of its revelation and/or the revelation of its gift", L. Baccari, Episteme e Rivelazione, Borla, Rome 2000, 169-170. See also G. Lafont, God, Time, Being, Piemme, Casale Monferrato 1992, 227-232.*

*51 With* *the term "Source Q" some exegetes have proposed the existence of a source represented by the common verses in Matthew and Luke but not present in Mark.*

*53* *"The community of the first Christians is a community of disciples. They were people over whom Jesus had exercised a powerful fascination and attraction; they therefore held that exceptional master in high esteem and veneration, jealously preserved his sayings and faithfully handed down the memory of the events of which they had been eyewitnesses. […]. The primitive community is, moreover, a community of apostles. These had already been formed by Jesus for missionary activity (consider the pre-Easter mission reported by Lk 10:1-11) and who, in order to carry out the "great post-Easter mission", constantly needed to refer to the history of salvation that they were to announce. The kerygmatic-catechetical necessity and the apologetic necessity imposed on the apostles the need to go back, to go back to the words and gestures of Jesus. This is why in the first community "they were all assiduous in the teaching of the apostles" (Acts 2:42); as we can see, the news about Jesus was reported in a preaching that was controlled and authenticated by the group of fundamental witnesses, the twelve. Finally, the first Christian community presents itself as a community of martyrs or witnesses: the awareness of such a role was an indispensable condition for belonging to the circle of the twelve (thus in the election of Matthias in Acts 1:21-22), all the more so since there were still many people who could be denied, in case of falsehood; these witnesses are ready to pay with blood their adherence to the message and works of Jesus of Nazareth", Lambiasi, Gospels, in DISF, II, 1496.*

*54PCB**, Instructio "Sancta Mater Ecclesia" de historica evangeliorum veritate, 21.4.1964, in EV 2, 151-181.*

*55It may* *be significant to note that PCB's judgement on the improper use of this method seems to be aimed, among other things, at protecting the correct understanding of typically theological-fundamental issues, such as "the possibility and existence of miracles and prophecies", or the historical roots of faith itself, blaming those ways of presenting it "as if it did not care about historical truth, or was even incompatible with it", EV 2,153.*

*62**"The way that leads to the heavenly Christ passes through the historical manifestation of Jesus. It is impossible to separate Jesus from Christ. Early Christianity already proclaimed this truth by pronouncing the sacred name "Jesus Christ" and solemnly professing its faith with this formula: "Jesus is the Christ." [...] Precisely because the "historical Christ" is inseparable from the "heavenly Christ", from the "Christ according to the spirit", for those who exclude a priori the Christ of miracles, the heavenly Christ, there remains logically no other way out than that of erasing the entire figure of Christ", Adam, Jesus the Christ, 51.*

*63Cfr**. ibidem, 47.*

*66* *"So they said unto one another, Let us not tear it up, but let us draw lots for the one who touches it. Thus was the Scripture fulfilled, saying: My garments were divided among them, and upon my robe they cast lots. And the soldiers did so. (John 19:24). "After this, Jesus, knowing that everything was now complete, so that the Scriptures might be fulfilled, said: 'I am thirsty'". (John 19:28). "But when they came to Jesus, seeing that he was already dead, they did not break his legs, but one of the soldiers hit his side with a spear, and immediately blood and water came out. He who has seen bears witness to this, and his testimony is true; he knows that he speaks the truth, because you also believe. For it came to pass, that the scripture might be fulfilled: no bone shall be broken from him. And another passage of the Scripture says again: They will turn their eyes to him whom they have pierced" (Jn 19:33-37). "Then also the other disciple entered, who had come first to the tomb, and he saw and believed. For they had not yet understood the Scripture, that he was to rise from the dead" (Jn 20:8-9).*

*67H**.U. von Balthasar, All in the fragment, Jaca Book, Milan 1990, 146-147.*

*68 In the* *light of this concreteness, the prologue of the First Letter of John should also be read, because the decisive recognition of Jesus as the Word of life, the subject of the pericope, can only be fully realized after the experience of an encounter and contact with the humanity of the Risen One.*

*69Cfr**. L. Melina, Vita, in DISF, 1519-1530.*

*70 Thus* *Gregory the Great: "For the things that are seen are not the object of faith, but of knowledge. Why, then, when Thomas saw, when he touched with his hands, was he told "because you saw me you believed" (Jn 20:29)? But he saw something else and believed something else. The divinity, in fact, cannot be seen by mortal man. He saw man, but confessed God, saying "My Lord and my God"", Homiliarum in Evangelia libri, II, 26, 8.*

*71 For* *biblical-theological insights on the Resurrection, parts of the volume of the Anglican theologian N.T. Wright, Resurrection (2003), Claudiana, Turin 2006, can be read with fruit. In addition to what is presented in Tanzella-Nitti, La credibilità del cristianesimo, ch. V: "La verità della risurrezione di Gesù Cristo fra fede e storia", 279-364, see also: G. Ghiberti, La risurrezione di Gesù, Paideia, Brescia 1982; Idem, Testimonianze sulla risurrezione di Gesù, in "Il messaggio di salvazione", LDC, Turin - Leumann 1988, vol. VI, 382-444; F.G. Brambilla, Il crocifisso risorto, Queriniana, Brescia 1998; B. Maggioni, I racconti evangelici della Risurrezione, Cittadella, Assisi 2001; F. Bianchi, Morte-Risurrezione nella Bibbia, Borla, Rome 2006.*

*72Cfr**. Adam, Jesus Christ, 184.*

*73* *"The event of the Resurrection of Jesus Christ was deposited, if we can say so, in a rather approximate, sporadic and disparate narrative tradition [...]. The aspect of confusion that the whole entails is neither disturbing nor restless. A correct picture of the course of events is not necessary, since the fact as such is certain, and how everything happened can and must not be defined. The mystery of the resurrection from the dead preserves its character also in its journey through history, both in the apparitions and in the empty tomb", H. Schlier, On the Resurrection of Jesus Christ (1968), Morcelliana, Brescia 2005, 20-21.*

*74Cfr**. Latourelle, Miracles of Jesus and the Theology of the Miracle, 219-220.*

*75Cfr**. Schlier, On the Resurrection, 27-33.*

*76On the* *verisimilitude of such an episode one could advance some reasonable perplexity: interpreting it in a strictly literal sense, one would have to admit that both the guards and the Pharisees would have been aware of the miraculous removal of the tombstone, an awareness that would certainly have given rise to significant effects in the course of the events. It is not to the high priests that Pilate's guards should have gone to report what happened, but to their legitimate superiors; nor, according to Roman military law, would a version of the facts as the Pharisees suggest to the guards to spread, have been sufficient to exonerate them before the competent authorities. It should be noted, however, the plausibility of the fact that the Jews did not employ, to guard the tomb, their own group of guards, chosen from those dedicated to the order of religious worship, because the place of the tomb, being within an area reserved for death sentences inflicted by the Romans, could not admit this type of intrusion. In any case, as far as we are interested here, the episode reported by the evangelist manifests the existence of critical judgement, that of the intentional removal of the corpse of Jesus, already consolidated at the end of the first century and to which the evangelist tries to replicate in order to support the preaching of the resurrection, reaffirming that everything concerning the death penalty of Jesus had been done under the control of the Roman military authority.*

*77Cfr**. Dialogue with Triphon, 108, 2.*

*78The* *theft of Jesus' body then comes up against the natural reluctance of people of Jewish faith to enter a tomb and remove a corpse. To come into contact with a corpse was in fact considered, according to mosaic prescriptions, an impure action, something psychologically difficult to conceive for a Jewish mentality. The aromas and ointments brought by the pious women on Easter morning were not in fact to be applied to the body of Jesus, but to be sprinkled on the corpse and left near it.*

*79 Among* *the reasons "unknown" even to the disciples, we could still cite two natural causes, i.e. not due to intentional agents, that rationalism has advanced to justify the disappearance of the corpse of Jesus: a) the effect of physical causes such as, for example, an earthquake or a subsidence of the subsoil that led to the disappearance of the corpse from the precise place where it had been laid; b) the possibility that the crucifix had been the protagonist of an apparent death, from which it would then recover in the hours following the deposition of his body still alive. There are, however, well-founded reasons to exclude, with a high degree of confidence, both hypotheses. The first is that a phenomenon of this kind, easily found by the disciples' adversaries, would sooner or later have led them to seek, or at least to indicate, the new place where the corpse would have ended up. The second - even ignoring the spear blow mentioned by John (cf. Jn 19:33-34) because reported only from this source - would easily conflict with the experience of the Romans in the execution of crucified men condemned to death and with the description of the general physical state of the condemned man, on which the four Gospels agree.*

*80 Thomas* *Aquinas also addresses the same objection, asking himself "If the resurrection of Christ should be manifested to all", cf. S.Th. III, q. 55, a. 1. The answer given, however, proposes the general law of order in the revelation of spiritual things, which come down to us through the ministry of higher beings, which then gradually involves the lower levels of communication.*

*81Cfr**. Origene, Contra Celsum, II, 63-67.*

*82John* *Henry Newman explains the logic of this interpersonal transmission in one of his sermons, cf. J.H. Newman, Witnesses of the Resurrection, in Parochial and Plain Sermons, Longmans and Co., London 1891, vol. I, Sermon XXII, 282-294.*

*83 The* *use of mass media technology and philosophical-theological reflection on the use of these means for evangelization does not primarily respond to the possibility/necessity of reaching more people, but to the need to convey a content, in this case the proclamation of the Risen One, in a way that is perceptible, meaningful and comprehensible today. In fact, the place of communication and the way of communicating have changed. "The first areopagus of modern times is the world of communications, which is unifying humanity making it - as they say - 'a global village'. The means of social communication have reached such an importance that for many people they are the main informative and formative tool, guiding and inspiring individual, family and social behaviour. […]. The commitment in the mass media, however, is not only aimed at multiplying the annunciation: it is a deeper fact, because the very evangelization of modern culture depends largely on their influence. It is not enough, therefore, to use them to spread the Christian message and the Magisterium of the Church, but it is necessary to integrate the message itself into this "new culture" created by modern communication. It is a complex problem, since this culture is born, even before its contents, from the very fact that there are new ways of communicating with new languages, new techniques and new psychological attitudes", John Paul II, Redemptoris missio, n. 37.*

*97Before the* *end of the first century the Letter to the Corinthians by Clement Romano was certainly written and before the year 107 the seven Letters of Ignatius of Antioch to the Christian communities, on the road to his martyrdom towards Rome. The wording of the Didaché could belong to the second half of the first century and the Letter of Barnabas is dated between the end of the first and the beginning of the second century.*

*98* *"This is the nature of the human spirit: it needs time to fully understand ideas and bring them to their full perfection. The most sublime and most wonderful truths, although they were made known to the world once and for all by inspired masters, could not have been immediately understood by those to whom they were transmitted, but because they were received and transmitted by uninspired minds and through instruments that were only human, they needed a longer time and a deeper rethinking to be placed in their full light. This can be called the theory of doctrinal development. [...] On the other hand, this process cannot be taken as a development if all the aspects, which constitute its terminal form, are not implicit in the idea from which they flow", J.H. Newman, The Development of Christian Doctrine, Il Mulino, Bologna 1967, 35-36 and 44.*

*99* *"We see, therefore, that in every age Christianity has shown a certain general aspect, which made it immediately recognizable, and the only difference that could be detected in it was similar to that which lies between the aspect one has in one's youth and that which one assumes in one's maturity. [...] It has remained intact from its origins onwards, despite that process of development which, it seems, all opinions attribute, for better or for worse, to the doctrines, rites and practices in which Christianity consists. In other words, the changes that have taken place in Christianity have not been such as to destroy its original type, that is, they are not corruptions", ibid., 341.*

*105The* *analogy and the resulting sacramental dynamism are made explicit in a page of Lumen Gentium: "The society made up of hierarchical organs and the mystical body of Christ, the visible assembly and the spiritual community, the Church of the earth and the Church now in possession of heavenly goods, should not be considered as two realities, but form a single complex reality resulting from a human element and a divine element. By a non-weak analogy, therefore, it is compared to the mystery of the Incarnate Word. In fact, just as the nature assumed is at the service of the divine Word as the living organ of salvation, inseparably united to him, in a not dissimilar way the social organism of the Church is at the service of the Spirit of Christ who enlivens it, for the growth of the body" (n. 8).*

*106Cfr**. Pié-Ninot, Fundamental Theology, 477-478.*

La Chiesa come mistero dello Spirito Santo in Cristo e nei cristiani, Città Nuova, Rome 1968; L. Bouyer, La Chiesa di Dio: corpo di Cristo e tempio dello Spirito, Cittadella, Assisi 1971; R. Guardini, La realtà della Chiesa (1922), Morcelliana, Brescia 1979; H.U. von Balthasar, Sponsa Verbi (1960), Morcelliana, Brescia 1985; Idem, I santi nella storia della Chiesa, in Nuovi punti fermi, Jaca Book, Milan 1980, 279-293; B. Gherardini, Saint or sinner? Meditazione sulla santità della Chiesa, ESD, Bologna 1992; M. de Salis, Concittadini dei santi e familiari di Dio. Historical-theological study on the holiness of the Church, Edusc, Rome 2009.*

*110The* *importance of preaching is rooted in the preaching of some authors, such as St. Josemaría Escrivá, who have committed themselves to bringing the tension back to holiness within an intra-historical dynamism, without letting it be absorbed within a merely eschatological dimension. The answer to scandals and problems should not be sought by idealizing and moving holiness further away, but by continuing to believe that it is really possible to live it. This is what he expressed in a homily in 1972: "We can admire in the Church a perfection that we could call original, and another eschatological finale. Saint Paul refers to both in the Epistle to the Ephesians [Eph 5:25-27]. The original and constitutive holiness of the Church can be obscured, but never destroyed, because it is indefectible". J. Escrivá, Loyalty to the Church (1972), n. 22, in La Chiesa nostra Madre, Ares, Milan 1993.*

*111Cfr**. Pié-Ninot, Fundamental Theology, 477-478.*

*112Cf.* *C. Izquierdo, Teología Fundamental, Eunsa, Pamplona 2009, 553-556.*

*114The* *witness of Christian joy is decisively proposed as one of the central elements of the new evangelization by Pope Francis, Evangelii gaudium, nn. 2-8.*

*115Missale* *Romanum, Preface of Saints II.*

*116**"Let us not deceive ourselves: if in our lives we cost moments of momentum and victory, we also cost moments of decay and defeat. Such has always been the earthly pilgrimage of Christians, not excluding those we venerate on the altars. Do you remember Peter, Augustine, Francis? I have never appreciated those biographies that present to us - with naivety, but also with a lack of doctrine - the deeds of the saints as if they had been confirmed in grace from the womb. This is not so. The true biographies of the heroes of the faith are like our personal history: they fought and won, fought and lost; in that case, in contrition, they returned to the struggle", Escrivá, È Gesù che passa, n. 76; cf. also ibidem, n. 9 and Idem, Cammino, Ares, Milan 2002, nn. 133, 164.*

*117Cfr**. Escrivá, Camino, 576.*

IV, 457-648, stands out for its extension and depth. See also Idem, La Chiesa come tema teologico fondamentale, in R. Fisichella (ed.), Gesù Rivelatore, Piemme, Casale Monferrato 1988, 162-163.*

*119Cfr**. GS, 43.*

*120**"[The priests] should remember, in their daily conduct and solicitude, to present to the faithful and infidels, Catholics and non-Catholics, the image of a truly priestly and pastoral ministry, and that they must bear witness to all the truth and life and, as good pastors, also seek those (cf. Lk 15:4-7) who, although baptized in the Catholic Church, have abandoned the practice of the sacraments, or even the faith" (LG, 28). For the role of religious, cf. e.g. PC, 25.*

*121Cfr**. AG, 11; LG 10.*

*122Cf* *AA, 3, 11, 13; LG, 35.*

*123**"In this field the laity can exercise the apostolate of the similar towards the similar. Here they complete the testimony of life with the testimony of the word. Here in the field of work, or of profession, or of study, or of home, or of leisure, or of association, they are the most suitable to help the brethren. The laity fulfill this mission of the Church in the world above all with that consistency of life with faith, through which they become the light of the world; with their honesty in any business, through which they attract everyone to love of truth and goodness, and ultimately to Christ and the Church; with fraternal charity through which, having become sharers in the conditions of life, work, sorrows and aspirations of their brothers and sisters, they gradually dispose the hearts of all to the action of the grace which saves; with full awareness of their role in the building of society for which they strive to carry out their domestic, social and professional activities with Christian magnanimity. Thus their way of acting penetrates a little at a time the environment of life and work" (AA, 13).*

*124Cfr* *LG, 10, 39; GS, 43. The consideration that the "universal call to holiness" represents the heart of the Council's message has been made on several occasions by Paul VI: cf. General Audience, 16.3.1966, in "Insegnamenti di Paolo VI" IV (1966) 728-730; General Audience, 14.6.1972, in ibidem X (1972) 633-636; and by John Paul II: spec. Christifideles laici, nn. 16-17. The rediscovery of this doctrine can be attributed to authors such as Alfonso Maria de' Liguori, Francesco di Sales, and above all, by extension and depth of theological-spiritual development, to Josemaría Escrivá, who spread it from 1928. Cf. for example, Escrivá, It is Jesus who passes, n. 120. On the role of this author and saint as precursor of the Second Vatican Council, cf.: John Paul II, Homily during the s. Mass of beatification of Josemaría Escrivá, 17.5.1992, in "Teachings of John Paul II" XV,1 (1992) 1468-1471; Homily during the s. Mass of canonization of Josemaría Escrivá, 6.10.2002, in ibidem XXV,2 (2002) 413-416.*

*125* *"Sometimes I have wondered which martyrdom is greater: that of one who receives death for the faith, from the hands of the enemies of God; or the martyrdom of one who spends his years working for no other purpose than serving the Church and souls, and grows old smiling, and passes unnoticed... For me, martyrdom that is not spectacular is more heroic... This is your path" (J. Escrivá, Via Crucis, Ares, Milan 1989, VII Station, no. 4).*
